Chapter 3 Psychodynamic Therapies and Their Descendants

In this chapter
  1. 3.1 From Hypnosis to the Talking Cure: trace how mesmerism, hypnosis and Breuer's cathartic method led Freud to free association.
  2. 3.2 Classical Psychoanalysis as a Treatment: describe the goals, setting, rules and techniques of Freudian analysis, from free association to working through.
  3. 3.3 Transference, Countertransference and the Alliance: distinguish the patient's transference, the analyst's countertransference, the working alliance and the real relationship.
  4. 3.4 Ego Psychology and Object Relations: explain how Anna Freud, Hartmann, Klein, Winnicott, Fairbairn, Mahler and Kernberg changed what analysts listen for and do.
  5. 3.5 Self Psychology, Relational Analysis, Attachment and Mentalisation: explain Kohut's selfobject transferences and the attachment-based treatments, including mentalisation-based treatment.
  6. 3.6 Brief Psychodynamic Psychotherapies: compare the short-term methods of Malan, Sifneos, Davanloo, Mann, Luborsky, and Strupp and Binder.
  7. 3.7 Interpersonal Psychotherapy: describe the phases, the four problem areas, the techniques and the evidence for Klerman and Weissman's interpersonal psychotherapy.
  8. 3.8 The Early Dissenters as Therapists: describe how Jung, Adler, Horney and Sullivan practised therapy and which techniques belong to whom.
  9. 3.9 Transactional Analysis: use Berne's ego states, transactions, strokes, games, scripts and life positions to analyse an interaction.
  10. 3.10 Supportive Psychotherapy, Hypnotherapy and Narcoanalysis: state when supportive, hypnotic and drug-assisted methods are used and what their limits are.

Weightage. 48 of the 346 questions mapped in this book (13.9%) test this chapter, 29 of them from real past papers. 5 of its 11 sections are High yield.

In the summer of 1880 a wealthy Viennese merchant named Siegmund Pappenheim fell seriously ill, and his 21-year-old daughter Bertha took charge of nursing him. She sat by his bed night after night. By the end of the year Bertha was ill herself: a nervous cough, a squint, blurred vision, crushing headaches, and then a paralysis that drew her right arm and both legs into rigid contractures. Stranger symptoms followed. For long stretches she could not understand her native German and spoke only English. She slipped between two states of mind, one fairly ordinary and one dark, wild and abusive, and later remembered nothing of the second. The family's physician, the respected internist Josef Breuer (1842 to 1925), began visiting her every evening.

Breuer noticed something that would change the history of the mind sciences. In the evenings Bertha drifted into a dreamy, self-induced trance, and if she was allowed to talk in that state, telling stories and recounting whatever had troubled her during the day, she woke calmer and clearer. She gave the procedure two names of her own, both in English: the "talking cure" and, more playfully, "chimney sweeping".

The most famous episode came during a spell of hot weather. For about six weeks Bertha could not bring herself to drink water. She would lift a longed-for glass and then push it away, and she lived on melons and other fruit to ease her thirst. One evening, under hypnosis, she grumbled about her English lady-companion, a woman she disliked, and described how she had once walked into the companion's room and seen the woman's little dog, "horrid creature", drinking out of a glass. Out of politeness Bertha had said nothing at the time. Now, having given full and angry expression to the disgust she had swallowed, she asked for a drink, drank a large quantity of water without any difficulty, and woke from hypnosis with the glass at her lips. According to Breuer the symptom never returned.

Breuer went after symptom after symptom in the same way, tracing each one back to the moment it first appeared and letting Bertha relive the feeling that had been blocked at that moment. In 1895 he and a younger colleague, the neurologist Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939), published the case in Studies on Hysteria. To protect her identity they called her "Anna O.", shifting each of her initials, B. P., one letter back in the alphabet. Breuer called his procedure the cathartic method. Out of it grew psychoanalysis, and out of psychoanalysis grew almost every therapy in this chapter.

Hold the story lightly, though. The case of Anna O. is the founding legend of psychotherapy, and legends are tidier than lives. Was Bertha Pappenheim really cured by talking? Keep the question in mind as you read; we return to it at the end of the chapter, and the answer is one of the most instructive facts in the history of clinical psychology.

3.1 From Hypnosis to the Talking CureMedium yield · 4 Qs

Every therapy in this chapter is called psychodynamic, a word that needs unpacking before anything else. "Dynamic" here means forces in motion: a psychodynamic therapy assumes that much of what we feel and do is driven by mental forces (wishes, fears, prohibitions) that push against one another, often outside awareness, and that symptoms are the visible result of that hidden struggle. The term covers Freud's original psychoanalysis and all the therapies that grew from it, including several whose founders quarrelled bitterly with Freud. To see why the family took the shape it did, you need to go back a century before Anna O., to a Viennese physician with a tub of magnetised water.

Mesmer, Charcot and the Nancy school

In the 1770s Franz Anton Mesmer (1734 to 1815) claimed that an invisible fluid, which he called animal magnetism, flowed through all living bodies, and that illness came from its blockage. In Vienna and then Paris he "magnetised" patients with passes of his hands and iron rods dipped in a tub (the baquet), and many of them fell into convulsive "crises" and declared themselves cured. A French royal commission of 1784, whose members included Benjamin Franklin and Antoine Lavoisier, tested the claims by blindfolding patients and found that the effects appeared only when patients believed they were being magnetised. The fluid did not exist; the cures were real enough. That paradox, a real effect produced by belief and relationship rather than by the stated mechanism, runs through the whole history of psychotherapy.

The Scottish surgeon James Braid (1795 to 1860) stripped mesmerism of its fluid and in Neurypnology (1843) renamed the trance state "neuro-hypnotism", soon shortened to hypnotism, the source of our word hypnosis (from the Greek hypnos, sleep), explaining it as concentrated attention. By the 1880s two French schools disputed what hypnosis was. At the Salpêtrière hospital in Paris the great neurologist Jean-Martin Charcot (1825 to 1893) held that only hysterics could be deeply hypnotised, so hypnosis was itself a sign of a diseased nervous system. In the provincial town of Nancy, Hippolyte Bernheim (1840 to 1919), building on the country doctor Ambroise-Auguste Liébeault, answered that hypnosis was simply heightened suggestibility, a normal capacity that most people possess, and that suggestion could cure. History sided with Nancy.

The young Freud visited both camps. He studied with Charcot in Paris from October 1885 to February 1886 and came home convinced that hysteria was a real disorder of ideas, not malingering, and that it occurred in men too. In 1889 he spent several weeks with Bernheim in Nancy, whose book on suggestion he had translated into German. There he watched a demonstration that stayed with him: patients who "forgot" everything that had happened under hypnosis could be made to remember it in the waking state if Bernheim simply insisted, pressing them that they did know. Forgotten did not mean gone.

Breuer, Anna O. and the cathartic method

Freud's real teacher in therapy, however, was Breuer, who told him about Anna O. years before they published her. From the case the two men drew a theory and a technique. The theory, set out in their "Preliminary Communication" of 1893, was that "hysterics suffer mainly from reminiscences": a symptom is the frozen remnant of a traumatic moment whose emotion was never discharged. The technique, the cathartic method, was to hypnotise the patient, lead her back to the moment the symptom began, and let her relive it with its full feeling.

Two terms from this period are still examined, and they are easy to blur. Catharsis (Greek for purging or cleansing; Aristotle used it for the purging of pity and fear that a tragedy produces in its audience) is the general release of pent-up emotion through expressing it. Abreaction is the more specific process Breuer and Freud described: discharging the emotion attached to a particular repressed memory by reliving that memory in words and feeling. Bertha's outburst about the dog was an abreaction; the relief that followed was catharsis. Abreaction is the mechanism; catharsis is the result and the name of the method.

Exam trap

The "talking cure" and "chimney sweeping" were coined by the patient, Bertha Pappenheim, not by Freud or Breuer. The cathartic method is Breuer's (exam lists sometimes write "abreaction therapy: Josef Breuer"). Bernheim belongs to the Nancy school of suggestion, Charcot to the Salpêtrière. Freud is the father of psychoanalysis; Bernheim, Breuer and Charcot are its grandparents. And do not confuse catharsis (emotional release) with insight (understanding the connection between past and present); classical analysis came to value insight far more than catharsis.

Janet and Freud: dissociation versus repression

Freud was not the only man trying to explain hysteria. In Paris, Charcot's pupil Pierre Janet (1859 to 1947) argued in Psychological Automatism (1889) that hysterical symptoms arise from dissociation: a split in consciousness in which a cluster of memories and functions ("fixed ideas") breaks away from the main personality and runs on its own, because the person lacks the mental strength to hold everything together. For Janet the split was a weakness, an inability to integrate. Janet is also the name most often attached to the phrase la belle indifférence, the curious calm some patients with conversion symptoms show towards a paralysis or blindness (the disorder itself, and the modern evidence that the sign is unreliable, belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book).

Freud's answer was different and, for therapy, decisive. The split, he argued, is motivated. Painful ideas are pushed out of awareness because they are unbearable, and they are kept out by an active force. He called the pushing repression: the unconscious exclusion from awareness of wishes, memories and feelings that would arouse anxiety. In 1914 he called the theory of repression "the cornerstone on which the whole structure of psychoanalysis rests". If repression is active, it will fight back when the therapist tries to undo it; that fighting back is resistance, and much of analytic technique is the art of dealing with it. Because most psychodynamic writers followed Freud rather than Janet, the standard psychodynamic answer to "what produces dissociative symptoms?" is repression.

Exam trap

Asked what "most psychodynamic theorists" say produces dissociative symptoms, choose repression (Freud), not dissociation as a weakness (Janet) and not "ego states". Ego states are a real idea but a different one: Paul Federn's concept of ego states was later turned into ego-state therapy, and Eric Berne built transactional analysis on it (Section 3.9). "Conflict" is too general to be the answer: conflict is what every defence responds to, whereas repression is the specific mechanism that pushes the conflicting material out of awareness. Repression is also the answer to "the primary or basic defence"; every other defence was thought to presuppose it.

Why Freud gave up hypnosis

Freud used hypnosis and the cathartic method through the early 1890s and then abandoned them, for three reasons he gave repeatedly. First, many patients could not be hypnotised at all, or only lightly. Second, the cures were often temporary and seemed to depend on the patient's attachment to the physician; when the relationship soured, the symptoms came back. Third, and most important, hypnosis worked by going around the patient's resistance, so the resistance itself was never seen, understood or overcome. A cure that bypasses the problem leaves the problem in place.

Remembering Bernheim, Freud tried the pressure technique (also called the concentration technique): the patient lay with eyes closed, Freud pressed a hand on her forehead and assured her that when he released it a picture or idea would come to mind. It often did. But his patients taught him the final step. One of them, Frau Emmy von N., whom he treated in 1889, told him crossly to stop asking where this or that came from and to let her tell him what she had to say. Over the 1890s Freud gave up hypnosis, then pressure, then questioning, and settled on simply asking the patient to say everything that came to mind. That method, free association, is the subject of the next section, and with it the cathartic method became psychoanalysis.

Table 3.1 Milestones in the birth of psychoanalytic technique.
WhenEventWhy it matters
1770s to 1784Mesmer's animal magnetism; French royal commissionEffects real, mechanism false: suggestion and belief
1843Braid coins "hypnotism"Trance explained by attention, not a fluid
1880 to 1882Breuer treats Anna O.Talking cure; cathartic method
1885 to 1886Freud with Charcot in ParisHysteria taken seriously as a disorder of ideas
1889Freud with Bernheim in NancyForgotten memories can be recovered by insistence
1889 to late 1890sPressure technique, then free associationHypnosis abandoned; resistance becomes visible
1893 and 1895"Preliminary Communication"; Studies on Hysteria"Hysterics suffer mainly from reminiscences"
1900The Interpretation of DreamsDreams as the "royal road" to the unconscious
1905The Dora case publishedTransference recognised as central
1910 to 1915Freud's papers on techniqueCountertransference, fundamental rule, neutrality, abstinence, working through

3.2 Classical Psychoanalysis as a TreatmentHigh yield · 12 Qs

The word psychoanalysis names three things at once, and exam questions slide between them: a theory of the mind (the unconscious, id, ego and superego, the psychosexual stages), a method of investigating the mind (free association and interpretation), and a form of treatment. The theory of personality is taught in the Personality book; here you meet psychoanalysis as something a clinician does with a patient in a room.

The goals: making the unconscious conscious

Freud stated the aim of analysis in several famous formulas, and each tells you something different. In the Introductory Lectures (1916 to 1917) the aim is to make the unconscious conscious: to bring repressed wishes and memories into awareness, where the adult ego can judge them instead of being ruled by them. In 1933 he put the same idea structurally: "Where id was, there ego shall be." The goal is not to abolish the id's wishes but to widen the territory that the reasonable, reality-testing ego governs. And as early as 1895, with a modesty that still disarms students, he told a patient that much would be gained if her "hysterical misery" could be transformed into "common unhappiness". Analysis promises not bliss but freedom from needless, self-made suffering.

Behind all three formulas lies one idea: change comes through insight, the patient's emotionally felt understanding of how present feelings and actions repeat buried wishes and conflicts from the past. Analysts therefore aim at structural change in the personality, not merely at the removal of a symptom. A symptom that is simply suppressed, they argue, will return or be replaced by another; this predicted "symptom substitution" was one of the claims behaviour therapists later tested and largely failed to find (Chapter 4).

Remember

Each major school locates the origin of disorder in a different place, and multiple-choice questions turn on this. Psychodynamic: unresolved unconscious conflicts and emotional experiences from childhood. Behavioural: faulty or inappropriate learning (conditioning). Cognitive: disturbed or distorted patterns of thinking. Humanistic: blocked growth, a failure to live up to one's full potential because of conditions of worth. If the option mentions childhood emotional trauma or unconscious conflict, it is the psychodynamic answer.

The setting: couch, frequency and frame

Classical analysis is famous for its furniture. The patient lies on a couch; the analyst sits behind, out of sight. Freud gave a frank personal reason in 1913: he could not put up with being stared at by other people for eight hours a day or more. He also gave a technical one. With the analyst out of view, the patient cannot read the analyst's face and adjust what he says to it, and the analyst's own expressions cannot steer the material. Lying down also relaxes conscious control and encourages a mild regression in which old feelings surface more easily.

Freud saw most patients six days a week. Modern classical analysis usually means four or five sessions a week of about fifty minutes each, for several years. The fixed time, the fixed fee, the regular place and the rules of conduct together form the analytic frame, and how a patient treats the frame (arriving late, forgetting to pay, asking for extra time) becomes material to be understood. Analysis on this scale is called classical psychoanalysis; once or twice weekly, face-to-face work using the same ideas is usually called psychoanalytic or psychodynamic psychotherapy, and it is what most psychodynamically trained clinicians actually practise.

Finally, every analyst must first be analysed. Freud credited the Zurich school around Carl Jung with the demand, which he endorsed in 1912, that anyone who wants to analyse others should first undergo analysis himself. This training analysis remains the centrepiece of analytic training everywhere, for a reason you will understand fully when you meet countertransference in Section 3.3.

The fundamental rule and free association

The single instruction on which analysis rests is the fundamental rule: the patient must say whatever comes to mind, without selecting, censoring or arranging it, even if it seems trivial, irrelevant, embarrassing, rude or nonsensical. Freud gave patients an image: behave like a traveller sitting by the window of a railway carriage, describing to someone inside the carriage the changing views outside. The process of following the rule is free association, and it is the defining technique of psychoanalysis, the one that replaced hypnosis.

Why should rambling talk reveal anything? Because, Freud argued, thoughts are never truly random. When conscious steering is relaxed, ideas follow the paths laid down by unconscious wishes and conflicts, so the sequence of associations points towards what is hidden. Equally revealing are the places where association fails: the sudden blank, the change of subject, the "nothing comes to mind", the joke at the crucial moment. These failures mark the edges of what is repressed. Notice that free association is not the same as Jung's word association test (Section 3.8), in which the examiner reads out stimulus words and times the patient's replies, and that neither is a "story telling" or "narration" technique.

Exam trap

Free association is the central technique of psychoanalysis and only psychoanalysis. When an option list pairs it with systematic desensitisation, flooding or modelling, those are behaviour therapy (Chapter 4); paradoxical intention is Viktor Frankl's logotherapy (Chapter 6) and an Adlerian technique (Section 3.8); transference interpretation is psychoanalytic but is not the technique of saying whatever comes to mind. "Free association, dream analysis, interpretation, transference" is the standard psychoanalytic package in any option list. So when a question asks which therapy treats transference and free association as its central techniques, the answer is psychoanalytic therapy, and behaviour therapy, person-centred therapy (Carl Rogers, Chapter 6) and reality therapy (William Glasser's present-focused therapy of choice and responsibility, Section 6.6) are the distractors; none of them interprets the unconscious.

The analytic attitude: attention, neutrality and abstinence

The patient's half of the bargain is the fundamental rule; the analyst's half is a particular attitude, set out in Freud's papers on technique between 1912 and 1915. First, the analyst listens with evenly suspended attention: not concentrating on anything in particular, not taking notes or deciding in advance what is important, but letting the patient's words register equally so that the analyst's own unconscious can catch the connections. It is the listener's counterpart of free association.

Second, the analyst keeps neutrality. The analyst does not take sides in the patient's conflicts, does not advise, moralise or push the patient towards the analyst's own values. Anna Freud (1895 to 1982) later gave the classic definition: the analyst stands at an equal distance from the id, the ego and the superego, siding with none of them. Freud's related image was the mirror: the doctor should be opaque to the patient and, like a mirror, show nothing but what is shown to him. Students summarise this as the blank screen: the less the patient knows about the analyst's real life and opinions, the more clearly the feelings the patient brings will show up as the patient's own.

Third, the treatment is carried out in abstinence. In his 1915 paper on transference love Freud wrote that the treatment must be carried out in abstinence, meaning that the analyst does not gratify the patient's transference wishes (for love, comfort, praise, special treatment), because a gratified wish stops being something to understand. Some frustration keeps the wish alive in the room, where it can be analysed. This has nothing to do with abstaining from alcohol or drugs.

Exam trap

When "free association, transference, abstinence" appears as a distractor in a motivational interviewing question, it is the psychoanalytic package; abstinence there means the analyst's refusal to gratify transference wishes, not substance abstinence. Neutrality is not coldness: the analyst can be warm and still not take sides. And "evenly suspended attention" (also translated "free-floating attention") is the analyst's attitude, while "free association" is the patient's task.

Dream analysis

In The Interpretation of Dreams, dated 1900 (it actually appeared in November 1899), Freud called the interpretation of dreams "the royal road to a knowledge of the unconscious activities of the mind". His theory has two layers. The manifest content is the dream as remembered and reported: the story, the characters, the scenery. The latent content is the hidden meaning: the unconscious wishes and the day's leftover thoughts (the "day residue") that the dream disguises. Every dream, Freud claimed, is the disguised fulfilment of a wish.

The disguise is produced by the dream-work, the set of operations that turns latent thoughts into the manifest dream so that sleep is not disturbed by forbidden wishes. Condensation compresses several thoughts or people into one image (a figure with your teacher's face and your uncle's voice). Displacement shifts emotional intensity from an important element to a trivial one, so the dream's emotional centre seems to lie somewhere harmless. Representation (or considerations of representability) turns abstract thoughts into concrete pictures, including symbols. Secondary revision (also called secondary elaboration) smooths the result into a more or less coherent story on waking. In the consulting room the analyst does not consult a dictionary of symbols; the patient associates freely to each element of the dream, and the latent content is reconstructed from those associations.

Resistance and its analysis

If you have ever cancelled a dentist's appointment because "something came up", you already understand resistance. Resistance is everything in the patient's words and actions that opposes the progress of the treatment and access to unconscious material: arriving late, forgetting sessions, falling silent, going blank, changing the subject, intellectualising, endlessly discussing trivia, or suddenly feeling much better and wanting to stop. Resistance is not bad faith. It is repression showing itself in the consulting room, the same force that pushed the material out of awareness now working to keep it out. In the economic language you will meet in a moment, repression is maintained by an anticathexis, and resistance is that anticathexis met face to face.

Freud's crucial shift was to stop treating resistance as an obstacle to be overpowered (by hypnosis or insistence) and to treat it as material to be understood. Analysis of resistance means noticing the obstruction, showing it to the patient and exploring what it protects against. A patient who always talks about her job when her mother comes up is telling the analyst, without meaning to, where the pain lies. In 1926, in Inhibitions, Symptoms and Anxiety, Freud classified resistances by their source (Table 3.2).

Table 3.2 Freud's five kinds of resistance (1926).
SourceResistanceWhat it looks like
EgoRepression resistanceForgetting, going blank, inability to remember the painful material
EgoTransference resistanceFeelings for the analyst used to avoid remembering (being in love with the analyst instead of working)
EgoSecondary gainClinging to the illness because of its advantages: sympathy, care, escape from duties
IdRepetition compulsionOld patterns repeat even after insight; overcome only by working through
SuperegoUnconscious guilt, need for punishmentGetting worse when praised or helped: the negative therapeutic reaction

Two of these terms deserve a definition of their own. Secondary gain is the external advantage a symptom brings, such as attention, relief from responsibility or compensation; it contrasts with primary gain, the internal relief from anxiety that the symptom achieves by keeping a conflict out of awareness. The negative therapeutic reaction is the paradox in which a patient gets worse precisely when the treatment progresses or when the analyst offers hope, which Freud explained as an unconscious sense of guilt that cannot allow the patient to get well.

Exam trap

Four options keep appearing together. Saying everything that comes to mind is free association. Redirecting feelings about significant others onto the therapist is transference. Obstructing the analysis, with the therapist exploring the reasons, is analysis of resistance. Interpreting dreams is dream analysis. Note too that resistance is unconscious; a patient who consciously hides a secret is being uncooperative, which is different. Fear of the unknown, low motivation or feeling overwhelmed are common sources of resistance, not definitions of countertransference.

Interpretation: confrontation, clarification, interpretation

The analyst's main intervention is the interpretation: a statement that makes conscious the unconscious meaning, source, history or cause of something the patient says or does. "You went quiet just as you began to speak of your father, as you did on Monday; I wonder whether being angry with him feels dangerous" is an interpretation. It links an observable piece of behaviour (the silence) to a hidden motive (the frightening anger), and often to its history.

The American analyst Ralph Greenson (1911 to 1979), in The Technique and Practice of Psychoanalysis (1967), described the steps that lead up to an interpretation. Confrontation draws the patient's attention to something he has been doing without noticing ("You have changed the subject three times"). Clarification brings the phenomenon into sharper focus, separating out its details ("It happens whenever we approach your brother's wedding"). Interpretation then offers its unconscious meaning, and working through (below) repeats the process until it holds. Classical rules of timing go with this: interpret only when the material is close to consciousness, so that the patient nearly sees it already; work from the surface towards the depths; and interpret the defence before the content it hides, because telling a patient what he is defending against before he can see that he is defending only increases the resistance.

In 1934 James Strachey (1887 to 1967), the translator of Freud's collected works, argued that the interpretations that change people most are mutative interpretations: interpretations of the transference made at the moment the feeling is alive in the room, so that the patient can compare the figure he imagines the analyst to be with the real analyst in front of him. Insight that is purely intellectual ("I suppose I resent my father") changes little; insight that is felt in the here and now is what analysts mean by emotional insight.

Working through and termination

Patients who have just grasped an interpretation often expect to be cured on the spot. They are usually disappointed, and Freud explained why in "Remembering, Repeating and Working-Through" (1914). A single insight does not dissolve a pattern that has been laid down over a lifetime; the same conflict will reappear in new disguises, in dreams, in the transference, at work, in the marriage, and each time it must be recognised and interpreted again. Working through is this repeated, laborious re-examination of the same conflicts, resistances and interpretations across many different contexts until the insight is integrated and lasting change in behaviour and character follows. It is the step that turns intellectual insight into change, and it is what the id's resistance (the repetition compulsion) demands.

Figure 3.1 puts the techniques together as a single cycle. Free association produces material; resistance and transference appear; the analyst confronts, clarifies and interprets; and working through sends the same conflict round again in new contexts until it no longer has its old power.

Analysis ends with termination, ideally a planned phase of its own in which the patient's feelings about ending, often echoes of earlier separations, are themselves analysed. Freud's late paper "Analysis Terminable and Interminable" (1937) was gloomy about how complete any analysis can be, and conceded that analysts themselves might need to return to analysis every few years.

The analyst's vocabulary: energy and defence

Analysts describe the mind partly in an "economic" language of psychic energy, and a few of its terms are examined directly. Libido is Freud's name for the energy of the sexual (later, the life) drive. Cathexis is the investment of that energy in the mental representation of a particular object, person or idea; when you are preoccupied with someone you love, Freud would say you have cathected them. (Freud's German word was Besetzung, "occupation"; Strachey coined "cathexis" from the Greek for "holding" in translating it.) An anticathexis (or countercathexis) is energy the ego spends to keep a repressed idea out of awareness; it is the economic face of repression and resistance. Fixation is the persistent attachment of libido to an earlier psychosexual stage, which leaves a person prone to regress to it under stress. Sublimation is the redirection of a drive's energy to a socially valued, non-sexual aim, such as aggression channelled into surgery or competitive sport.

Exam trap

"Psychic energy invested in a particular object, person or idea" is cathexis, not libido. Libido is the energy itself; cathexis is where it is placed; fixation is energy stuck at a stage; sublimation is energy redirected to a valued aim. A useful image: libido is your monthly salary, cathexis is what you spend it on, fixation is money locked in an old account, sublimation is money moved to a charity.

The same applies to the defence mechanisms, the unconscious strategies the ego uses to protect itself from anxiety arising from conflict among the id, the superego and reality. Their theory belongs to the Personality book. What a clinician needs is to recognise them as they happen in a session, because in psychodynamic work defences are among the main things that are interpreted, and in assessment they indicate how mature and flexible a patient's functioning is (Chapter 2 shows how they enter a case formulation). Table 3.3 gives the defences most often examined, as a clinician meets them.

Table 3.3 Defence mechanisms as the clinician meets them (theory in the Personality book).
DefenceWhat happensHow it shows in the clinic
RepressionThreatening wish or memory kept out of awarenessCannot recall a painful period at all; the "primary" defence
SuppressionDeliberate, conscious postponement of a worry"I'll think about the biopsy after the exam"; conscious, so a mature coping style rather than a classical defence
DenialRefusing to acknowledge an obvious external realityA man with a heart attack insists it was indigestion
ProjectionOwn unacceptable feelings attributed to someone elseA hostile patient is sure the therapist dislikes him
DisplacementFeeling redirected from a dangerous target to a safer oneShouted at by the boss, he shouts at his younger brother
Reaction formationUnacceptable impulse turned into its exaggerated oppositeExcessive sweetness towards a resented sibling
RationalisationPlausible, acceptable reasons given for behaviour driven by other motives"I failed because the paper was unfair"
IntellectualisationFeelings handled through abstract talkA patient discusses his cancer statistics with no visible emotion
Isolation of affectIdea kept, its feeling split offDescribes his father's death in a flat voice; common in obsessional patients
UndoingAn act meant to cancel a forbidden thought or actRitual washing after an aggressive thought
RegressionReturn to an earlier level of functioning under stressBed-wetting in a child after a sibling's birth
SublimationDrive redirected to a socially valued aimAggression channelled into competitive sport; a mature defence
SplittingSelf or others seen as all good or all badThe therapist is idealised one week, despised the next (Section 3.4)

Two further mechanisms often appear as distractors. Conversion is not a defence in the strict sense but the psychoanalytic name for the transformation of a psychological conflict into a bodily symptom, as in Anna O.'s paralysis. Dissociation, as you saw, is Janet's split in consciousness. The psychiatrist George Vaillant (born 1934), in Adaptation to Life (1977), arranged defences in a hierarchy from psychotic (delusional projection, psychotic denial) through immature (projection, acting out) and neurotic (repression, displacement, reaction formation, intellectualisation) to mature (sublimation, suppression, altruism, humour, anticipation), and showed that men who used mature defences had better health and happier lives over decades. The direction of growth in therapy, on this view, is up the hierarchy.

Exam trap

Projection attributes your own unacceptable feeling to someone else; displacement keeps the feeling as yours but changes its target; rationalisation offers a respectable excuse; sublimation redirects a drive into a valued activity. Suppression is conscious, which is why it is the odd one out in lists of unconscious defences. Isolation (of affect) and undoing are the classic obsessional defences; conversion is the classic hysterical mechanism.

Indian context

Psychoanalysis reached India early. Girindrasekhar Bose (1887 to 1953), a Calcutta physician, wrote his doctoral thesis on repression (published as The Concept of Repression in 1921), corresponded with Freud, and in 1922 founded the Indian Psychoanalytical Society, which affiliated with the International Psychoanalytical Association; he later started the journal Samiksha (1947). Bose proposed a "theory of opposite wish" (every wish is accompanied by an unconscious opposite wish), which Freud found interesting but did not accept. Indian clinicians have long debated how well a long, individual, insight-oriented therapy suits patients whose selves are embedded in joint families, a question you will meet again in Section 3.10.

3.3 Transference, Countertransference and the AllianceHigh yield · 8 Qs

Of all Freud's discoveries in the consulting room, the most unsettling was one he did not go looking for. Patients who had come to talk about their fathers, their marriages or their paralysed arms began, sooner or later, to have strong feelings about him. They fell in love with him, resented him, feared his disapproval, sulked when he went on holiday, competed for his praise. At first Freud treated these feelings as a nuisance that interrupted the real work. Within a few years he had reversed his view completely: the feelings were the real work. This section explains why, and introduces the three other strands of the relationship between patient and therapist that every psychodynamic clinician now learns to track.

What transference is

Transference is the patient's unconscious redirection onto the therapist of feelings, wishes, fears and expectations that originally belonged to important figures of the past, usually the parents or siblings. The patient does not experience it as a transfer at all. A young woman who grew up with a critical father does not think "I am treating my therapist as if he were my father"; she simply feels, with complete conviction, that the therapist is quietly judging her, and she reads his every pause as proof. The feelings are real; their target is mistaken. Freud's first name for the phenomenon, in Studies on Hysteria (1895), captured exactly that: a "false connection" between an old feeling and a new person.

Transference is not confined to therapy. You have probably seen it in a college corridor: the student who bristles at every male teacher as she once bristled at her strict father, or the new employee who treats a mild manager with the anxious deference he learned towards a frightening grandfather. What the analytic setting does is intensify it and make it visible. The analyst who reveals little about himself (the blank screen of Section 3.2), who sees the patient four or five times a week and who does not gratify the patient's wishes gives old templates very little real information to correct them, so the templates show themselves clearly.

Freud learned this the hard way. In October 1900 he began treating an 18-year-old woman he later called "Dora" (Ida Bauer), who had been drawn into a tangle of affairs and propositions among her father's friends. After about eleven weeks she abruptly broke off the treatment. In the case history, published in 1905 as "Fragment of an Analysis of a Case of Hysteria", Freud admitted in a postscript that he had failed to notice and interpret in time that Dora was treating him as she treated Herr K., the family friend who had propositioned her: she took her revenge on Freud by deserting him, as she believed she had been deceived and deserted. From then on transference stood at the centre of the method. In "The Dynamics of Transference" (1912) Freud explained why it was indispensable as well as troublesome. Transference is the strongest form of resistance, because the patient can live out a feeling towards the analyst instead of remembering where it came from; but it is also the only place where the old conflict becomes alive and present, where it can actually be changed. As he put it, it is impossible to destroy anyone in absence or in effigy.

Positive, negative and erotic transference; the transference neurosis

Freud sorted transference by the direction of its feeling. Positive transference consists of affectionate, trusting and admiring feelings towards the analyst. Its mild, conscious part (later called the "unobjectionable positive transference") is a great asset: it is what makes the patient keep coming, bear painful interpretations and believe that the analyst means well. Negative transference consists of hostile, suspicious, contemptuous or competitive feelings towards the analyst, the anger once felt towards a depriving parent now aimed at the person in the chair. Neither is "good" or "bad" in itself; both are material to be understood. A third variety needs its own name. In erotic transference (Freud's "transference love", 1915) the patient falls in love with the analyst. Freud insisted that this love is genuine but is provoked by the analytic situation and is a form of resistance, and that the analyst must neither return it nor reject the patient for it, but analyse it. That is the context of the rule of abstinence you met in Section 3.2.

When analysis goes well, the patient's illness gradually reorganises itself around the analyst. The old symptoms fade in importance while the conflicts behind them are re-enacted in the relationship with the analyst, in the patient's reactions to the analyst's holidays, fees, silences and interpretations. Freud called this new, artificial edition of the illness the transference neurosis ("Remembering, Repeating and Working-Through", 1914), and he saw it as an advantage: the ordinary neurosis is replaced by one that lives in the consulting room, where it can be observed and resolved. In the same paper he described acting out, repeating a conflict in action (falling for a married colleague, quitting a job) instead of remembering it and putting it into words. Modern psychodynamic psychotherapy, meeting once or twice a week, seldom produces a full transference neurosis, but therapists still follow the principle that the patient's way of relating to the therapist is a sample of the patient's way of relating to everyone.

The analyst's main tool for dealing with transference is the interpretation of the transference, showing the patient, at the moment the feeling is alive, that it repeats an old pattern. This is Strachey's mutative interpretation (Section 3.2). Table 3.4 sets the main forms side by side.

Table 3.4 Forms of transference and countertransference.
TermDirectionWhat it looks like
Positive transferencePatient to therapistTrust, affection, admiration; keeps the patient working
Negative transferencePatient to therapistHostility, suspicion, contempt, rivalry
Erotic transferencePatient to therapistFalling in love with the therapist; analysed, never gratified
Transference neurosisPatient to therapistThe whole illness re-staged around the analyst
Positive countertransferenceTherapist to patientOver-involvement, favouritism, wish to rescue, extra time
Negative countertransferenceTherapist to patientIrritation, boredom, dislike, punitive comments, forgetting the session
Exam trap

Direction decides the term. Patient to therapist is transference; therapist to patient is countertransference. "The therapist's emotional reaction to the client" is never transference, however the option is worded. In a definition question, "a homework assignment between sessions" is cognitive-behavioural, "direct interpretation of dreams" is dream analysis, "free association of unconscious thoughts" is the fundamental rule, and "resistance to interpretations" is resistance; none is transference. Transference is the client redirecting feelings about significant figures from the past onto the therapist.

Countertransference: from blind spot to instrument

If the patient brings the past into the room, so does the analyst. Freud first named countertransference in 1910, in "The Future Prospects of Psycho-Analytic Therapy", as the analyst's own unconscious emotional reactions to the patient, stirred up by the patient's influence and arising from the analyst's unresolved conflicts. His conclusion was stern: no analyst goes further than his own complexes and internal resistances permit. Countertransference, on this classical view, is a blind spot. An analyst whose own mother was intrusive may miss his patient's intrusiveness, or attack it too hard; an analyst who needs to be admired may fail to interpret a patient's flattery. This is the reason for the training analysis of Section 3.2: the analyst must know his own complexes so that he can recognise when they are speaking.

Feelings towards a patient can run either way, and the exam vocabulary follows the same logic as for transference. Positive countertransference appears as over-involvement: liking the patient so much that the therapist avoids confronting him, extends sessions, gives special favours or fantasises about rescuing him. Negative countertransference appears as irritation, boredom, dislike, sleepiness, sarcastic or punitive interpretations, lateness for this patient's sessions or "forgetting" them altogether. When a question asks for the process in which a therapist expresses negative feelings towards a patient, the answer is negative countertransference, not negative transference (which is the patient's hostility).

A different view took hold in the late 1940s. Donald Woods Winnicott (1896 to 1971), a London paediatrician turned analyst, argued in "Hate in the Counter-Transference" (1949) that some of the analyst's feelings are objective: any reasonable person would feel them in response to this patient's behaviour, as a mother inevitably sometimes hates a demanding baby, and the analyst must be able to bear and understand such hatred rather than deny it. In 1950 Paula Heimann (1899 to 1982) went further in a short, famous paper, "On Counter-Transference". The analyst's emotional response to the patient, she wrote, is one of the most important tools of the work: an instrument of research into the patient's unconscious. If you feel strangely helpless with one patient, contemptuous with another and protective with a third, those feelings may be telling you what the patient is doing to you, and so what was once done to the patient or what the patient cannot bear to feel. In Buenos Aires, Heinrich Racker (1910 to 1961) refined the idea in the 1950s by distinguishing concordant identifications, in which the therapist feels what the patient feels (the patient's despair seeps into the therapist), from complementary identifications, in which the therapist feels what the patient's inner figures felt (the therapist finds herself becoming as critical as the patient's critical mother).

Modern clinicians hold both views at once. The broad, "totalistic" definition, which counts all of the therapist's reactions to the patient as countertransference, treats them as data. The narrow, classical definition reminds the therapist that some of the data come from her own history and must not be blamed on the patient. What separates useful from harmful countertransference is not whether the therapist has feelings (she always will) but whether she notices them, understands them and refrains from acting them out. The safeguards are self-awareness, personal therapy, supervision and consultation with colleagues.

Clinical link

A trainee in a Bengaluru outpatient clinic notices that she dreads Thursdays because of one client, a domineering businessman who corrects her grammar and talks over her. In supervision she recognises two strands. One is hers: he sounds exactly like her elder brother, and she is shrinking as she did at fourteen. The other is his: when she gently explores, it emerges that he was belittled by his father and now belittles everyone before they can belittle him. Her shrinking is the complementary identification Racker described; she is being made to feel the small boy he once was. Once she can see this, her dread becomes a hypothesis she can work with, and the Thursday sessions change.

Exam trap

Countertransference is the therapist's emotional reaction to the client arising from the therapist's own unresolved conflicts. It is not the client's projection onto the therapist (that is transference), not the client's resistance, not behavioural reinforcement, not "the need for therapists to maintain appropriate boundaries" (that is a professional duty, below) and not "fear of the unknown, lack of motivation or feeling overwhelmed" (sources of resistance). If an option says "when therapists experience similar (emotional) feelings towards the client", it is describing countertransference. Freud named it in 1910; Heimann (1950) turned it into a tool.

The working alliance and the real relationship

If everything between patient and analyst were transference, how could any patient ever trust an interpretation? Something in the patient must stand beside the analyst and look at the transference with him. In 1934 the Viennese analyst Richard Sterba described this as a split in the patient's ego: one part experiences, another part observes alongside the analyst. In 1956 Elizabeth Zetzel named it the therapeutic alliance, and in 1965 Ralph Greenson (whom you met in Section 3.2) gave it the name that stuck. The working alliance is the relatively non-neurotic, rational, cooperative relationship between the patient's reasonable ego and the analyst's analysing ego, which makes it possible for the patient to work purposefully in the treatment even when the transference is stormy. A patient who can say "I know this is irrational, but I'm furious that you went on holiday; let's understand why" is showing transference and working alliance at the same moment.

In The Technique and Practice of Psychoanalysis (1967) Greenson added a third strand. The real relationship is the genuine, realistic, person-to-person bond between patient and analyst, undistorted by transference: the patient's accurate perception that this analyst is in fact kind, or punctual, or tired today, and the analyst's genuine liking and respect for the patient. Greenson thought that classical analysts had underrated it, and that a cold, mechanical analyst could not cure anyone. In 1985 the counselling psychologists Charles Gelso and Jean Carter generalised this into a tripartite model that applies to every school of therapy: every therapeutic relationship contains a working alliance, a transference configuration (with countertransference) and a real relationship, in proportions that vary from school to school. Figure 3.2 shows the four strands together.

The most influential step came from Edward Bordin, who in 1979 lifted the alliance out of psychoanalysis altogether. Bordin's therapeutic alliance has three components: agreement on the goals of therapy, agreement on the tasks that will reach them, and the emotional bond of trust and liking between the two people. Because it says nothing about the unconscious, Bordin's alliance applies equally to a cognitive therapist and a family therapist, and it became the most researched relationship variable in psychotherapy. Its measurement and the evidence that it predicts outcome belong to Chapter 8.

One consequence is often examined. A good working relationship is defined by its features (a realistic, collaborative contract to work together, an observable ability of the worker and the client to work together, and a committed belief in the helping relationship), not by any particular theory. It needs no psychodynamic theory base.

Exam trap

The working alliance (Zetzel 1956, Greenson 1965, Bordin 1979) is a psychodynamic idea that became pantheoretical; the "real relationship" is Greenson's psychoanalytic term too. Yet a practice question asks which approach focuses on the real relationship and unconditional positive regard "rather than the working alliance per se", and keys person-centred therapy. The key is defensible because of the phrase "unconditional positive regard": Carl Rogers (Chapter 6) treated the genuine relationship itself as the therapy and did not talk in alliance terms. Read the whole option, not just "real relationship". And remember: a psychodynamic theory base is not a characteristic of a working relationship.

Boundaries and the frame

Transference makes patients unusually open to influence. A patient in the grip of an idealising or erotic transference may agree to almost anything the therapist suggests, which is exactly why the therapist must suggest nothing that serves the therapist. Psychodynamic writers therefore pay close attention to boundaries, the edges of the professional relationship: time, place, money, gifts, physical contact, self-disclosure, language and dual relationships. In 1993 the psychiatrists Thomas Gutheil and Glen Gabbard drew a distinction that is now standard. A boundary crossing is a departure from the usual frame that is harmless or even helpful, such as accepting a small box of sweets at Diwali, walking a frail patient to the lift, or a brief, considered self-disclosure. A boundary violation is a crossing that exploits or harms the patient, and is usually repeated and kept secret: business deals with patients, using them for the therapist's emotional needs, and above all any sexual relationship, which is always a violation, whatever the patient appears to want. Violations rarely begin dramatically; they tend to follow a "slippery slope" of small crossings that go unexamined, such as extended sessions, then first-name intimacy, then meetings outside the office. The ethical rules themselves, including the prohibition of sexual contact with clients and the handling of dual relationships, are set out in Chapter 1.

Why is transference work the hallmark of the psychodynamic family in particular? Every therapist has a relationship with the client, and cognitive-behavioural therapists use a warm, collaborative relationship and will discuss a rupture openly. But only psychodynamic models make the analysis of transference and countertransference the central route to change. Behaviour modification works on contingencies, cognitive-behavioural therapy on thoughts and behaviour, and solution-focused therapy (Chapter 6) on exceptions and future goals; none interprets the client's feelings about the therapist as repetitions of the past.

Exam trap

"Managing transference and countertransference is critical in" psychodynamic models of therapy, not behaviour modification, cognitive-behavioural therapy or solution-focused therapy. "Transference, free association, interpretation" and "working alliance, transference, resistance interpretation" are psychodynamic packages offered as wrong answers to questions about Rogers' core conditions (empathy, unconditional positive regard, congruence; Chapter 6).

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  5. Psychic energy invested in a particular person, object or idea is called .

3.4 Ego Psychology and Object RelationsHigh yield · 4 Qs

Freud's own theory changed shape in 1923, when The Ego and the Id replaced his earlier map of conscious, preconscious and unconscious with the structural model of id, ego and superego (taught in the Personality book). The change opened two roads, and after Freud's death in London in 1939 his followers took both. One road led towards the ego: how it defends, how it adapts, how strong or weak it is. That road, called ego psychology, dominated American psychoanalysis for thirty years. The other led towards the objects, the people a child loves, hates and takes inside itself. That road, called object relations, was built mainly in London. Both changed what analysts listen for and what they do.

Anna Freud and the analysis of defence

Anna Freud, Freud's youngest child and the only one of his children to become an analyst, gave ego psychology its starting point in The Ego and the Mechanisms of Defence (1936). Her argument was technical. If the analyst attends only to the id, to the forbidden wishes beneath the surface, he will keep running into the ego's defences without understanding them. The defences themselves, the ways in which the ego keeps a wish out of awareness, are just as unconscious and just as worthy of analysis. She collected the ten defences analysts had described by then (regression, repression, reaction formation, isolation, undoing, projection, introjection, turning against the self, reversal into the opposite, and sublimation), and added two of her own. In identification with the aggressor a child who has been frightened or hurt copies the aggressor, as when a boy who has just had a painful injection plays at giving his little sister injections. In altruistic surrender a person gives up her own wishes and lives them out through others, helping in order not to feel helpless. Her famous technical rule, that the analyst should stand at an equal distance from id, ego and superego, follows directly: the analyst's job is to observe all three, not to side with the wishes against the defences.

Anna Freud was also a pioneer of child analysis, which she adapted to children's realities. A child, she argued, does not come to treatment of his own accord and has not yet separated from his parents, so the analyst must first win the child's trust in a preparatory phase, must work with the parents, and must take an educational as well as an interpretive role. She believed that children do not form a full transference neurosis, because their original objects, the parents, are still present in their lives. In London she ran the Hampstead War Nurseries during the Second World War and later the Hampstead Child Therapy Clinic, which after her death became the Anna Freud Centre. In Normality and Pathology in Childhood (1965) she described developmental lines, sequences along which a child moves from dependence to self-mastery (from being fed to feeding oneself, from wetting to bladder control, from egocentrism to companionship), so that a clinician could judge whether a child was developing normally in each area.

Hartmann, Erikson and the adaptive ego

If Anna Freud showed the ego defending, Heinz Hartmann (1894 to 1970) showed it adapting. In Ego Psychology and the Problem of Adaptation (1939) he argued that not every ego function is born of conflict. Perception, memory, motor control, language and thinking develop in a conflict-free ego sphere, maturing according to their own timetable in what he called an "average expectable environment" (a baby needs no conflict to learn to see). He called such functions apparatuses of primary autonomy. Other patterns begin in conflict but later become independent of it and serve adaptation; a child who becomes orderly to defend against messy impulses may grow into an adult whose orderliness simply helps her run a hospital department. Hartmann called this secondary autonomy. With Ernst Kris and Rudolph Loewenstein, Hartmann made ego psychology the mainstream of American analysis, and he is usually called its father.

For the clinician, ego psychology contributed a way of assessing patients. Before deciding whether to interpret or to support, the therapist estimates ego strength: the patient's capacity to test reality, tolerate anxiety and frustration, control impulses, think clearly, form relationships and use mature rather than primitive defences. Patients with strong egos can bear the anxiety that interpretation stirs up; patients with fragile egos need their defences shored up instead. You will see this assessment decide the choice between expressive and supportive therapy in Section 3.10 and the selection of patients for brief therapy in Section 3.6.

The most widely read ego psychologist was Erik Erikson (1902 to 1994), a student of Anna Freud's, who in Childhood and Society (1950) extended Freud's psychosexual stages into eight psychosocial stages across the whole lifespan, each defined by a crisis to be resolved. He coined the term identity crisis for the adolescent struggle of identity versus role confusion. The stages themselves are taught in the Developmental and Personality books; here you need only remember that Erikson belongs to ego psychology and that the identity crisis is his.

Exam trap

The "identity crisis" in adolescence is Erik Erikson's, not John Bowlby's (attachment, Section 3.5), Melanie Klein's (object relations, below) or Eric Berne's (transactional analysis, Section 3.9). The "father of ego psychology" is Heinz Hartmann; the conflict-free ego sphere is his. The book that made defences the analyst's business is Anna Freud's The Ego and the Mechanisms of Defence (1936). Identification with the aggressor is hers.

What "object relations" means

The word "object" in psychoanalysis is a technical term that confuses everyone at first. Freud used it for the target of a drive, the thing through which a drive reaches its aim, and in practice the object is almost always a person or a part of a person (the mother, the mother's breast, the father's voice). Object relations refers to the internalised mental representations of self and others, and of the relationships between them, that are built up from early experience and then shape how a person perceives, feels about and behaves towards real people for the rest of life. When a man who was repeatedly abandoned as a child expects every girlfriend to leave him, and behaves so jealously that she does, he is acting from an internal object relation: a frightened, needy self linked to a rejecting, unreliable other, joined by the feeling of panic.

What makes this a new theory rather than new jargon is the change of motive. For Freud the infant is driven above all by the search for pleasure, and other people matter because they satisfy or frustrate drives. For the object relations theorists the infant is driven above all by the need for relationship. The Scottish analyst William Ronald Dodds Fairbairn (1889 to 1964) put it most bluntly in Psychoanalytic Studies of the Personality (1952): libido is primarily object-seeking, not pleasure-seeking. A child stays attached even to an abusive parent, Fairbairn observed, because a bad object is better than no object at all, and he takes the badness into himself ("I must be bad, or she would love me") rather than lose the parent. Fairbairn described how such internalised bad objects form split-off parts of the self, among them an "internal saboteur" that attacks the person's own longing for closeness, and how a schizoid withdrawal from others can protect a person from a love that feels destructive.

Exam trap

"Object relations" means the internalised mental representations of self and others and the relationships between them. It does not mean the relationship between id, ego and superego (the structural model), the use of defence mechanisms to protect the ego (ego psychology) or the genital stage of psychosexual development. "Libido is object-seeking, not pleasure-seeking" is Fairbairn's.

Melanie Klein: phantasy, splitting and the two positions

Melanie Klein (1882 to 1960), born in Vienna, was analysed first by Sándor Ferenczi in Budapest and then by Karl Abraham in Berlin, and moved to London in 1926. She is the founder of the object relations tradition and its most radical thinker. Her clinical innovation was the play technique: she gave very young children, some not yet three years old (her patient Rita was two years and nine months), a set of small toys (figures, cars, bricks, water) and treated their play as the equivalent of an adult's free associations, interpreting it directly, including its aggressive and sexual meanings. From these analyses she concluded that infants have a rich and violent inner life of unconscious phantasy (Kleinians spell it with "ph" to distinguish it from conscious daydreams) from the first months, and that the Oedipus complex and the superego begin far earlier than Freud had thought.

Klein described infant development not in stages but in two positions, ways of organising experience that begin in infancy and to which people return throughout life. In the paranoid-schizoid position of the first months (she named it in 1946), the infant is overwhelmed by persecutory anxiety, the fear of being attacked and destroyed. It copes by splitting: dividing the self and its objects into wholly good and wholly bad parts and keeping them apart, so that there is a perfect, feeding "good breast" and a cruel, withholding "bad breast", as if they were two different mothers. Splitting protects the good from contamination by the bad. In the same paper Klein described projective identification: an unconscious phantasy of putting unwanted parts of the self into another person and then identifying with, and trying to control, that person. "Paranoid" names the anxiety (persecution from outside); "schizoid" names the defence (splitting).

Around the middle of the first year, as the infant comes to recognise that the mother who feeds and the mother who frustrates are one and the same whole person, it enters the depressive position (described in 1935). Now the anxiety changes: the infant fears not for itself but for the loved person, whom it has attacked in phantasy. The characteristic feelings are guilt, sadness and concern, and the characteristic response is reparation, the wish to repair the damage done. Reaching the depressive position is a developmental achievement: it brings the capacity for ambivalence (loving and hating the same person), for gratitude and for real concern. Klein also wrote about primitive envy (Envy and Gratitude, 1957), the wish to spoil what is good precisely because it is good and belongs to someone else, which she thought lay behind some negative therapeutic reactions.

Why does this matter clinically? Because adults under stress fall back into the paranoid-schizoid position. A patient who idealises her therapist one week and denounces her as heartless the next is splitting; a patient who makes his therapist feel inexplicably useless and contemptible may be using projective identification to make her carry his own unbearable sense of worthlessness. Paranoia, in Kleinian terms, is the paranoid-schizoid position revived: the world split into persecutors and saviours. Klein's successor Wilfred Bion (1897 to 1979) turned projective identification into a theory of how minds grow: a mother (and later a therapist) acts as a container who receives the infant's unbearable feelings, digests them and returns them in a bearable form. Kleinian technique reflects all this. Kleinian analysts interpret early and deeply, addressing unconscious phantasy and the negative transference directly from the first sessions.

Exam trap

In paranoia, "the ego's defence mechanism as given by Melanie Klein" is splitting, the defence of the paranoid-schizoid position. Projection is the tempting wrong option because it is Freud's classical explanation of paranoia: in the Schreber case (1911) Freud traced persecutory delusions to a forbidden homosexual wish that was denied and projected ("I love him" becomes "I hate him" becomes "he hates me"). Reaction formation and suppression are not Kleinian answers. Note also that Klein's "positions" are not stages: people move between them all their lives.

Klein's ideas collided with Anna Freud's in London. The two women disagreed about almost everything in child analysis: whether infants have an inner world of phantasy from birth, whether children form transferences, whether deep interpretation is safe. During the Second World War the British Psychoanalytical Society held a series of formal debates, the "Controversial Discussions" (1942 to 1944), which ended not in victory but in a compromise: the Society divided its training into Kleinian, Anna Freudian and "Middle" (later Independent) groups. The Independents, analysts who belonged to neither camp, produced some of the most humane ideas in the whole tradition.

Winnicott and the British Independents

Winnicott, whom you met as the author of "Hate in the Counter-Transference", is the Independent everyone quotes. Trained as a paediatrician, he saw tens of thousands of mothers and babies in his clinic, and his ideas have an observer's plainness. "There is no such thing as a baby," he once said in a meeting: wherever you find a baby you find a baby and someone caring for it.

The baby does not need a perfect mother. It needs a good-enough mother: one who, in the first weeks, adapts almost completely to the baby's needs (Winnicott called the state that makes this possible "primary maternal preoccupation"), and who then, gradually and tolerably, fails to adapt, so that the baby learns that the world is not under its magical control and begins to manage frustration for itself. A mother who is never allowed to fail would rob the child of this learning. Around such a baby the mother provides a holding environment: the physical and emotional holding, reliable, attentive and survivable, within which the infant can have experiences without being overwhelmed. Where holding is good enough, a true self develops, spontaneous and alive; where the environment repeatedly impinges on the infant and demands compliance, the child develops a false self, a polite, adapted surface that protects a hidden true self and leaves the person feeling unreal and empty (Winnicott, 1960).

Between the inner world of the infant and the outer world of real people lies a third area, the area of play, illusion and culture. Its first inhabitant is the transitional object (described in "Transitional Objects and Transitional Phenomena", 1953): the blanket, soft toy or corner of a cloth that a toddler clings to at bedtime and refuses to have washed. It is the child's first "not-me" possession, neither wholly invented nor wholly external, and it helps the child bear the mother's absence by standing between the two. Adults' transitional phenomena include art, religion and creative play, and Winnicott thought psychotherapy itself takes place "in the overlap of two areas of playing, that of the patient and that of the therapist". With children he used the squiggle game, in which he and the child took turns turning each other's random scribbles into drawings.

For the therapist, Winnicott's message was that the setting itself heals. The reliable frame of therapy (the same time, the same room, a therapist who survives the patient's anger without retaliating or collapsing) is a holding environment that can provide some of what the early environment failed to provide, and for some patients, especially those with a false self, this holding matters more than any interpretation.

Clinical link

A ward nurse who keeps the same kind, predictable routine with a frightened young patient admitted for a first psychotic episode, who is still there the morning after the patient has thrown a tray at her, is offering Winnicott's holding environment. Many of the "non-specific" ingredients of good mental health care (continuity of staff, predictable schedules, a team that does not retaliate) can be understood in these terms.

Another Independent, Michael Balint (1896 to 1970), a Hungarian analyst who settled in London, described the basic fault (1968), a sense deep in some patients that something is missing or wrong in them from the start, which ordinary interpretation cannot reach. Balint is also remembered for the "Balint groups" in which general practitioners discussed their difficult patients with an analyst, and for the brief focal therapy workshop he ran at the Tavistock Clinic with David Malan, which you will meet in Section 3.6.

Mahler: separation-individuation and the psychoses

In New York, the Hungarian-born paediatrician and analyst Margaret Mahler (1897 to 1985) set out to watch object relations being formed. She and her colleagues observed mothers and toddlers in a specially equipped nursery over several years and reported the results in The Psychological Birth of the Human Infant (1975, with Fred Pine and Anni Bergman). The infant's biological birth, Mahler said, is not matched by its psychological birth, which takes about three years and which she called separation-individuation: separation is the child's emergence from fusion with the mother; individuation is the development of the child's own characteristics and abilities.

Mahler described two forerunner phases and then four subphases, shown in Figure 3.3. In the normal autistic phase of the first weeks the newborn seems to live in a closed world of its own, more asleep than awake. In the normal symbiotic phase, up to about five months, the infant behaves as though it and the mother formed a single, all-powerful system. Separation-individuation proper then unfolds in four subphases. In differentiation or "hatching" (about 5 to 10 months) the baby turns outwards, explores the mother's face and jewellery, and shows the first reactions to strangers. In practising (about 10 to 16 months) the newly walking toddler has a "love affair with the world", exploring delightedly and returning to the mother now and then for "emotional refuelling". In rapprochement (about 15 to 24 months) the toddler realises how separate and small it is: it alternates between shadowing the mother and darting away, wants her near and yet resents her help, and goes through the clinging, tantrums and mood swings of the rapprochement crisis. Finally, in consolidation of individuality and the beginnings of emotional object constancy (about 24 to 36 months), the child forms a stable inner image of the mother, good and bad together, that comforts it when she is away. Later writers such as James Masterson linked borderline personality to difficulties in the rapprochement subphase, and Mahler herself, in her last years, conceded that infant research had made the idea of a normal "autistic" phase doubtful.

Mahler began her career with severely disturbed children, and her first major theory was a theory of psychosis. In 1952 she described two forms of childhood psychosis, an autistic form, in which the child behaves as though the mother did not exist, and a symbiotic form, in which the child cannot bear any separation from her. Generalising, she proposed that schizophrenia corresponds to the autistic phase of development: the person has failed to develop an adequate and stable symbiosis, and so never achieved the foundation from which a separate self could grow. Several other analysts wrote developmental and psychodynamic accounts of psychosis, and they appear together as options. Silvano Arieti (1914 to 1981), in Interpretation of Schizophrenia (1955, revised 1974), described the "palaeologic" thinking of schizophrenia, which identifies things that merely share a predicate (the von Domarus principle: "the Virgin Mary was a virgin; I am a virgin; therefore I am the Virgin Mary"). Ping-Nie Pao, at Chestnut Lodge, argued in Schizophrenic Disorders (1979) that the symptoms are the ego's attempts to adapt after an overwhelming "organismic panic". Edith Jacobson (1897 to 1978), who integrated ego psychology and object relations in The Self and the Object World (1964), described psychosis as a regressive fusion of the representations of self and object, and wrote influentially on depression. Paul Federn (1871 to 1950) traced schizophrenia to weak ego boundaries. (The disorder itself, and the much stronger modern evidence on its causes, belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book.)

Exam trap

"Schizophrenia corresponds to the autistic phase of development and the person fails to develop an adequate and stable symbiosis" is Margaret Mahler, not Silvano Arieti (palaeologic thinking), Ping-Nie Pao (organismic panic) or Edith Jacobson (fusion of self and object representations). Mahler's "autistic" phase has nothing to do with autism spectrum disorder as now defined. And in her sequence rapprochement (about 15 to 24 months) comes after practising and before object constancy; it is the subphase of shadowing and darting away and of the rapprochement crisis. "Emotional refuelling", the brief return to the mother between explorations, belongs to the practising subphase.

Kernberg: borderline organisation and transference-focused psychotherapy

Otto Kernberg (born 1928), who grew up in Vienna and Chile and later worked at the Menninger Clinic and in New York, brought ego psychology and Kleinian object relations together to explain the patients classical analysis could not help. He proposed that personality can be organised at three levels, judged by three criteria: how well integrated the person's identity is, how mature the defences are, and whether reality testing is intact. At the neurotic level identity is integrated, defences are mature and centred on repression, and reality testing is intact. At the psychotic level identity is poorly integrated, defences are primitive and reality testing is lost. Between them lies borderline personality organisation (described in 1967; Borderline Conditions and Pathological Narcissism, 1975): identity diffusion (a contradictory, unstable sense of self and others), primitive defences centred on splitting (with projective identification, primitive idealisation, devaluation, omnipotence and denial), but reality testing that is generally preserved. Kernberg traced this organisation to intense early frustration and aggression, which left the child unable to bring loving and hating images of the same person together, so that splitting persisted. Note that borderline personality organisation is a broad level of functioning that includes several personality disorders, not just the DSM's borderline personality disorder.

Kernberg's treatment, transference-focused psychotherapy (TFP), was manualised with John Clarkin and Frank Yeomans in 1999. It is a twice-weekly, face-to-face psychodynamic therapy that begins with an explicit treatment contract, which spells out how threats to the treatment (suicide attempts, self-harm, missed sessions) will be handled, so that destructive acting out does not swallow the therapy. Within that frame the therapist watches for the dominant object relation dyad that is active in the transference at each moment, for example "a cruel, controlling parent and a helpless, abused child", and notices how patient and therapist swap the roles (the patient who felt bullied last week is bullying the therapist today). The therapist uses Greenson's sequence of clarification, confrontation and interpretation in the here and now, keeps technical neutrality, and helps the patient bring the split images together into a more integrated, realistic view of self and others.

Key study: Clarkin and colleagues' borderline trial (2007)

John Clarkin, Kenneth Levy, Mark Lenzenweger and Kernberg randomly assigned 90 patients with borderline personality disorder to a year of transference-focused psychotherapy, dialectical behaviour therapy (DBT, Marsha Linehan's skills-based therapy taught in Chapter 5) or supportive psychodynamic therapy. All three groups improved on depression, anxiety and global and social functioning. TFP and DBT both reduced suicidality; TFP improved the most domains, including anger, irritability and assault. A companion analysis by Levy (2006) found that only TFP increased the patients' reflective function, their capacity to think about mental states. The criticism: a small sample, no untreated control, and the unexpected strength of the supportive condition. In a Dutch trial reported by Josephine Giesen-Bloo and colleagues in 2006, schema therapy (Jeffrey Young's integrative therapy, Chapter 5) produced more recovery and fewer dropouts than TFP over three years.

Table 3.5 gathers the theorists of this section. Notice how each one changed what the therapist listens for: Anna Freud the defences, Hartmann the adaptive capacities, Klein the phantasies and splits, Winnicott the holding and the true self, Mahler the struggle for separateness, Kernberg the split object relations played out in the room.

Table 3.5 Ego psychology and object relations: who said what.
