TheoristKey ideasWhat it means for therapy
Anna Freud (1936)Defences analysed; identification with the aggressor; developmental linesInterpret defences; child analysis with parents
Heinz Hartmann (1939)Conflict-free ego sphere; adaptation; autonomyAssess ego strength; support adaptive functions
Erik Erikson (1950)Psychosocial stages; identity crisisDevelopment continues across the lifespan
Melanie KleinPhantasy; splitting; projective identification; two positionsPlay technique; early, deep interpretation
Ronald Fairbairn (1952)Libido is object-seeking; internal saboteurAttachment to bad objects explained
Donald WinnicottGood-enough mother; holding; transitional object; false selfTherapy as a holding environment
Margaret Mahler (1975)Separation-individuation; rapprochement; object constancySeparation struggles in borderline patients
Otto Kernberg (1967, 1975)Borderline personality organisation; splittingTransference-focused psychotherapy
Exam trap

Match the treatment to its founders. Transference-focused psychotherapy is Otto Kernberg's (with Clarkin and Yeomans); mentalisation-based treatment is Anthony Bateman and Peter Fonagy's (Section 3.5); dialectical behaviour therapy is Marsha Linehan's; schema therapy is Jeffrey Young's. In an option list, "object relations, transference, splitting, projection" is Kernberg's vocabulary; "schema analysis, mode work, limited reparenting" is schema therapy; "mindfulness, distress tolerance, emotion regulation, interpersonal effectiveness" are DBT's four skills modules; and "free association, dream work, interpretation, working through" is classical psychoanalysis.

3.5 Self Psychology, Relational Analysis, Attachment and MentalisationLower yield · 1 Q

Section 3.4 ended with Otto Kernberg confronting the split, rageful object relations of borderline patients and interpreting them in the transference. In the same years another Viennese émigré, working in Chicago with a neighbouring group of patients, was reaching almost the opposite conclusion about how to help them. His ideas began a chain that runs through this whole section: from the self and its need for responsive people (Kohut), through the claim that the therapist is never a neutral observer but always a participant (relational analysis), to a research tradition that took the infant's tie to its mother into the laboratory (attachment theory) and then brought it back into the clinic as a treatment for borderline personality disorder (mentalisation-based treatment). You will feel the same shift of emphasis in every subsection: from conflict towards deficit, from interpretation alone towards the relationship as an agent of change, and from the patient's drives towards the patient's need to be understood.

Kohut and the psychology of the self

Heinz Kohut (1913 to 1981) qualified in medicine in Vienna, fled the Nazis in 1939 and reached Chicago in 1940, where he trained first in neurology and then at the Chicago Institute for Psychoanalysis. For twenty years he was a model of orthodoxy: a meticulous Freudian, president of the American Psychoanalytic Association in 1964 to 1965, known to colleagues, half admiringly and half mockingly, as "Mr Psychoanalysis". Then, in The Analysis of the Self (1971), The Restoration of the Self (1977) and the posthumous How Does Analysis Cure? (1984), he built a new school, self psychology, so different from Freud's that many analysts doubted whether it was psychoanalysis at all.

The patients who changed his mind had what he called narcissistic personality disorders. They were not neurotic in Freud's sense, because they did not suffer from a clear conflict between a forbidden wish and a prohibition, and they were not psychotic. What they described was harder to name: a sense of emptiness and inner deadness, self-esteem that soared at a compliment and collapsed at a slight, a hunger for admiration, sudden cold rages when they felt ignored, and a fear of falling apart. Freud had argued that patients whose libido is withdrawn into the self form no transference and so cannot be analysed. Kohut found something else. When he gave these patients classical interpretations ("your demand for my admiration defends you against your rivalry with your father"), they did not gain insight. They felt attacked, misunderstood and diminished, and they got worse. When instead he tried simply to understand, from inside, how it felt to need him so badly, they formed intense and peculiar transferences of their own and began, slowly, to improve.

From this experience Kohut drew a radical conclusion. Narcissism, the investment of love in the self, is not merely an immature stage to be outgrown on the way to loving others, as Freud thought. It has its own line of development, from archaic forms (the toddler's "Look at me!", the child's conviction that his parents are perfect) to mature ones. In a 1966 paper Kohut listed the mature transformations of narcissism as creativity, empathy, the capacity to contemplate one's own impermanence, humour and wisdom. Healthy narcissism is what lets a person feel real, valuable and alive; disorders arise when its development is derailed.

For Kohut the self is the centre of a person's initiative and experience, and it can be cohesive (firm, continuous, vital) or prone to falling apart. It does not grow on its own. It grows inside a matrix of others whom Kohut called selfobjects. A selfobject is another person experienced not as a separate centre of initiative but as part of the self, performing for it functions that it cannot yet perform for itself: soothing, admiring, calming, steadying. A mother whose face lights up when her toddler shows her a scribbled drawing (the "gleam in the mother's eye", in Kohut's phrase) is serving as a selfobject; so is the father who picks up a frightened child and lets him feel the father's calm strength as if it were his own. Kohut eventually wrote the word without a hyphen to stress that, in experience, the other person is part of the self rather than an object outside it.

Kohut described two main selfobject needs, and in 1977 he built a model of the self around them. The grandiose self (in full, the grandiose-exhibitionistic self) is the child's healthy wish to show off and be admired, and it needs mirroring: a responsive other who reflects back the child's delight in himself. The idealised parental imago is the child's image of a perfect, all-powerful parent, and it needs an idealisable other with whom the child can merge and from whom he can borrow calm and strength. In The Restoration of the Self these become the two poles of the bipolar self: a pole of ambitions, laid down by mirroring, and a pole of ideals, laid down by idealisation, joined by a "tension arc" of talents and skills with which the person pursues his ambitions under the guidance of his ideals. A child who was poorly mirrored can still build a strong self if an idealisable parent makes up for it, and the reverse; Kohut called these compensatory structures. Only when both poles fail does the self remain chronically enfeebled. In his last book Kohut added a third need, for twinship: the need to feel essentially alike, a human being among human beings, as when a small girl "shaves" beside her father at the mirror or rolls chapatis beside her grandmother in the kitchen.

When these needs are chronically unmet, Kohut argued, the result is not a neurosis organised around a conflict but a deficit, a self that lacks structure. Such a self is prone to fragmentation, the frightening sense of falling apart, feeling unreal and losing continuity (Kohut called the fear of it disintegration anxiety); to depletion, an emptiness and deadness that feels like depression without guilt; and to narcissistic rage, the cold, unforgiving fury, out of all proportion to its cause, with which an injured self reacts to a slight. Kohut contrasted two pictures of human beings. Freud's "Guilty Man" is torn between drives and conscience; Kohut's "Tragic Man" struggles, often without success, to realise the ambitions and ideals of a self that never received what it needed. The two pictures call for different therapies: the first for the interpretation of conflict, the second for an understanding relationship in which arrested development can resume. Kohut also held, unlike Freud, that selfobject needs never disappear. Mature adults still need people who appreciate them, ideals they can admire and others like themselves; health means finding and using such people well, not outgrowing the need for them.

Key study: "The Two Analyses of Mr Z" (1979)

In 1979 Kohut published the case of a young man he said he had analysed twice. In the first analysis he worked along classical lines, interpreting Mr Z's demanding sense of entitlement and his attachment to his mother as defences against Oedipal rivalry and castration anxiety. Mr Z complied and some symptoms eased, but Kohut later judged the change shallow. Some years later Mr Z returned, and the second analysis was conducted from the standpoint of self psychology. Now Kohut understood the mother as possessive and controlling, a woman who treated her son as an extension of herself, and saw the boy's need for a father he could admire. Freed from interpretations that implied he was guilty of forbidden wishes, Mr Z worked through his relationship with his mother and found a self of his own. The case was Kohut's demonstration that the same patient looks different, and changes differently, through two theories. The criticism: the case was written to make a point, and Kohut's son Thomas and his biographer Charles Strozier have argued that Mr Z was Kohut himself, thinly disguised. Kohut never said so, and Strozier admits that the evidence is circumstantial, but if they are right the "two analyses" are less a controlled comparison than a piece of autobiography.

Exam trap

A client says she is "no one", feels "empty inside", fears abandonment and has a history of intense, unstable relationships. What does the psychoanalytic account blame? Choose "a failure in parental mirroring and idealising, leading to a fragmented self". That is Kohut's self psychology, even when the stem says "object relations theory": many textbooks group self psychology with object relations, and the question uses the label loosely. The clue words are "pathology of the self", "empty", "fragmented", "mirroring" and "idealising". Fixation at the oral stage is classical drive theory; an unresolved Oedipus complex with castration anxiety is the classical account of neurosis; harsh superego demands with constant guilt describe Freud's "Guilty Man", exactly what Kohut said these patients were not. Kernberg would explain the same picture as identity diffusion maintained by splitting (Section 3.4), but that is not among the options.

The selfobject transferences

What Kohut discovered in the consulting room was that these patients do form transferences, but not the kind Freud described. In a classical transference the patient experiences the analyst as a figure from the past (a rival father, a seductive mother) towards whom he has forbidden wishes. In a selfobject transference the patient experiences the analyst as a selfobject: as part of himself, needed to hold his self together. An old, thwarted developmental need comes alive again and fastens onto the therapist. Kohut described three forms, summarised in Table 3.6.

In the mirror transference the revived need is for the grandiose self to be confirmed. The patient needs the therapist to notice, appreciate and admire him, and may talk at length about his achievements while barely registering the therapist as a person. A therapist who glances at the clock during the story of a promotion may be met with a wounded silence or a contemptuous attack. Kohut also described an archaic version, the merger transference, in which the patient treats the therapist almost as an extension of himself, expected to think and feel exactly as he does.

In the idealising transference the revived need is for a perfect, powerful figure to merge with. The patient experiences the therapist as wise, strong and calm, and feels soothed and held together simply by being in the presence of someone so admirable. Such patients often fall apart during the therapist's holidays, or on discovering that the therapist is ordinary: has a cold, forgets a name, drives an old car.

In the twinship transference (also called the alter ego transference) the need is to feel essentially like the therapist. The patient takes comfort from shared tastes, discovers with delight that the therapist also grew up in a small town, or quietly adopts the therapist's turns of phrase. Kohut first described twinship in 1971 as a variety of the mirror transference and in 1984 raised it to a third selfobject transference in its own right.

Table 3.6 Kohut's three selfobject transferences.
TransferenceNeed revivedWhat the therapist is needed to beTypical sign in sessions
Mirror (including merger)Grandiose self: to be seen and admiredAn attentive, admiring audienceHurt or rage when the therapist seems inattentive
IdealisingIdealised parental imago: to merge with strengthWise, calm and powerfulCalm in the therapist's presence; collapse at breaks or on finding a flaw
Twinship (alter ego)To feel essentially alikeLike the patientDelight in shared tastes; copying the therapist's style

The decisive technical point is what the therapist does with these transferences. A classical analyst would interpret them as resistances, defences against deeper wishes, to be exposed and given up. Kohut insisted that they be accepted and allowed to unfold, because they are not defences but the very developmental needs whose earlier frustration caused the damage. The therapist does not gratify them (he does not flatter the patient or pretend to be perfect), but neither does he interpret them away. He understands them and lets the patient use him, for a time, as the missing selfobject. Kohut's patient Mr J., a creative writer in his early thirties, once mentioned in passing that he carefully rinsed his shaving brush, cleaned his razor and scrubbed the sink before washing his face. Kohut heard the arrogance with which the trivial detail was offered and traced it, over many months and against strong resistance born of shame, to a self-centred mother who had seemed to value her jewellery and silverware more than her son. As the mirror transference was understood and worked through, Mr J. became able, as he put it jokingly, "to prefer my face to the razor".

Exam trap

Kohut's three selfobject transferences are mirror, idealising and twinship. Twinship and alter ego are the same transference, not two different ones; merger is an archaic form of the mirror transference. Do not confuse the idealising transference with Freud's positive transference: positive transference is affection for a figure from the past, whereas idealisation in Kohut's sense is a need to borrow strength from a perfect figure in order to feel whole. Do not confuse Kohut's mirroring with Freud's image of the analyst as a mirror (Section 3.2): Freud's analyst was an opaque mirror that shows only what the patient shows, while Kohut's mirroring is the warm, admiring response a child needs. Splitting, projective identification and the paranoid-schizoid position belong to Klein and Kernberg, not to Kohut.

Empathy, optimal frustration and transmuting internalisation

If the therapist does not interpret the selfobject transferences away, how does the patient ever change? Kohut's answer rests on three linked ideas.

The first is empathy, which Kohut defined in a famous 1959 paper as vicarious introspection: the attempt to experience the inner life of another person while remaining oneself. For Kohut empathy was first of all a method of observation, the only means by which one mind can gather data about another, much as a telescope is the astronomer's means of seeing a distant planet. The therapist's continuous effort to understand the patient from the patient's own point of view, to sense what it is like to be him from moment to moment, is called empathic attunement, and it is the medium in which self psychology works. Empathy in this sense is not sympathy, agreement or niceness. A therapist can understand from inside why a patient wants to humiliate his brother without approving of it.

The second idea is that empathy inevitably fails. No therapist, however attuned, understands perfectly: the therapist is late, misses the point, announces a holiday, answers the telephone. Each failure can revive the old injury, and the patient reacts with a disruption: sudden coldness, rage out of proportion, a return of symptoms, a feeling of falling apart. Kohut taught that these moments are not accidents to be regretted but the engine of the cure, provided they are small enough to bear and are understood afterwards. He called a failure of this kind, non-traumatic, tolerable and followed by repair, optimal frustration. The therapist's response to a disruption comes in two steps, which Kohut (1984) called understanding and explaining. In the understanding phase the therapist conveys that he grasps what the patient felt ("When I changed the time of our session, it seemed to you that you did not matter to me at all"). In the explaining phase he offers an interpretation of why the patient reacts in this particular way, linking it to the history of selfobject failures ("as it felt when your mother left you with your aunt for months and seemed not to notice how much you missed her").

The third idea is what this repeated cycle builds. Each time a manageable failure is understood and repaired, the patient takes over, in a small way, the function the therapist had been performing for him. He soothes himself a little; he holds his self-esteem a little steadier; he waits a little more calmly for the next session. Kohut called this transmuting internalisation: the gradual, piecemeal conversion of a selfobject's function into a lasting part of the patient's own self structure. "Transmuting" means changing form: what was once something the other person did becomes something the self can do. Kohut thought it happens in childhood in exactly the same way. A mother who always comes when her baby cries, but occasionally arrives a minute late, gives the baby a minute in which to calm itself a little with the remembered sense of her; a mother who never fails gives no such opportunity, and a mother who fails massively gives terror instead of a lesson. Figure 3.4 draws the cycle.

You may notice how close this is to Winnicott's good-enough mother (Section 3.4), who fails gradually and tolerably so that the baby learns to manage frustration. The resemblance is real: the two men described the same developmental truth from different starting points. Later self psychologists softened Kohut's emphasis on frustration; Howard Bacal (1985), for example, argued that what heals is the therapist's optimal responsiveness, responding in whatever way this patient needs at this moment, rather than frustration as such. Kohut's ideas also provoked one of the great debates of modern psychoanalysis, his disagreement with Kernberg about narcissism, which Table 3.7 sets out.

Table 3.7 Kohut and Kernberg on pathological narcissism.
QuestionKohut (self psychology)Kernberg (object relations)
What is it?An arrest of normal narcissistic developmentA pathological structure, different in kind from normal narcissism
Core problemDeficit: unmet mirroring and idealising needsConflict: a pathological grandiose self defends against rage and envy
OriginChronic failures of parental empathyCold, rejecting care and intense early aggression
The grandiosityA normal need, revived in the transferenceA defence to be confronted
TechniqueEmpathic immersion; let the transference unfold; understand, then explainConfront and interpret, especially negative transference and envy
Danger each sees in the otherConfrontation re-traumatisesEmpathy colludes with grandiosity
Clinical link

Rohan, a 31-year-old software architect in Pune, comes to therapy after his engagement collapses. For weeks he speaks of his successes and hardly seems to notice his therapist, though he always arrives early and visibly relaxes as he talks. One week the therapist, distracted, gets the name of his company wrong. Rohan goes icily quiet, then announces that therapy is a waste of money. A classical response would interpret his arrogance as a defence. A self-psychological response begins with understanding: "When I got the name wrong, it felt as if everything you have told me had gone in one ear and out of the other, as if I was not really here for you at all." Rohan's coldness softens. Explaining comes later: his father, a busy surgeon, rarely remembered his school results, and Rohan learned to perform achievements for people who did not look. Over many such cycles Rohan becomes less dependent on being admired and better able to let his partner's attention wander without feeling erased.

Exam trap

Transmuting internalisation, optimal frustration, selfobject, bipolar self, narcissistic rage and "vicarious introspection" are all Kohut's. Kohut's empathy is not Carl Rogers' accurate empathy (Chapter 6): for Rogers empathy is one of three core conditions and is curative in itself, while for Kohut it is first a mode of observation, and cure comes through understanding, explaining and the transmuting internalisation that follows repaired failures. In Kohut's theory pathological narcissism is a deficit (a developmental arrest); in Kernberg's it is a defensive structure built on rage and envy. "Confront the grandiose self" is Kernberg; "allow the idealising transference to unfold" is Kohut.

Relational psychoanalysis and intersubjectivity

Kohut still thought of the analyst as an observer of the patient's inner world, however empathic. From the 1980s a group of mainly New York analysts pushed further. In Object Relations in Psychoanalytic Theory (1983) Jay Greenberg and Stephen Mitchell (1946 to 2000) sorted the whole of psychoanalysis into two families. In the drive/structure model of Freud and the ego psychologists, the mind is built from the fate of the drives, and other people matter because they satisfy or frustrate them. In the relational/structure model of Sullivan, Fairbairn and Winnicott, the mind is built from relationships themselves. Kohut, in their reading, was a "mixed model" theorist who tried to keep a foot in both camps. In Relational Concepts in Psychoanalysis (1988) Mitchell argued for the second family, and in 1991 he founded the journal Psychoanalytic Dialogues, which gave the movement a home. Relational psychoanalysis is the resulting school: an integration of Harry Stack Sullivan's interpersonal psychoanalysis (Section 3.8), British object relations and self psychology, held together by the conviction that the mind is relational through and through.

The most important consequence concerns the analyst. Classical theory is a one-person psychology: the analyst stands outside the patient's conflicts like a neutral scientist and observes a process that unfolds inside the patient. Relational analysts hold that therapy is a two-person psychology. Both people bring their own subjectivity, history and unconscious into the room; each influences the other all the time; and what happens between them is created by both. The analyst cannot be a blank screen, because even silence and neutrality are communications that the patient reads and reacts to. The classical virtues of neutrality and anonymity (Section 3.2) are therefore not merely difficult but impossible, and pretending to them can do harm. The analyst's own feelings, reactions and mistakes become legitimate material, and judicious self-disclosure is sometimes used. Countertransference, which Heimann had already turned into a tool (Section 3.3), becomes something the patient also perceives and is entitled to talk about.

Lewis Aron (1952 to 2019), in A Meeting of Minds (1996), gave the most balanced statement of the position. The therapeutic relationship, he argued, is mutual, because both people are subjects who affect each other and the patient often perceives the analyst accurately, but it is also asymmetrical, because their roles, responsibilities and purposes differ: the therapy exists for the patient's benefit, and the analyst carries the responsibility for the frame. A parallel school developed similar ideas under the name intersubjectivity. Robert Stolorow, George Atwood and Bernard Brandchaft, who began within self psychology, argued in Psychoanalytic Treatment: An Intersubjective Approach (1987) that every psychological event in therapy arises in an intersubjective field, the meeting point of two differently organised subjective worlds, so that a patient's "resistance" or "transference" can never be understood apart from the particular analyst who meets it. Jessica Benjamin, another relational writer, built a theory around mutual recognition, the capacity of two people to experience each other as separate centres of feeling.

Relational analysts pay special attention to enactments. An enactment is an episode in which patient and therapist, without realising it, act out together a pattern from their inner worlds, as when a therapist who meant to stay neutral finds herself repeatedly rescuing a helpless patient and so playing the part of the patient's anxious mother. Classical analysts treated such events as failures of technique; relational analysts see them as inevitable and, once noticed and talked about, among the richest sources of understanding. Picture a patient who begins a session in real distress because her analyst greeted her in the waiting room without using her name, which felt to her unbearably impersonal. The analyst's first reaction is to feel stung. Instead of treating her reaction simply as a symptom of her problem (a one-person view), a relational analyst asks what he has contributed: perhaps he has been feeling held at a distance by her, and has unconsciously retaliated by toning down his warmth at the beginning and end of their sessions. The exploration of what both of them were doing, not just what she was doing, became the work.

Exam trap

Relational psychoanalysis is Stephen Mitchell's (with Jay Greenberg in 1983; Mitchell alone in 1988), and Lewis Aron's "mutuality and asymmetry" belongs to it. Intersubjectivity theory is Robert Stolorow and George Atwood's. The shared idea is a two-person psychology: the analyst is a participant, not a neutral observer, so classical neutrality and the blank screen are rejected. Do not confuse Sullivan's interpersonal psychoanalysis, one root of the relational school (Section 3.8), with Klerman and Weissman's interpersonal psychotherapy (Section 3.7), a manualised, time-limited treatment for depression that does not interpret the transference.

Attachment theory in the consulting room

Every theorist so far built a picture of infancy from adult patients on couches, from child analyses or, in Mahler's case, from nursery observation guided by analytic theory. John Bowlby (1907 to 1990) wanted something different: a theory of the child's tie to its mother that could be tested by direct observation and that took seriously what had actually happened to a child, not only what the child imagined. Bowlby trained as an analyst in London in the 1930s within the Kleinian group, and Klein herself supervised one of his child cases. In a story he told late in life, the mother of the little boy he was treating was admitted to a mental hospital, and Klein would not allow him to speak with her, because what mattered in the analysis was the child's phantasies, not the real events of his life. Bowlby never accepted this. His early study "Forty-four Juvenile Thieves" (1944) traced the "affectionless" character of some young offenders to prolonged early separations from their mothers, and his report for the World Health Organization, Maternal Care and Mental Health (1951), made maternal deprivation a public health issue. In the trilogy Attachment and Loss (1969, 1973, 1980) he drew on ethology, evolutionary biology and control systems theory to build attachment theory.

The theory's central claims can be stated simply. Human infants are born with an attachment behavioural system, shaped by evolution to keep them close to a protective caregiver; crying, clinging, following and smiling all serve to maintain closeness, especially when the infant is frightened, ill or tired. The caregiver serves as a safe haven to return to in distress and as a secure base from which to explore, a phrase that came from the observations of Bowlby's collaborator Mary Ainsworth (1913 to 1999) and that Bowlby made the title of his clinical lectures, A Secure Base (1988). Bowlby also proposed monotropy, the infant's tendency to form its strongest bond with one principal figure, although later research showed that most infants form a small hierarchy of attachments, a finding that will not surprise anyone raised by a mother, a grandmother and two aunts in an Indian joint family. From thousands of repeated interactions the child builds an internal working model (Bowlby took the term from the psychologist Kenneth Craik): a mental representation of whether attachment figures can be relied on to respond, and of whether the self is the kind of person who deserves care. These models are carried into later relationships, largely outside awareness, and they shape what a person expects, notices and does. You will recognise the family resemblance to Freud's transference and to Klein's internal objects; the difference is that Bowlby's models are built from real experience and can be studied.

The research bridge was Ainsworth's Strange Situation, a twenty-minute laboratory sequence of brief separations from and reunions with the mother, which sorted one-year-olds into secure, insecure-avoidant and insecure-resistant (ambivalent) patterns (Patterns of Attachment, 1978, with Mary Blehar, Everett Waters and Sally Wall); Mary Main (born 1943) and Judith Solomon later added a fourth, disorganised pattern. The procedure and the infant patterns are taught in the Developmental book and in Chapter 10. Here what matters is what attachment theory asks of a therapist.

In A Secure Base Bowlby set out five tasks for the therapist. The first is to provide the patient with a secure base from which he can explore the unhappy and painful aspects of his life, past and present. The second is to help him explore how he engages in relationships with the significant figures in his current life. The third is to encourage him to examine the relationship with the therapist, where his expectations of attachment figures will inevitably appear. The fourth is to help him consider how his current perceptions, expectations, feelings and actions may be the product of what happened to him in childhood and adolescence, especially with his parents, or of what he was repeatedly told by them. The fifth is to help him recognise that his models of himself and others, built from those painful experiences or misleading messages, may not fit his present and future, and so to revise them. Notice the order: safety first, exploration second, and only then the link between past and present that classical analysis had put at the centre.

Attachment theory also gives the clinician a way of reading how a patient uses therapy itself. Patients whose attachment is dismissing tend to keep the therapist at arm's length: they minimise their problems, intellectualise, insist that their childhoods were "fine", and cancel sessions precisely when they begin to feel something. Preoccupied patients tend to flood the sessions with distress, cling, telephone between sessions and find breaks and endings agonising. Patients with unresolved trauma or loss may become confused or dissociated when the trauma is approached. Each needs something different: the dismissing patient gentle encouragement towards feeling and need, the preoccupied patient structure and containment. In a 1994 study Mary Dozier and her colleagues found that case managers who were themselves securely attached tended to respond to their clients' underlying needs, gently countering a client's habitual style, whereas insecure case managers tended to respond to the surface presentation and so to confirm it. The therapist's own attachment history, like the countertransference of Section 3.3, enters the room.

Exam trap

Attachment theory, internal working models, monotropy and the therapist as a "secure base" are Bowlby's; the Strange Situation is Mary Ainsworth's; the disorganised infant pattern was added by Mary Main and Judith Solomon. Bowlby is not the answer to "identity crisis" (Erik Erikson, Section 3.4). Attachment theory is a close cousin of object relations theory but not the same thing: object relations theorists infer internalised relationships and phantasies from clinical material, while Bowlby studied observable behaviour and real events. Bowlby, with Colin Murray Parkes, also described four phases of grief (Chapter 9).

The Adult Attachment Interview

The Strange Situation measures attachment in infants. Could attachment be measured in adults, and would a parent's attachment predict her child's? The breakthrough came from Mary Main, a former student of Ainsworth's, who with Carol George and Nancy Kaplan developed the Adult Attachment Interview (AAI) at the University of California, Berkeley, in the mid-1980s. The AAI is a semi-structured interview of about an hour and some twenty questions about the speaker's childhood. The speaker is asked for five adjectives describing the relationship with each parent in childhood and then for specific memories that illustrate each adjective; what she did when she was upset, hurt or ill as a child; what her first separations were like; whether she ever felt rejected or threatened; why she thinks her parents behaved as they did; whether she lost anyone important; and how she thinks these experiences shaped her as an adult.

Main's insight lay in the scoring. The AAI is not scored for what happened, for whether the childhood was happy or cruel, but for how the speaker talks about it: the coherence of the narrative. Coherence is judged against the philosopher Paul Grice's maxims of cooperative conversation: quality (be truthful and have evidence for what you say), quantity (be succinct yet complete), relation (be relevant) and manner (be clear and orderly). Its authors described the interview as "surprising the unconscious": the speaker must recall painful matters and at the same time keep track of her own discourse, and the way she manages this reveals her current state of mind with respect to attachment. Four main classifications result.

  • Secure-autonomous speakers give a coherent, balanced account, whether their childhood was good or bad. They value attachment relationships and can reflect on them, including on their parents' failings, with some understanding and often some forgiveness.
  • Dismissing speakers play down the importance of attachment. They often describe their parents in idealising general terms ("loving", "normal", "wonderful") that their specific memories fail to support or even contradict, insist that they cannot remember much of their childhood, and give short, guarded answers.
  • Preoccupied speakers are still entangled with their parents. Their accounts are long, rambling or confused, slip into present-tense anger at a parent ("and she still does that, she never listens") or become vague and passive, and lose track of the question.
  • Unresolved (or disorganised) speakers show lapses in the monitoring of reasoning or discourse when they discuss loss or abuse: speaking of a dead person as though still alive, falling into long silences, or drifting into eulogy. This classification is given alongside one of the other three.

The remarkable finding is that each adult category corresponds to an infant pattern, as Figure 3.5 shows. In 1985 Main, Kaplan and Jude Cassidy reported that parents' interview classifications matched the Strange Situation classifications their children had received years earlier. The most persuasive test came from London, from Peter Fonagy (born 1952), a Hungarian-born British psychoanalyst and researcher at University College London and the Anna Freud Centre.

Key study: Fonagy, Steele and Steele's London Parent-Child Project (1991)

If a parent's state of mind shapes her infant's attachment, rather than the infant's behaviour shaping the parent's memories, then the parent's interview should predict the attachment of a baby who has not yet been born. Peter Fonagy, Howard Steele and Miriam Steele gave the AAI to about a hundred first-time mothers in London during pregnancy and then observed each infant with its mother in the Strange Situation at one year. The mothers' interviews predicted whether their infants would be secure or insecure in about three cases in four. A meta-analysis by Marinus van IJzendoorn (1995) confirmed the correspondence across many samples, while pointing to a "transmission gap": the mother's observed sensitivity explains only part of the link. The criticism: correspondence is not proof of causation, and genes, temperament and shared circumstances may contribute. The clinical point is striking all the same. It is not a happy childhood that makes a secure parent but a coherent account of whatever childhood one had: adults who describe painful histories coherently, said to show earned security, tend to have secure infants too.

Exam trap

The Adult Attachment Interview is Mary Main's (with Carol George and Nancy Kaplan), not Ainsworth's (the Strange Situation) or Bowlby's. It is scored for the coherence of the narrative, not for the content of the childhood. Learn the pairs: secure-autonomous with secure infants, dismissing with avoidant, preoccupied with resistant (ambivalent), unresolved with disorganised. "Dismissing" and "preoccupied" are adult labels; "avoidant" and "resistant" are infant ones. The AAI as an instrument belongs with the assessment tools; it matters here because it led directly to the idea of mentalisation.

Mentalisation and mentalisation-based treatment

Analysing the London interviews, Fonagy's group found something the four categories did not capture. Some parents, describing their own parents, spontaneously thought about minds: "I think my father was frightened himself, and that is why he shouted"; "I didn't understand then that she was depressed." Others described only events and behaviour. Fonagy and Mary Target called this capacity reflective function and devised a scale for rating it in AAI transcripts. Parents with high reflective function were much more likely to have secure infants, and among mothers who had themselves suffered severe deprivation, high reflective function seemed to protect the next generation: in a 1994 report, all ten of the deprived mothers who could reflect well on minds had secure babies, against only one of the seventeen who could not.

Fonagy made this capacity the centre of a theory. Mentalisation (or mentalising) is the capacity to understand oneself and others in terms of intentional mental states: feelings, wishes, beliefs, intentions and goals. Its clinicians like to sum it up as "seeing yourself from the outside and others from the inside", or as "holding mind in mind". You are mentalising when you realise that your friend snapped at you because she is anxious about her viva, not because she despises you, or when you notice that your irritation this morning has more to do with a bad night's sleep than with your brother. Reflective function is the measured form of the same capacity. Mentalising is fragile in everyone. It switches off when we are frightened, enraged or in the grip of intense attachment feelings, which is why partners in a quarrel say things about each other's motives that neither believes an hour later.

How does a child acquire it? Fonagy and his colleagues argued that a child discovers its own mind by finding it in the mind of a caregiver. When a baby is distressed, an attuned mother reflects the distress back, but in a special way that the Hungarian developmental psychologist György Gergely and John Watson (1996) called marked mirroring. Her face shows the baby's feeling, so the mirroring is contingent (it matches what the baby feels), but in an exaggerated, slightly playful, "as if" form, so that it is marked as a picture of the baby's state rather than as the mother's own feeling. The baby learns, in effect, "that is what I am feeling, and it can be held and thought about". If the mother shows real panic instead (unmarked mirroring), the baby's fear is amplified rather than contained; if she consistently mirrors something other than what the baby feels, the baby takes in a representation that does not fit its experience, which Fonagy called an "alien self". A secure attachment relationship is the natural training ground for mentalising.

Before full mentalising develops, at around four years of age, young children experience the mind in three more primitive ways, which the theory calls prementalising modes (Table 3.8). In psychic equivalence inner and outer are equated: what I feel or think is simply true of the world ("I feel worthless, so I am worthless"; a nightmare feels real). In pretend mode the inner world is cut off from reality, so that thoughts and feelings float free of anything that could test them; in adults it appears as fluent, plausible psychological talk that changes nothing (sometimes called pseudomentalising) or as dissociation. In the teleological mode mental states are recognised only through physical, observable outcomes, so that love is proved only by action ("if you cared, you would reply within a minute"). Adults return to these modes whenever mentalising collapses.

Table 3.8 The three prementalising modes, as they appear in borderline personality disorder.
ModeWhat happens to the mindHow it sounds in therapy
Psychic equivalenceInner experience is taken as outer reality"You think I'm pathetic. I can see it. Don't deny it."
Pretend modeThoughts and feelings are cut off from reality and from felt emotionFluent talk about "my abandonment issues" with no feeling and no change
Teleological modeOnly physical action counts as evidence of a mental state"If you really cared you'd give me your mobile number"; self-harm to make distress visible

Anthony Bateman, a British psychiatrist, and Fonagy proposed that the core problem in borderline personality disorder (the disorder itself is described in the Psychiatric Disorders book) is a fragile capacity to mentalise, rooted in disrupted early attachment and often in trauma, combined with an attachment system that is easily and intensely switched on. When a person with this vulnerability feels rejected, mentalising collapses, the prementalising modes take over, and the familiar crises follow: the certainty that one is hated (psychic equivalence), desperate actions to force a response or to get rid of unbearable states (teleological mode), and the hollow sense that nothing is real (pretend mode). This is a deficit model, closer to Kohut than to Kernberg: the patient lacks a capacity rather than defending against a conflict.

Mentalisation-based treatment (MBT) was developed by Bateman and Fonagy at the Halliwick Unit of St Ann's Hospital in north London, first as an eighteen-month day-hospital programme and later as an intensive outpatient programme that combines weekly individual and weekly group sessions over about eighteen months. Its aim is not insight into the unconscious but the recovery and strengthening of mentalising, above all in the heat of attachment relationships. Its techniques follow from that aim.

  • The therapist takes a not-knowing stance: genuinely curious, asking rather than telling, never claiming to know what is in the patient's mind ("Help me understand what went through your mind when he didn't reply"). The patient learns curiosity about minds by seeing it modelled.
  • The focus is on the patient's current mental states and recent events, not on the distant past or on deep unconscious meanings.
  • Interventions are short and simple. Long, complex interpretations, the stock in trade of transference-focused psychotherapy and classical analysis, are avoided, because a patient whose arousal is high cannot use them and may hear them as attacks or as proof that the therapist knows his mind better than he does.
  • The therapist manages arousal. When emotion is too intense, she first offers support and empathy to bring it down to a level at which thinking is possible; when the talk is emotionally flat (pretend mode), she brings feeling back in.
  • After a crisis, therapist and patient "stop, rewind and explore": they go back to the moment before the crisis and examine, step by step, what each person was feeling and thinking.
  • The relationship with the therapist is examined in the here and now ("mentalising the relationship"), but cautiously and later in treatment, and the therapist openly owns her own misunderstandings, modelling the fact that minds are opaque and that mistakes can be repaired.

Here is how it sounds. Priya, a 22-year-old student in Delhi, cut her arm on Saturday night after her boyfriend did not answer her messages for two hours. A classical therapist might look for the unconscious meaning of the act. An MBT therapist rewinds. "Let's slow down and go back to just before you picked up the blade. You had sent the third message. What was going on in your mind?" Priya: "He was with his friends, laughing about me. I knew it." Therapist: "You knew it. It felt completely certain." (She is naming psychic equivalence without the jargon.) "Did any other explanation for two hours of silence cross your mind, even for a second?" Priya, after a pause: "His phone was nearly dead in the afternoon... but it didn't feel like that." Therapist: "So part of you had another idea, but the feeling of being laughed at was so strong it drowned it out. What happened in your body when you felt that?" Nothing here interprets Priya's childhood. The work is to slow the moment down until two minds, hers and her boyfriend's, become visible again where there had been only one certainty.

Key study: Bateman and Fonagy's Halliwick trials (1999 to 2009)

In 1999 Bateman and Fonagy reported a randomised trial in which 38 patients with borderline personality disorder received either up to eighteen months of a psychoanalytically oriented day-hospital programme (the treatment later named MBT) or standard psychiatric care. The day-hospital group showed falls in suicidal and self-harming acts, inpatient days, depression and anxiety, and better social and interpersonal functioning, beginning at about six months; the control group showed little change or got worse. In 2008, in the American Journal of Psychiatry, they reported an eight-year follow-up, five years after treatment had ended: suicidal acts during the follow-up years had occurred in 23 per cent of the MBT group against 74 per cent of the group that had received treatment as usual, and only 13 per cent of the MBT group still met diagnostic criteria for the disorder, against 87 per cent. A larger outpatient trial in 2009 (134 patients) compared MBT with structured clinical management, a good-quality non-specialist treatment; both groups improved substantially, and MBT produced steeper falls in suicidal and self-harming acts and in hospital admissions. The criticism: the early trials were small, single-site and run by the treatment's developers, the comparison treatment in the first trial was weak, and independent replications have produced more mixed results.

Exam trap

Mentalisation-based treatment is Anthony Bateman and Peter Fonagy's. It is a psychodynamic, attachment-based treatment for borderline personality disorder, not a cognitive-behavioural or mindfulness therapy. Watch the look-alike abbreviations: MBT (mentalisation-based treatment), MBCT (mindfulness-based cognitive therapy, Segal, Williams and Teasdale) and MBSR (mindfulness-based stress reduction, Kabat-Zinn), the last two in Chapter 5. Mentalising is not mindfulness: mindfulness is non-judgemental attention to present experience, while mentalising is understanding behaviour in terms of mental states. Reflective function is the measured form of mentalising, rated on the Adult Attachment Interview. Psychic equivalence, pretend mode and the teleological mode are the three prementalising modes.

How MBT differs from TFP, and other attachment-based treatments

Mentalisation-based treatment and transference-focused psychotherapy (Section 3.4) are the two best-known psychodynamic treatments for borderline personality disorder. Both are manualised, both have been tested in randomised trials, and both were developed by psychoanalysts. Students therefore confuse them, and examiners know it. They differ in their theory of the disorder, in their aim and in almost everything the therapist says, as Table 3.9 shows.

Take the theories first. Kernberg's TFP rests on object relations theory and a conflict model: borderline patients hold split, all-good and all-bad representations of self and others, kept apart by splitting because intense aggression threatens to destroy the good ones. MBT rests on attachment theory and a deficit model: the patient lacks a robust capacity to mentalise, and what capacity there is collapses under attachment stress. The aims follow from the theories. TFP seeks to integrate the split representations into a coherent identity, a structural change in personality; MBT seeks to restore mentalising, so that the patient can go on thinking about minds when feelings run high. The techniques follow from the aims. The TFP therapist, from a stance of technical neutrality and within a firm treatment contract, identifies the dominant object relation dyad in the transference and interprets it, using clarification, confrontation and interpretation in the here and now. The MBT therapist, from a not-knowing stance, avoids complex interpretation, asks rather than tells, and works mostly on the patient's mental states in recent events, turning to the relationship with the therapist cautiously and late. In TFP the therapist's understanding of the patient's mind is offered to the patient; in MBT the therapist is careful not to know better, because a patient who is told what is in his mind is excused the effort of working it out. Both can claim research support: TFP from the trial by Clarkin and colleagues described in Section 3.4, in which it also raised reflective function, and from a German and Austrian trial led by Stephan Doering (2010), in which it outperformed treatment by experienced community psychotherapists; MBT from the Halliwick trials above.

Table 3.9 Transference-focused psychotherapy and mentalisation-based treatment compared.
FeatureTFP (Kernberg, Clarkin, Yeomans)MBT (Bateman and Fonagy)
Root theoryObject relations (Kernberg, after Klein)Attachment theory and developmental research
Model of the disorderConflict: split images defend against aggressionDeficit: fragile mentalising that collapses under stress
AimIntegrate split images into a coherent identityRestore and strengthen mentalising
Therapist's stanceTechnical neutrality; the expert who interpretsNot-knowing and curious; owns mistakes
Main techniqueClarify, confront and interpret the active dyadShort questions; stop, rewind, explore; manage arousal
Use of the transferenceCentral, from early in treatmentCautious and late ("mentalising the relationship")
Usual formatIndividual, twice weekly, with a treatment contractIndividual plus group, about 18 months

MBT is the best known of a wider family of attachment-based treatments, therapies that take attachment theory as their map. With infants and parents, the American social worker and analyst Selma Fraiberg (1918 to 1981) described in "Ghosts in the Nursery" (1975, with Edna Adelson and Vivian Shapiro) how parents' unremembered childhood pain intrudes into their care of their babies. She treated parent and baby together, often in the family's own kitchen, and her infant-parent psychotherapy grew into Alicia Lieberman's child-parent psychotherapy for traumatised children under five. The Circle of Security programme teaches parents, with video clips of their own interactions, to act as a secure base for exploration and a safe haven in distress, and video-feedback programmes from Leiden in the Netherlands use filmed play to increase parental sensitivity. With adolescents, Guy Diamond's attachment-based family therapy repairs ruptured trust between depressed or suicidal teenagers and their parents. With couples, Sue Johnson's emotionally focused couple therapy treats partners as each other's attachment figures (Chapter 6 and Section 7.8). In individual adult therapy, Bowlby's five tasks underlie a great deal of practice that carries no brand name at all. Schema therapy and cognitive analytic therapy (Section 5.7) also borrow attachment ideas, and dialectical behaviour therapy (Section 5.8) is the main non-psychodynamic rival in borderline personality disorder.

Exam trap

A question asking who developed interpersonal psychotherapy may list four founders who are easy to scramble. Klerman and Weissman developed interpersonal psychotherapy (Section 3.7). Peter Fonagy and Anthony Bateman developed mentalisation-based treatment. Otto Kernberg developed transference-focused psychotherapy. "L. Benjamin" is Lorna Smith Benjamin, who developed the Structural Analysis of Social Behavior and interpersonal reconstructive therapy (Section 3.6). In a question about borderline treatments, "object relations, transference, splitting, projection" is TFP's vocabulary; "mentalising, not-knowing stance, stop and rewind" is MBT's; "mindfulness, distress tolerance, emotion regulation, interpersonal effectiveness" are DBT's skills modules; "schema analysis, mode work, limited reparenting" is schema therapy. If an option makes the interpretation of split object relations in the transference the main technique, it describes TFP, not MBT.

3.6 Brief Psychodynamic PsychotherapiesLower yield · not asked yet

On an August afternoon in 1910, in the Dutch university town of Leiden, Freud spent about four hours walking the streets with the composer Gustav Mahler, who had cancelled his appointment three times before he finally kept it. Mahler was in anguish over his marriage. In that single conversation Freud helped him see connections between his feelings for his much younger wife, Alma, and his attachment to his own mother, and Mahler left, by most accounts, considerably relieved; he died the following year. Freud's early treatments were often short. Dora's lasted about eleven weeks, and many of the treatments in Studies on Hysteria took weeks or a few months. Over the following half century, however, analyses grew steadily longer, until four or five sessions a week for several years became the norm. Some of the reasons were good ones: analysts had learned how slowly character changes and how much working through takes. Others were less good: the analyst's perfectionism, the patient's reluctance to end, and a technique that encouraged both to follow the material wherever it led, for as long as it took. This section is about the analysts who decided that psychodynamic therapy could be both brief and deep, how they made it so, and how their six best-known methods differ.

The forerunners: Ferenczi, Rank and Freud's time limit

The first serious attempts to shorten analysis came from inside Freud's own circle. Sándor Ferenczi (1873 to 1933) of Budapest, the closest to Freud of all his followers and, as you saw in Section 3.4, Melanie Klein's first analyst, was troubled by analyses that went round in circles. From about 1919 he experimented with an active technique: instead of waiting passively, the analyst gave the patient instructions or prohibitions designed to raise tension and bring repressed material to the surface. A phobic patient might be told to enter the situation she feared; a patient who discharged tension through some habitual action might be asked to give it up for a time, so that the feelings behind it would appear in the session. Ferenczi later abandoned the most forceful versions and swung towards warmth and indulgence, and even, controversially, towards "mutual analysis", in which he let a patient analyse him in turn. But his insistence that the analyst may be active, and that emotional experience in the session matters more than intellectual reconstruction, lived on.

In 1924 Ferenczi and Otto Rank (1884 to 1939), the Viennese lay analyst who had long served as secretary of the Vienna Psychoanalytic Society, published The Development of Psychoanalysis, which argued that reliving the past in the here and now, rather than simply remembering it, is the decisive factor in cure. The same year Rank published The Trauma of Birth, which made the separation from the mother at birth the prototype of all later anxiety, and he drew a practical conclusion. Because every analysis repeats the struggle for separation, the analyst should set the date of ending in advance, so that the patient has to confront separation directly instead of postponing it for ever. Rank's "end-setting", together with his theory of birth trauma, cost him his place in Freud's circle. His ideas travelled on, however: through the "functional" school of social work developed by Jessie Taft and Virginia Robinson they reached Carl Rogers (Chapter 6), and the time-limited therapies of this section echo his concern with time and separation.

Freud himself had used a time limit once. In 1914, with the Russian patient he called the Wolf Man, whose analysis had settled into a comfortable, obliging inertia after several years, Freud fixed a date by which the treatment would end whether or not it was complete, and under that pressure the patient produced crucial material. In "Analysis Terminable and Interminable" (1937), however, Freud was cautious about the device and about Rank's attempt to build a method on it. A time limit, he wrote, works only if it is set at the right moment and is never withdrawn, and it cannot guarantee that the work will be finished; as the proverb he quoted puts it, a lion springs only once.

Alexander and French: the corrective emotional experience

The decisive break with the assumption that analysis must be long came from Chicago. Franz Alexander (1891 to 1964), the Hungarian-born first director of the Chicago Institute for Psychoanalysis (and the author of the psychosomatic specificity hypothesis you meet in Chapter 9), and his colleague Thomas Morton French (1892 to 1976) drew on the Institute's experience with brief and flexible treatments in Psychoanalytic Therapy: Principles and Application (1946). Their argument had two parts.

The first was a principle of flexibility. There is no reason, they said, why every patient should lie on a couch five times a week for years. The therapist should plan each treatment to fit the patient, varying the frequency of sessions, sitting face to face, and even interrupting treatment for a time to test whether the patient could manage alone and to stop dependence on the analyst becoming an end in itself.

The second was the idea they are remembered for. Insight alone, Alexander argued, rarely cures. What cures is a corrective emotional experience: the patient is re-exposed, under more favourable circumstances, to the kind of emotional situation he could not handle in the past, and this time it turns out differently. Because the therapist reacts not as the original parent did but in a new way, the old expectation is disconfirmed in lived experience and not merely in thought. A scene from Victor Hugo's Les Misérables, which Alexander himself used in the 1946 book to illustrate the idea, makes it vivid. The convict Jean Valjean, hardened by years of brutal treatment, steals the silver of a bishop who has given him shelter; when the police bring him back, the bishop tells them that the silver was a gift and hands him two candlesticks as well. The unexpected kindness, which fits none of Valjean's expectations, transforms him. In therapy Alexander went further and suggested that the therapist might deliberately adopt an attitude opposite to that of the patient's parent, being permissive, for example, with a patient whose father had been harsh and domineering.

That last suggestion caused an uproar. Orthodox analysts accused Alexander of replacing analysis with manipulation and role-playing: a therapist who plays a part is not being genuine, and the patient's improvement may be compliance with a performance rather than lasting change. The psychoanalytic establishment closed ranks, and in 1953 Kurt Eissler argued that any deviation from interpretation (any "parameter", in his term) must be kept to a minimum and must itself eventually be analysed. Yet the core idea has proved remarkably durable. Therapists of almost every school now accept that a relationship which disconfirms a patient's old expectations, without any deliberate role-play, is one of the main ways therapy works. You will meet the corrective emotional experience again in Strupp and Binder's therapy later in this section, in Kohut's account of how repaired empathic failures build the self (Section 3.5), and as one of Leslie Greenberg's principles of emotional change (Chapter 6).

Exam trap

The corrective emotional experience is Franz Alexander and Thomas French's (1946), not Ferenczi's, Rogers' or Greenberg's, although Greenberg's emotion-focused therapy later borrowed the phrase. Ferenczi's contribution is the active technique; Rank's is the fixed termination date ("end-setting") and the trauma of birth. The same Franz Alexander is examined in health psychology for psychosomatic medicine and the specificity hypothesis (Chapter 9), so do not be surprised to meet him in two different subjects.

What makes a psychodynamic therapy brief

The brief therapies that grew up from the 1950s onwards differ in style, sometimes sharply, but they share a set of features that distinguish all of them from open-ended analysis. It is worth learning these first, because examiners ask what the brief psychodynamic therapies have in common, and the six methods that follow are best understood as six ways of carrying out the same programme.

  • Selection. Not every patient is suitable. Brief therapists assess carefully, usually looking for motivation, a capacity to relate, enough ego strength to bear anxiety (Section 3.4) and a problem that can be focused.
  • A focus. Instead of following free associations wherever they lead, therapist and patient agree on a central problem or conflict, and the therapist keeps returning to it, deliberately leaving other material alone (sometimes called selective neglect).
  • A time limit. The number of sessions or the end date is usually fixed, or at least anticipated, from the start, typically somewhere between about 12 and 40 sessions, and the limit itself is used therapeutically.
  • An active therapist. Free association and the silent analyst give way to questions, clarifications, confrontations and early interpretations, usually face to face and once a week.
  • Early work with the transference. Transference, negative transference especially, is addressed from the first sessions rather than being allowed to grow into a transference neurosis.
  • Attention to ending. Termination, with its feelings of loss, anger and separation, is treated as a central part of the work rather than an afterthought.

Selection and focus were the issues the brief therapists argued about most, because they decide who can be helped and how much. The prevailing opinion in the 1950s, which Malan called the conservative view, held that brief therapy could help only mildly ill patients with recent problems, and only by superficial means such as reassurance and advice. The work described next overturned it.

Remember

One line per method. Malan: the two triangles. Sifneos: strict selection and anxiety-provoking confrontation. Davanloo: pressure, challenge and the head-on collision with resistance. Mann: exactly twelve sessions and the central issue. Luborsky: the core conflictual relationship theme (wish, response of other, response of self). Strupp and Binder: the cyclical maladaptive pattern. The first four are the classic short-term dynamic therapists; the last two wrote research manuals that made dynamic therapy teachable and testable.

Malan and the Tavistock: the two triangles

In the mid-1950s Michael Balint (Section 3.4) gathered a small workshop of analysts at the Tavistock Clinic in London to find out what could be achieved in a limited number of sessions. Its most important member was the psychiatrist and analyst David Malan (1922 to 2020), who spent the rest of his career turning the workshop's experience into research. In A Study of Brief Psychotherapy (1963) and The Frontier of Brief Psychotherapy (1976) he described the Tavistock's focal psychotherapy: a treatment usually lasting from about ten to forty sessions, often with an end date set in advance, in which the therapist formulates a focus, usually a single central conflict, and pursues it actively through interpretation, especially interpretation of the transference.

Malan's research was unusually careful for its time. Before treatment began, the team wrote down a psychodynamic hypothesis about each patient and specified in advance what changes would count as a genuine resolution, not merely relief of symptoms. Patients were followed up for years and judged against those criteria. The results contradicted the conservative view: some patients with long-standing and fairly severe problems made deep and lasting changes in a few months. The best outcomes went with the patient's strong motivation for insight, with the therapist's enthusiasm, and with the therapist's readiness to interpret the transference early and to link it to the patient's parents, a connection Malan called the T/P link. Patients who did well had also faced and worked through their feelings about the ending.

Malan's most famous contribution is a pair of diagrams that compress psychodynamic theory into two triangles (Figure 3.6). The second of them grew from the "triangle of insight" that the American psychiatrist Karl Menninger (1893 to 1990) had described in Theory of Psychoanalytic Technique (1958).

The triangle of conflict describes what happens inside the patient. At its lowest corner lies the hidden feeling (Malan wrote I/F, for impulse or feeling): a true feeling or impulse, often anger, grief, longing or sexual desire, that the patient cannot allow himself to experience. Above it lie anxiety (A), which the feeling arouses whenever it comes near awareness, and defence (D), whatever the patient does to avoid the feeling and the anxiety: placating, intellectualising, joking, changing the subject, developing a symptom. The triangle stands on its point because the hidden feeling lies beneath the other two. Defence and anxiety are what the therapist meets first; the hidden feeling is what the therapist is trying to reach.

The triangle of person describes where the conflict is played out. Its corners are other people in the patient's current or recent life (O), the therapist in the transference (T), and the past, usually the parents (P), placed at the bottom because the past lies beneath the present. The same triangle of conflict appears at all three corners: the man who placates his boss (O) to avoid his anger also placates the therapist (T) and long ago placated his father (P). Malan stated what he called the universal principle of psychodynamic psychotherapy in these terms: the aim is to reach, beneath the defence and the anxiety, the hidden feeling, and then to trace it from present relationships (O and T) back to its origins in the past (P). An interpretation that joins all three corners of the triangle of person is the complete form.

A worked example shows the triangles in use. Mr Suresh, a 34-year-old bank officer in Mysuru, comes for help with headaches and a sense of being stuck. He has been passed over for promotion twice and says, smiling, that it does not matter, because his manager is under a great deal of pressure. The smile and the excuse are defence (D). When the therapist gently points out that he is smiling as he describes being passed over, Suresh becomes restless and changes the subject: anxiety (A) at the edge of a feeling. Over several sessions the hidden feeling (F) emerges: fury at being treated as a doormat. Then the triangle of person comes into play. When the therapist has to cancel a session, Suresh says it is "absolutely no problem" in exactly the voice he uses about his manager (T and O), and as they explore this he remembers how, as the eldest son in a tense household, he learned never to show anger to a father whose temper frightened the whole family (P). The interpretation that finally lands joins the corners: "With your manager, with me and long ago with your father, you have swallowed your anger and smiled, because anger felt as if it would bring disaster. Perhaps the headaches are where the anger has been going."

Malan's selection procedure was a dynamic assessment rather than a checklist. In the assessment interview the therapist offers one or two trial interpretations and watches how the patient responds. A patient who can work with an interpretation (becoming thoughtful, producing new material, feeling something) and who can agree on a focus is suitable; one who becomes confused, defensive or disorganised is not. Malan also listed grounds for exclusion: serious suicide attempts, drug addiction or chronic alcoholism, long-term hospitalisation, more than one course of electroconvulsive therapy, incapacitating chronic obsessional or phobic symptoms, and gross destructive or self-destructive acting out. Such patients were thought to need more support, or more time, than a brief interpretive therapy can give.

Exam trap

The two triangles are David Malan's, from the Tavistock Clinic. The triangle of conflict is defence, anxiety and hidden feeling (D, A, and F or I/F); the triangle of person is other, therapist (transference) and past or parents (O, T, P). Do not swap their corners: "therapist" is never part of the triangle of conflict, and "anxiety" is never part of the triangle of person. The triangle of person grew from Karl Menninger's triangle of insight (1958), and Malan's T/P link is the interpretation that connects the transference to the parents. Davanloo later used the same triangles, so "triangles" alone does not rule him out; "Tavistock", "focal therapy" and "Balint" point to Malan.

Sifneos: short-term anxiety-provoking psychotherapy

While Malan worked in London, Peter Sifneos (1920 to 2008), a Greek-born psychiatrist at the Massachusetts General Hospital in Boston, was developing a brief therapy of his own, described in Short-Term Psychotherapy and Emotional Crisis (1972) and Short-Term Dynamic Psychotherapy: Evaluation and Technique (1979). He called it short-term anxiety-provoking psychotherapy (STAPP), and the name is a manifesto. Supportive therapy aims to lower anxiety. Sifneos aimed to raise it, deliberately and in a controlled way, because the anxiety that rises as a defended conflict is approached is exactly what pushes the patient to face it. He contrasted STAPP with anxiety-suppressive psychotherapy, the supportive treatment he recommended for patients too fragile to bear confrontation (supportive therapy is the subject of Section 3.10).

Sifneos is best known for the strictness of his selection criteria, among the most examined lists in all of brief therapy. A patient was suitable for STAPP if he showed:

  1. a circumscribed chief complaint, that is, the ability to pick out one problem from among his difficulties and give it priority;
  2. at least one meaningful, give-and-take relationship in early childhood, evidence that he could relate to another person and so form an alliance;
  3. the ability to interact flexibly with the evaluator during the interview and to express feelings, positive and negative, freely;
  4. above-average psychological sophistication and intelligence, enough to grasp and use psychological explanations;
  5. motivation for change, not merely for relief of symptoms.

Motivation carried so much weight that Sifneos gave it seven criteria of its own. The patient should recognise that the symptoms are psychological; be introspective and honest about himself; be willing to participate actively in treatment; be curious about himself and want to understand; be willing to change, explore and experiment; have realistic expectations of the results; and be willing to make reasonable sacrifices of time and money. Patients who met all of these were, by any standard, among the healthiest in the clinic, and critics complained that STAPP succeeded partly because it selected people who would have done well in almost any therapy.

The preferred focus in STAPP was an unresolved Oedipal conflict, a triangular rivalry or love relationship (a young woman who repeatedly falls for married men, a man who competes with and fears every senior colleague), although Sifneos also accepted foci of loss and separation. Problems of early dependence and of character were avoided as too deep for brief work. The therapist works face to face, weekly, for a typical course of about 12 to 20 sessions, more like a challenging teacher than a neutral listener. He uses the positive transference from the start to build the alliance, asks anxiety-provoking questions and confronts the patient's defences directly ("You say you don't care about him, yet you have mentioned him four times in ten minutes"), makes early links between the transference and the past, and steers firmly away from material outside the agreed focus. There is no fixed end date. Therapy ends when there is tangible evidence, in the patient's behaviour outside the sessions, that the focal conflict has been resolved.

Sifneos is remembered for one more idea, which reaches far beyond brief therapy. Studying psychosomatic patients in the early 1970s, he noticed how many of them could not identify or describe their feelings and spoke instead of bodily sensations and external events. He coined the term alexithymia (Greek for "no words for feelings") for this difficulty. Alexithymic patients, by his own criteria, are poor candidates for STAPP.

Exam trap

STAPP is Peter Sifneos's short-term anxiety-provoking psychotherapy, and its opposite is anxiety-suppressive (supportive) psychotherapy. Sifneos is the answer to questions about strict selection criteria for brief therapy: a circumscribed complaint, one meaningful childhood relationship, flexible interaction with expression of feeling, above-average psychological sophistication, and motivation for change rather than symptom relief. His preferred focus is Oedipal, and he coined "alexithymia". Do not confuse STAPP with ISTDP (Davanloo) or with STDP, a general label for any short-term dynamic psychotherapy.

Davanloo: intensive short-term dynamic psychotherapy

If Sifneos raised anxiety, Habib Davanloo raised it further. An Iranian-born psychiatrist at the Montreal General Hospital and McGill University, Davanloo developed intensive short-term dynamic psychotherapy (ISTDP) from the 1960s onwards, and he did something no analyst before him had done systematically: he videotaped his sessions and showed the tapes at workshops, so that colleagues could see exactly what he did and what followed. Malan, who watched the tapes, became one of his warmest advocates. Davanloo set out the method in Basic Principles and Techniques in Short-Term Dynamic Psychotherapy (1978) and Unlocking the Unconscious (1990).

Davanloo's premise is that the patient's resistance, the system of defences that keeps feelings out of awareness, is the real obstacle to change, and that it can be overcome quickly if it is confronted relentlessly and repeatedly. The core of the method is a recurring series of steps that Davanloo called the central dynamic sequence.

  1. Inquiry: the therapist explores the patient's problems, insisting on specific examples rather than generalities.
  2. Pressure: the therapist presses the patient, again and again, towards the actual experience of his feelings in specific situations ("How did you feel towards him at that moment? Not what you thought; what you felt").
  3. Challenge: as the patient responds with defences (vagueness, intellectualising, passivity, a smile), the therapist names and challenges each one, inviting the patient to turn against it ("Do you notice how you become vague? Do you want to keep doing that to yourself?").
  4. Transference resistance: the resistance crystallises in the relationship with the therapist, as the patient becomes detached, defiant or compliant towards him, and the therapist meets it with the head-on collision (below).
  5. Direct access to the unconscious: when the resistance gives way there is a breakthrough, often an intense, physically felt surge of the hidden feeling (commonly rage towards a figure from the past, followed by guilt and grief), which Davanloo called "unlocking the unconscious".
  6. Systematic analysis of the transference: therapist and patient look together at what has just happened between them.
  7. Dynamic exploration of the unconscious: the experience is linked, using Malan's two triangles, to current relationships, the transference and the past.

The head-on collision deserves a word, because it is Davanloo's signature and the most examined phrase in ISTDP. It is an attack not on the patient but on the resistance, and it is delivered as an alliance. A head-on collision might sound like this: "You and I can both see that this wall of detachment is here with me, as it has been with everyone. It has already cost you your marriage. If we let it, it will defeat our work too. Is that what you want?" The intensity is meant to mobilise the patient's own wish to be free of the defences that are defeating him. Davanloo also began treatment with a trial therapy, an initial interview that could run for several hours and served at once as assessment and as a first dose of treatment: the therapist applied the central dynamic sequence and judged the patient's suitability by how he responded.

Because the method was designed to break through resistance rather than avoid it, Davanloo accepted a much wider range of patients than Malan or Sifneos, including people with long-standing character problems and very rigid defences, whom he arranged on a spectrum from highly responsive to highly resistant. Treatment ranged from a single extended session to about forty sessions, with the more resistant patients needing more. Critics found the technique aggressive and worried that it could overwhelm fragile patients; supporters replied that the pressure is always graded to the patient's capacity to tolerate anxiety. Later researchers, notably Allan Abbass in Canada, assembled trials and meta-analyses suggesting that ISTDP is effective for a range of problems, including somatic symptoms and personality disorders. A gentler, attachment-informed descendant is Diana Fosha's accelerated experiential dynamic psychotherapy (AEDP; The Transforming Power of Affect, 2000), which keeps Davanloo's focus on deep feeling but replaces challenge with emotional safety and the therapist's active warmth.

Exam trap
