Pressure, challenge, the head-on collision with the resistance, trial therapy, the central dynamic sequence and "unlocking the unconscious" are all Habib Davanloo's (ISTDP, Montreal). Do not confuse Davanloo's "pressure" (pressing the patient towards the experience of feeling) with Freud's pressure technique of the 1890s (a hand on the forehead to bring back memories, Section 3.1). "Anxiety-provoking" is Sifneos's term, not Davanloo's, although both confront. Of the classic brief therapists, Davanloo accepts the widest range of patients and Sifneos is the most selective.

Mann: time-limited psychotherapy

The Boston psychiatrist and analyst James Mann (born 1913), of Boston University School of Medicine, built his therapy not around a conflict or a technique but around time. In Time-Limited Psychotherapy (1973) he proposed exactly twelve sessions, no more and no fewer, with the date of the last session fixed and stated at the start. The precise number matters less than its fixity. The patient knows from the first meeting that the relationship will end on a particular day, and that knowledge, Mann argued, is itself the most powerful therapeutic agent in the room.

Why should time be so powerful? Mann distinguished two kinds of time. Child time is the timeless, infinite time of the unconscious and of early childhood, in which wishes never expire and a loving relationship lasts for ever. Adult time, calendar time, is finite and ends in death. Open-ended therapy colludes with child time, quietly promising the patient that the longed-for relationship will never end. A fixed ending confronts the patient with adult time and therefore with the central drama of human development, the struggle with separation and loss, which Mann linked to Mahler's separation-individuation (Section 3.4). Every patient, he believed, brings some version of four universal conflicts: independence versus dependence, activity versus passivity, adequate versus diminished self-esteem, and unresolved or delayed grief.

Mann's focus is the central issue: a short statement, formulated by the therapist after the assessment and offered to the patient in the first sessions, of the patient's present and chronically endured pain, put in terms of how the patient feels about himself. It is not an interpretation of an unconscious conflict but an empathic statement of a lifelong hurt, chosen to be something the patient will recognise at once. Imagine a 45-year-old schoolteacher in Chennai who has nursed her mother, helped raise her brothers' children and run a household in which her own wishes always waited. Her central issue might be put like this: "You have always given a great deal to others and tried hard to do what was expected of you, and yet you have always felt that what you needed came last and was never really noticed." Because it speaks to the self-image rather than to forbidden wishes, the central issue usually produces a quick, strong alliance.

The twelve sessions then pass through three predictable phases. In the opening phase, roughly the first three or four sessions, the patient often improves quickly: feeling understood at last, he idealises the therapist in a honeymoon of positive transference and feels that his old wishes may finally be met. In the middle phase, around the sixth or seventh session, disappointment sets in. The patient realises that twelve sessions will not undo a lifetime, symptoms may return, and ambivalence towards the therapist appears, often as veiled anger. In the final phase, the last few sessions, the work centres on termination: the patient's feelings about losing the therapist are interpreted as a repetition of earlier losses and linked to the central issue. The aim is that this time the patient experiences a separation that is fully felt, sad and angry, and survived, so that he can take the therapist away with him as a good internal figure rather than as yet another person who left. In Mann's therapy the termination is not the end of the treatment but its heart.

Mann accepted a fairly broad range of patients but excluded those who could not engage and disengage quickly, such as patients with psychotic or severe borderline disorders and those whose dependence was too extreme to survive a fixed ending.

Exam trap

"Twelve sessions with a fixed termination date" is James Mann's time-limited psychotherapy (1973), and its focus is the central issue. Do not confuse Mann's time-limited psychotherapy with Strupp and Binder's time-limited dynamic psychotherapy (TLDP, about 25 sessions, below): the names differ by one word. Interpersonal psychotherapy usually runs 12 to 16 sessions (Section 3.7), and Sifneos's STAPP has no fixed ending at all. The idea of setting the end date in advance goes back to Otto Rank; Mann made it the centre of a method.

Luborsky: supportive-expressive therapy and the core conflictual relationship theme

The last two methods in this section come from researchers, and they changed brief dynamic therapy in a different way: by describing it precisely enough to be taught, measured and tested. Lester Luborsky (1920 to 2009) spent his early career at the Menninger Foundation in Kansas, the home of a long-term research project that compared supportive and expressive psychotherapies, and later moved to the University of Pennsylvania. His Principles of Psychoanalytic Psychotherapy: A Manual for Supportive-Expressive Treatment (1984) was one of the first treatment manuals for any psychodynamic therapy.

Supportive-expressive therapy (SE) has, as its name says, two components, used in proportions that vary with the patient. The supportive component builds and protects the helping alliance: the therapist conveys understanding, acceptance and realistic hope, so that the patient experiences the therapist as helpful and the work as shared. Luborsky's research had shown that this experience, which he measured with his helping alliance scales, is among the best predictors of outcome. The expressive component helps the patient understand his central relationship problem through listening, understanding and interpretation, and its target is a pattern Luborsky devised a way to measure. SE can be open-ended, but time-limited versions of about sixteen sessions are common. It was among the dynamic therapies tested in addiction research, including a 1983 trial by George Woody, Luborsky and colleagues with opiate addicts on methadone, in which patients who received SE or cognitive therapy in addition to drug counselling did better than those who received drug counselling alone.

The pattern is the core conflictual relationship theme (CCRT), which Luborsky introduced in 1977. It is extracted from the stories patients tell about their interactions with other people ("Yesterday my sister-in-law...", "When I went to see my guide..."), each of which Luborsky called a relationship episode. Every episode is broken down into three components:

  • the wish (W), what the patient wants from the other person: to be loved, respected, listened to, left free;
  • the response of other (RO), how the other person responds or is expected to respond: rejects, controls, ignores, criticises;
  • the response of self (RS), how the patient then reacts in feelings, actions and symptoms: withdraws, becomes depressed, gives in, explodes.

The components that recur most often across ten or so relationship episodes make up the patient's CCRT. Suppose Anjali, a doctoral student in Hyderabad, tells of her mother-in-law dismissing her career, her guide ignoring her ideas in a lab meeting, and a friend talking over her at dinner. In each story she wants to be taken seriously (W), the other brushes her aside (RO), and she falls silent, feels small and later gets a headache (RS). That is her CCRT: "I want to be taken seriously; others dismiss me; I go quiet and feel worthless." The expressive work consists of helping Anjali see this theme in episode after episode, including, sooner or later, in her relationship with the therapist, and of understanding where it came from, perhaps in a family in which her elder brother's opinions always counted and hers never did. As she recognises the theme she begins to test whether others really respond as she expects, and her responses of self are usually the first thing to change.

The CCRT gave psychoanalysis something it had lacked: a reliable, quantifiable measure of transference. Luborsky and Paul Crits-Christoph reported in Understanding Transference (1990) that a patient's CCRT is consistent across the different people in his stories, appears in his relationship with the therapist, is fairly stable over time and can be found even in dreams, which they took as evidence for the transference template Freud had described. Their research also found that interpretations that accurately addressed the patient's CCRT, especially the wish and the response of other, predicted better outcomes. Later clinicians, notably Howard Book (1998), built a sixteen-session brief therapy around the CCRT in three phases: identifying the theme (about sessions 1 to 4), working through its roots, especially the expected response of others (about sessions 5 to 12), and termination (about sessions 13 to 16).

Exam trap

The core conflictual relationship theme is Lester Luborsky's, and its three components are the wish, the response of other and the response of self (W, RO, RS), drawn from relationship episodes. Do not confuse it with Malan's triangle of conflict (defence, anxiety, hidden feeling) or with Strupp and Binder's cyclical maladaptive pattern (four components, below). Supportive-expressive therapy is Luborsky's too; it is not the same as supportive psychotherapy as a separate treatment (Section 3.10). Luborsky's 1975 review with Barton Singer and Lise Luborsky also popularised the "Dodo bird verdict" that different therapies produce similar outcomes (Chapter 8).

Strupp and Binder: time-limited dynamic psychotherapy

At Vanderbilt University in Nashville, Hans Strupp (1921 to 2006), a German-born psychologist who had spent decades studying what therapists actually do in sessions, arrived at his brief therapy through a research result that surprised everyone, himself included.

Key study: Strupp and Hadley's professors (1979)

Do trained therapists help because of their special techniques, or because they offer a good human relationship? Hans Strupp and Suzanne Hadley tested this at Vanderbilt with male college students troubled by anxiety, depression and social withdrawal. One group was treated by experienced professional psychotherapists. Another was treated by college professors from subjects such as English, history and philosophy, chosen because they were known for their warmth and were the kind of people students turned to, but who had no training in therapy. Both offered up to about 25 hours of twice-weekly sessions, and a further group waited. On average, the students treated by the professors improved about as much as those treated by the professionals. Within both groups, however, there were large differences, and the closer analysis of the recordings was the real lesson. Patients who could quickly form a good working relationship did well with either kind of helper, while hostile, mistrustful or withdrawn patients did badly with both, because even experienced professionals were drawn into negative, often subtly hostile exchanges with them and failed to recognise or repair what was happening. The criticism: the samples were small and the patients only moderately disturbed. The study is a classic of the common-factors debate (Chapter 8); for Strupp it was the starting point of a therapy designed to manage exactly those negative interpersonal cycles.

With Jeffrey Binder, Strupp set out the result in Psychotherapy in a New Key (1984), a guide to time-limited dynamic psychotherapy (TLDP), which typically runs about 25 sessions. TLDP is psychodynamic in its attention to the unconscious, to transference and countertransference and to repetition, but its language is interpersonal rather than drive-based. The patient's difficulties are understood as lifelong patterns of relating, learned in early relationships and kept going in the present because they draw from other people the very responses the patient fears. The therapist is not outside this. Sooner or later, Strupp and Binder argued, every therapist is pulled into the patient's pattern and starts to play the part the patient expects, becoming critical with the patient who expects criticism or distant with the one who expects to be abandoned. This is inevitable, and the art lies in noticing it, stepping out of the role and helping the patient explore what has happened between them. When the therapist then responds differently from everyone else in the patient's life, the patient has a new experience of a relationship: Alexander's corrective emotional experience, arrived at without any role-play.

The focus of TLDP is the cyclical maladaptive pattern (CMP), a working model of the patient's central interpersonal pattern, built up in four categories.

  1. Acts of the self: the patient's thoughts, feelings, wishes and behaviour towards others ("I want to be close to people, but I hold back and say little").
  2. Expectations of others' reactions: what the patient imagines others will do in response ("If I show what I feel, people will find me boring and turn away").
  3. Acts of others towards the self: how others actually behave, as the patient perceives it ("People do lose interest in me and turn to someone else").
  4. Acts of the self towards the self, or the introject: how the patient treats himself, often in the way he was once treated ("I tell myself I am dull and deserve to be ignored").

The pattern is cyclical because each element feeds the next. Holding back while expecting rejection provokes the very loss of interest that confirms the expectation and the self-criticism, and these lead to more holding back. Hanna Levenson, who developed the model further in Time-Limited Dynamic Psychotherapy: A Guide to Clinical Practice (1995), summarised the two goals of TLDP as a new experience and a new understanding of the pattern, and later added the therapist's own reactions to the patient as a further element of the formulation. Table 3.10 sets the CMP beside Luborsky's CCRT, because the two are easily confused.

Table 3.10 Two research formulations of a patient's central relationship pattern.
What is describedLuborsky's CCRTStrupp and Binder's CMP
What the patient wants or doesWish (W)Acts of the self
What the patient expects of othersFolded into the response of otherExpectations of others' reactions
What others doResponse of other (RO), actual or expectedActs of others towards the self
The patient's own reactionResponse of self (RS): feelings, behaviour, symptomsActs of the self towards the self (the introject)
Number of componentsThreeFour

Strupp's group studied these patterns with a coding system created by the Utah psychologist Lorna Smith Benjamin, the Structural Analysis of Social Behavior (SASB, 1974). SASB classifies every interpersonal message by its focus (on the other person, on the self, or on oneself as an introject), by its warmth (from loving to attacking) and by its degree of control (from controlling to granting autonomy). Coding taped sessions with SASB, William Henry, Thomas Schacht and Strupp (1986) found that in cases with poor outcomes therapists sent more subtly hostile, controlling or mixed messages than in the same therapists' cases with good outcomes. A later study of therapists being trained in TLDP (Henry and colleagues, 1993) found that training raised their adherence to the manual, but some aspects of their interpersonal manner unexpectedly got worse, with more subtly negative exchanges on SASB coding: technique is no substitute for the quality of the relationship. Benjamin went on to develop her own interpersonal reconstructive therapy (IRT) for patients with severe personality disorders who had not responded to other treatments.

Exam trap

The cyclical maladaptive pattern is Strupp and Binder's (TLDP, Vanderbilt, 1984), and it has four parts: acts of the self, expectations of others' reactions, acts of others towards the self, and acts of the self towards the self (the introject). The CCRT, with three parts, is Luborsky's. The professors study (1979) is Strupp and Hadley's. "L. Benjamin" in an option list is Lorna Smith Benjamin, the creator of SASB and of interpersonal reconstructive therapy, not a developer of interpersonal psychotherapy, which is Klerman and Weissman's (Section 3.7).

The six methods compared

Table 3.11 sets the six methods side by side, and read down its rows it tells a single story. The early methods (Malan, Sifneos) selected healthy, motivated patients and focused on one conflict, often an Oedipal one. Davanloo widened the range of patients by attacking resistance head on. Mann replaced the conflict with time and separation as the organising theme. The research methods of Luborsky and of Strupp and Binder translated the focus into measurable relationship patterns, and the technique into manuals that could be taught and tested.

Table 3.11 The six brief psychodynamic psychotherapies compared.
Method (founder)Base and key datesLengthFocusSignature technique; selection
Focal therapy (David Malan)Tavistock Clinic, London; 1963, 1976About 10 to 40; end date often setOne central conflictTwo triangles; T/P link; trial interpretation
STAPP (Peter Sifneos)Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston; 1972, 1979About 12 to 20; no fixed endOedipal conflict, or lossAnxiety-provoking confrontation; strictest selection
ISTDP (Habib Davanloo)Montreal General Hospital; 1978, 1990One long session to about 40Feelings hidden by resistancePressure, challenge, head-on collision; widest selection
Time-limited psychotherapy (James Mann)Boston University; 1973Exactly 12; end date fixedThe central issueTime and separation; termination as the heart
Supportive-expressive therapy (Lester Luborsky)University of Pennsylvania; 1977, 1984Open-ended, or about 16CCRT: W, RO, RSHelping alliance plus interpretation of the CCRT
TLDP (Hans Strupp and Jeffrey Binder)Vanderbilt University; 1984About 25Cyclical maladaptive patternNotice and step out of the pattern in the room

Do these therapies work? That question belongs to Section 3.11, which weighs the evidence for psychodynamic therapy as a whole, and to Chapter 8. Here it is enough to know that meta-analyses of short-term psychodynamic psychotherapy since the 1990s have found it clearly better than no treatment and broadly comparable to other active therapies for depression, anxiety and some personality disorders, with the usual caveats about small and uneven trials. The tradition has also gone on producing new brief methods. In Britain, Alessandra Lemma, Mary Target and Peter Fonagy developed dynamic interpersonal therapy (DIT, 2011), a sixteen-session treatment for depression that formulates an interpersonal and emotional focus much like a CCRT and borrows techniques from mentalisation-based treatment. Anthony Ryle's cognitive analytic therapy, a brief integration of object relations and cognitive ideas, is described in Section 5.7, and Klerman and Weissman's interpersonal psychotherapy, a brief therapy with psychodynamic roots that does not interpret the transference, follows in Section 3.7.

Clinical link

In a busy government hospital outpatient department, where a clinical psychologist may have a few months and a long waiting list, the brief dynamic methods offer a disciplined alternative both to open-ended exploration and to purely symptomatic work. The assessment questions they teach are useful whatever approach is finally chosen. Can this patient name one main problem? Has she had at least one good relationship? How does she respond to a trial interpretation, with curiosity or with confusion? Can she tolerate anxiety without acting on it? Can the two of you agree on a focus, and on an ending? A patient who passes these tests may do well with twelve to twenty-five sessions of focused psychodynamic work; one who does not may need supportive therapy (Section 3.10), a longer treatment, or a structured programme such as DBT or MBT.

Exam trap

Match the phrase to the founder. Triangle of conflict and triangle of person: Malan. STAPP, selection criteria, alexithymia: Sifneos. ISTDP, pressure, challenge, head-on collision, unlocking the unconscious: Davanloo. Twelve sessions, central issue, child time and adult time: Mann. Supportive-expressive therapy and the CCRT (wish, response of other, response of self): Luborsky. TLDP and the cyclical maladaptive pattern: Strupp and Binder. Corrective emotional experience: Alexander and French. Active technique: Ferenczi. Fixed end date and birth trauma: Rank. And brief psychodynamic therapy is neither solution-focused brief therapy (de Shazer and Berg, Chapter 6) nor the problem-focused brief therapy of the Mental Research Institute (Chapter 7).

  6. Mentalisation-based treatment was developed by Anthony Bateman and .

Answers: (1) twinship, (2) transmuting internalisation, (3) two, (4) avoidant, (5) teleological, (6) Peter Fonagy, (7) 1946, (8) past (the parents), (9) twelve, (10) self.

3.7 Interpersonal PsychotherapyHigh yield · 7 Qs

Every therapy you have met so far in this chapter was born in a consulting room. A clinician listened to patients for years, formed a theory and wrote it up as a set of cases. Interpersonal psychotherapy was born the other way round, inside a research protocol. Its founders wanted a psychotherapy that could be tested as strictly as a drug: one named disorder, a written manual, a fixed number of sessions, and therapists who could be trained and checked against the manual. What they built became, with cognitive-behaviour therapy (Chapter 5), one of the two most thoroughly tested psychotherapies for depression in the world. It sits in this chapter because of its ancestry, which runs back through Harry Stack Sullivan to the psychodynamic tradition. Yet you will see that it drops almost every technique you have learned so far: no couch, no free association, no dream analysis, no interpretation of the transference and no digging in childhood.

A therapy built to be tested

Gerald Klerman (1928 to 1992) was an American psychiatrist with an unusual conviction for his time. If a new antidepressant had to prove itself in controlled trials before doctors prescribed it, he argued, so did a psychotherapy; good intentions and persuasive case histories were not evidence. In the late 1960s Klerman and his colleagues in New Haven and Boston set out to answer a practical question. Women who had recovered from an episode of depression on the antidepressant amitriptyline often relapsed. Would continuing the drug prevent relapse, and would weekly psychotherapy add anything? To test the psychotherapy they first had to say exactly what it was, so they wrote down what their therapists actually did: they talked with the patient about her current relationships, her quarrels, her losses and her daily social life, and they helped her handle them better. The results, published in 1974, were tidy. The drug prevented relapse; the psychotherapy did little for relapse but improved how the women got on with their husbands, children and work; and the two effects added together rather than cancelling each other out.

That psychotherapy was refined, named and tested again in acute depression. In a 16-week trial published in 1979, the new interpersonal therapy and amitriptyline each did better than a control condition, and the combination did best of all. In 1984 Klerman, Myrna Weissman (born 1935), an epidemiologist who worked with him for many years and later became his wife, and their colleagues Bruce Rounsaville and Eve Chevron published the treatment manual, Interpersonal Psychotherapy of Depression. Interpersonal psychotherapy (IPT) can be defined as a time-limited, manualised psychotherapy, originally for major depression, which links the onset and course of the patient's symptoms to a current problem in his relationships and works on that problem. After his death Weissman and John Markowitz carried the work on, and their Comprehensive Guide to Interpersonal Psychotherapy (2000, with Klerman's name on it) is now the standard text.

Exam trap

Interpersonal psychotherapy was developed by Gerald L. Klerman and Myrna M. Weissman and colleagues; it took shape in the 1970s and its manual appeared in 1984. It is not L. Benjamin's (Lorna Smith Benjamin developed interpersonal reconstructive therapy, Table 3.15), not Peter Fonagy and Anthony Bateman's (mentalisation-based treatment, Section 3.5) and not Otto Kernberg's (transference-focused psychotherapy, Section 3.4). Nor is it Harry Stack Sullivan's "interpersonal psychoanalysis" (Section 3.8), which is its grandparent, not the same therapy.

The model: depression in an interpersonal context

Klerman drew on three thinkers who had each, in different ways, stepped outside Freudian orthodoxy. The Swiss-born American psychiatrist Adolf Meyer (1866 to 1950), who dominated American psychiatry from Johns Hopkins in the first half of the twentieth century, taught that mental disorders are best understood as a person's attempts, sometimes failed, to adapt to his environment, shaped by his whole life history. Harry Stack Sullivan, whose work you will meet in Section 3.8, had declared that psychiatry is the study of interpersonal relations, and that a person cannot be understood apart from the people around him. And John Bowlby (1907 to 1990), whose attachment theory Section 3.5 teaches, had shown how deeply human beings depend on close bonds and how their loss can lead to despair. Klerman added a fourth source that none of the others had used: research. Studies of life events, including work by Eugene Paykel in the New Haven group, found that depressions are often preceded by stressful events, especially "exits" from a person's social world such as a death, a separation or a child leaving home. In London, George Brown and Tirril Harris showed in Social Origins of Depression (1978) that women without a close, confiding relationship were far more likely to become depressed when a severe life event struck. Marital disputes, too, were tied to the onset and the course of depression.

From this Klerman built a deliberately modest model. Depression, the 1984 manual said, has three components: the symptoms themselves (low mood, poor sleep, guilt and so on), the patient's social and interpersonal relations, and his more enduring personality. IPT works on the first two and makes no attempt to reshape the third, which a 12 to 16 week therapy could not do anyway. Nor does IPT claim that relationships cause depression. Depression has many causes, genetic and biological among them. What IPT uses is the observable fact that the episode began, or worsened, in the middle of some interpersonal trouble, and that the link runs both ways: a quarrel or a loss can tip a vulnerable person into depression, and depression, with its irritability, withdrawal and hopelessness, damages the person's relationships in turn. Improve the relationships, and the symptoms usually lift; lift the symptoms, and the relationships become easier to improve.

Two features follow, and both surprise students who come to IPT from psychoanalysis. First, IPT adopts a frankly medical model. The therapist diagnoses depression, often measures it with a rating scale such as the Hamilton Rating Scale for Depression (Assessment book), names it to the patient and explains it as a common, treatable illness. Second, the therapist gives the patient the sick role. The term comes from the American sociologist Talcott Parsons (1902 to 1979), who described in The Social System (1951) the social role that a sick person is allowed to occupy. The sick role exempts the person from some normal obligations and from blame for being ill, but it carries obligations of its own: the sick person must want to get well and must seek and cooperate with competent help. In IPT the therapist tells the patient, in effect, "You are not lazy or weak; you have depression, and it is not your fault. It is reasonable for you to do less for the next few weeks while we treat it, and your job is to work with me on getting better." For a patient drowning in guilt, the relief is immediate. The role is temporary, and as the patient recovers it is gradually withdrawn.

Exam trap

IPT's theoretical roots are Adolf Meyer (psychobiology, adaptation to the environment), Harry Stack Sullivan (interpersonal psychiatry) and John Bowlby (attachment and loss), plus research on life events and social support. It is not rooted in Freud's drive theory, Beck's cognitive theory or Skinner's operant conditioning. The "sick role" is Talcott Parsons's sociological concept (1951); in IPT it is granted temporarily to relieve self-blame, and it comes with the duty to work at recovery. Do not read it as encouraging dependence. And IPT focuses on current interpersonal problems, not childhood unconscious conflicts.

The initial phase: diagnosis, the sick role and the interpersonal inventory

An acute course of IPT lasts 12 to 16 weekly sessions and moves through three phases, shown in Figure 3.7. The initial phase usually takes the first one to three sessions and has a long list of tasks. The therapist reviews the symptoms, gives the syndrome its name, explains depression and its treatment, gives the patient the sick role and decides whether medication is also needed (IPT was designed to be used alone or with antidepressants). Then comes the centrepiece of the opening phase.

The interpersonal inventory is a careful review, person by person, of the patient's important current and past relationships. For each important person (spouse, parents, children, in-laws, friends, boss, colleagues) the therapist asks how often they meet and what they do together, what each expects of the other and whether those expectations are being met, what is satisfying and what is unsatisfying in the relationship, and what the patient would like to change. Alongside the inventory, the therapist builds a timeline: what was happening in the patient's relationships in the weeks before the depression began? The inventory is not idle history-taking. It is a search for the interpersonal problem most closely tied to this episode.

With the inventory complete, therapist and patient choose a focus: one of four problem areas (next subsection), or occasionally two, but rarely more. The therapist then offers the interpersonal formulation, a short statement that links the diagnosis to the problem area and proposes what the two of them will work on: "You have major depression, a medical illness that is not your fault. It began when you retired and lost your work, your colleagues and your sense of being useful, and it seems tied to the difficulty of that change. Let's spend the next fourteen weeks helping you handle this transition; as you do, I expect the depression to lift." The patient is asked whether this makes sense, and the formulation is revised until he agrees. Finally they make a treatment contract: the number and length of sessions, the focus on the here and now, the patient's part in bringing important concerns to each session, and practical matters such as fees and missed appointments.

The four problem areas

Klerman's review of the research suggested that the interpersonal troubles surrounding depression fall into four broad groups. These four problem areas are the most examined fact about IPT, and each has its own goals and strategies (Table 3.12 and Figure 3.8).

Grief. Grief is a normal response to a death, not an illness, and IPT does not treat ordinary mourning. The problem area applies when the death of someone important is followed by a depressive episode, especially when the mourning has gone wrong: when it is delayed (the person seemed untouched at the time and collapses months later), distorted (the grief appears as physical symptoms or unexplained anger) or chronic (it never lessens). The older IPT manuals call this abnormal or complicated grief. The goals are to help the mourning process along and then to help the patient re-establish interests and relationships that can partly fill what was lost. The therapist relates the onset of symptoms to the death, asks the patient to describe the events just before, during and after it, reconstructs the relationship with the dead person in detail, and encourages the patient to express the full range of feelings, including the negative ones (anger, resentment, relief) that people often feel they are not allowed to have about the dead.

Interpersonal role disputes. A role dispute is a conflict in which the patient and a significant other (a spouse, a parent, a child, a boss) have nonreciprocal expectations of each other: each expects of the other something the other is not willing to give. The goals are to identify the dispute, choose a plan of action, and change expectations or faulty communication until the dispute is resolved. The therapist's first job is to decide which of three stages the dispute has reached, because the strategy depends on it. In renegotiation the two people know they disagree and are actively, often noisily, trying to settle it; the therapist calms things enough for a solution to be found. In impasse the negotiation has stopped: the couple no longer argue but live in a cold war of silent resentment. Here, paradoxically, the therapist may try to increase the open disharmony, getting the issues back on the table so that negotiation can begin again. In dissolution the relationship is irretrievably broken, and the therapist helps the patient end it and mourn it, treating what follows much like grief or a role transition. Throughout, the therapist asks what the dispute is really about, how the two people's values differ, what the options and resources are, whether the same pattern appears in other relationships, and what keeps the dispute going.

Role transitions. A role transition is a change in life status that the patient is finding hard to manage: leaving home for college, marriage, the birth of a child, a promotion or a demotion, losing a job, retirement, divorce, migration, or the diagnosis of a serious illness. Some transitions are welcome and planned, but even a wanted change involves losing an old role, with its familiar people, routines and sources of self-esteem. The goals are to mourn and accept the loss of the old role, to help the patient see the new role more positively, and to restore self-esteem by building a sense of mastery over the new role's demands. The therapist reviews the good and bad sides of both roles realistically, explores feelings about what has been lost and about the change itself, encourages the appropriate release of feeling, and helps the patient build the new skills and the new social supports that the new role requires.

Interpersonal deficits. The fourth area, interpersonal deficits, applies to patients who are socially isolated or whose relationships are few, superficial or chronically unfulfilling, and who cannot name any recent event that set off the depression. It is the default area, chosen when none of the other three fits, and it is the hardest to treat, because it implies long-standing problems with social skills and is more often accompanied by personality difficulties. Some later versions of IPT prefer the gentler name "interpersonal sensitivity". The goals are to reduce the patient's isolation and encourage new relationships. Because the patient may have few current relationships to work with, the therapist reviews past significant relationships, good and bad, looks for repetitive patterns, and in this one area makes explicit use of the relationship with the therapist: how the patient feels about the therapist, and whether those feelings parallel what goes wrong elsewhere. The relationship becomes a rehearsal ground, not an object of interpretation.

Table 3.12 The four problem areas of interpersonal psychotherapy (after Klerman, Weissman and colleagues, 1984).
Problem areaTypical situationGoalsKey strategies
GriefDepression after a death; delayed, distorted or chronic mourningHelp mourning; re-establish interests and relationshipsReconstruct the relationship; events around the death; mixed feelings
Role disputesNonreciprocal expectations with a significant otherIdentify dispute; plan; change expectations or communicationStage it: renegotiation, impasse or dissolution
Role transitionsMarriage, parenthood, job loss, retirement, migration, illnessMourn old role; value new role; build masteryWeigh old and new roles; new skills and supports
Interpersonal deficitsIsolation; few or unfulfilling relationships; no recent eventReduce isolation; form new relationshipsReview past relationships; patterns; use therapy relationship
Exam trap

The four IPT problem areas are grief, interpersonal role disputes, role transitions and interpersonal deficits. Learn the distractors as well. "Schemas, automatic thoughts, behaviours, biological factors" and "cognitive distortions and dysfunctional schemas" are cognitive therapy (Chapter 5). "Id, ego, superego, defences" is Freud's structural model. "Trauma reprocessing through bilateral stimulation" is EMDR (Chapter 4). "Free association and transference interpretation" is psychoanalysis (Sections 3.2 and 3.3). "Cognitive, affective, motor, somatic" is a list of symptom domains, not an IPT concept. Other names for the same areas: role disputes are also called interpersonal disputes or role conflicts, so an option such as "role conflict resolution" is IPT. Finally, the stages of a dispute: the therapist calms a renegotiation, stirs up an impasse to reopen negotiation, and helps the patient mourn a dissolution.

Techniques and the therapist's stance

A session in the middle phase usually begins with the same question, which Klerman recommended in the 1984 manual: "How have things been since we last met?" The question pulls the conversation towards recent events rather than the distant past, and it invites the patient to connect the week's mood with the week's interpersonal events: "I felt terrible on Thursday." "What happened on Thursday?" Over the weeks the patient learns to see the link for himself. The techniques that follow are described in Weissman, Markowitz and Klerman's guide (2000), and most of them are borrowed, honestly, from other therapies; what makes them IPT is that they are all aimed at the chosen problem area.

  • Exploratory techniques: open, non-directive questions and supportive acknowledgement that let the patient bring material forward, and more directive questioning when specific information is needed.
  • Encouragement of affect: helping the patient accept painful feelings, express suppressed ones (grief, anger) and use feelings to guide what he wants from relationships ("Losing your wife has been terrible for you. What might help you feel less alone?").
  • Clarification: restating, asking the patient to rephrase, or pointing out contradictions, such as the gap between what the patient says she feels and how she describes acting.
  • Communication analysis: the signature technique of IPT. The patient reconstructs a recent important conversation or argument in detail, word for word if possible, with tone of voice and what each person did next, so that the two of them can find where the communication failed: an assumption never stated, an indirect hint that was missed, a silence that was taken as agreement.
  • Use of the therapeutic relationship: noticing the patient's feelings and behaviour towards the therapist as an example of how he relates to others, chiefly in the interpersonal deficits area.
  • Behaviour change techniques: directive suggestions and education; decision analysis, in which the therapist helps the patient list the options in an interpersonal situation and weigh the likely consequences of each; and role playing, in which the therapist plays the spouse or boss so that the patient can rehearse a new way of speaking.
  • Adjunctive techniques: the treatment contract and the practical arrangements of therapy.

The therapist's stance differs sharply from the analyst's. The IPT therapist is the patient's advocate, not neutral; active, not passive; warm, hopeful and frankly optimistic that the depression will lift. The relationship is not interpreted as transference, and it is not a friendship. Unlike a cognitive-behaviour therapist, the IPT therapist does not set formal homework or work through thought records, though patients are encouraged to try out between sessions the changes they have planned. Table 3.13 sets IPT beside its two neighbours.

Table 3.13 Interpersonal psychotherapy compared with cognitive-behaviour therapy and psychodynamic therapy.
FeatureIPTCBT (Chapter 5)Psychodynamic
Main focusCurrent relationships and rolesCurrent thoughts and behaviourUnconscious conflict, often from childhood
Link to symptomsMood tied to interpersonal eventsMood tied to thoughtsSymptoms express hidden conflict
Therapist stanceActive, supportive advocateCollaborative, directive teacherNeutral, interpretive
TransferenceNot interpretedNot interpretedCentral
HomeworkInformal; try changesFormal, structuredNone
Length12 to 16 weekly sessionsAbout 12 to 20 sessionsBrief forms to years
Exam trap

A 2025 paper asked which technique or focus IPT does NOT typically include, offering "role playing and role reversal techniques", "grief and bereavement work", "role conflict resolution" and "role transition support", and keyed role playing and role reversal. Choose it: the other three are IPT problem areas, and role reversal (swapping parts with another person on a stage) belongs to Jacob Moreno's psychodrama (Section 7.11). But know the full truth for an interview: IPT does use role playing, as a behaviour change technique, to rehearse a difficult conversation. What IPT does not use is free association, dream analysis, transference interpretation, formal cognitive restructuring or exposure.

A worked example: Meena's role transition

Concepts like these only click when you push a case through them. Meena is 27. Until a year ago she worked as a software tester in Bengaluru, shared a flat with two friends and spoke to her mother every evening. Eight months ago she married Arun, who runs his family's hardware business in a town four hours away, and she moved into his parents' house. For the past three months she has slept badly, cried in the bathroom, lost weight and told her husband that she is "useless". A general practitioner refers her to a clinical psychologist.

In the first session the psychologist reviews her symptoms and scores them on a depression rating scale; they meet criteria for a moderate depressive episode. She names the illness, explains that depression is common and treatable, and gives Meena the sick role: "This is an illness, not a failing in you. It makes everything harder, including cooking and being sociable, and for now that is understandable." She asks whether medication should be considered and agrees with Meena and the referring doctor to begin with IPT and review in a month. In sessions two and three she takes the interpersonal inventory. Arun is kind but works twelve-hour days. Her mother-in-law is polite but corrects the way Meena cooks and speaks to the servants, and Meena swallows her irritation. Her father-in-law barely speaks to her. Her own mother tells her to "adjust". Her college friends have drifted away. The timeline shows that the low mood began soon after the job offer from a firm in the nearby city fell through, when it became clear that she would not be working again soon.

The psychologist offers a formulation: "You have depression, and it seems tied to a big role transition: from an independent working woman in a city, with your own friends and money, to a new wife in a joint family where you have not yet found your place. There is also the start of a dispute with your mother-in-law, which I think is still at the stage of renegotiation, because you both still care what the other thinks. Over the next twelve weeks, let's work mainly on the transition." Meena agrees. In the middle sessions they weigh the old role and the new one honestly (she misses her salary and her friends; she likes Arun, the town's quiet and her sister-in-law). She grieves openly for the life she gave up, which she had never felt allowed to do. Communication analysis of a quarrel over a spoiled dish shows that Meena had never told her mother-in-law what she found hurtful, only gone silent, which the older woman read as sulking. They role-play a calmer conversation, with the psychologist playing the mother-in-law. Decision analysis turns to work: a part-time remote testing job, tutoring, or a course; she chooses to apply for remote work and to ask Arun to talk with his parents about it. By session ten she has two calls a week with an old friend, a short evening walk with her sister-in-law, and an interview scheduled.

In the last three sessions the psychologist says plainly that therapy is ending, invites Meena's sadness about it, and credits the changes to Meena: "You did this." They list the warning signs of a return of depression (early waking, withdrawal from Arun) and what she would do if they appeared. Her depression scores have fallen to the normal range. Notice what did not happen: no one asked about her childhood toilet training, interpreted her feelings about the therapist, or set her a thought record. The work stayed with the people in Meena's life now.

Clinical link

Marital discord and depression often travel together. When the relationship problem is a central maintaining factor and both partners will attend, marital and couple therapy is usually the better choice; behavioural couple therapy for depression is taught in Chapter 7. When only the patient attends, IPT with a role-dispute focus is a strong alternative, and a conjoint form of IPT for depressed patients with marital disputes has also been manualised. The general lesson for interviews: match the intervention to what is keeping the problem going.

Termination and maintenance

Because the course is time-limited, the ending is announced at the start and discussed explicitly in the last two to four sessions. IPT treats termination as a small role transition in its own right, and often as a time of grieving: the patient is losing a supportive relationship, and the therapist invites those feelings rather than brushing them aside. At the same time the tone is positive, like a graduation. The therapist reviews what has changed, credits the patient with the changes so that he recognises his own independent competence, and plans for the future, including the possibility that depression may return. If the treatment has not worked, the therapist does not blame the patient. In a characteristic move, the IPT therapist blames the treatment ("IPT has not helped you enough; that is a failure of the therapy, not of you") and discusses alternatives such as medication or a different psychotherapy.

Depression is often a recurrent illness, so the Pittsburgh group of Ellen Frank and David Kupfer tested maintenance IPT, the same therapy given once a month for years to patients who had recovered. In their study of patients with highly recurrent depression (1990), monthly IPT on its own lengthened the time to the next episode compared with a placebo, although full-dose imipramine protected best. Maintenance IPT is now one option for patients who cannot or will not take long-term medication.

Exam trap

The three IPT phases are initial (sessions 1 to 3: diagnosis, sick role, interpersonal inventory, formulation, contract), middle (work on the problem area) and termination (the last few sessions). A course lasts 12 to 16 weekly sessions, not years, and maintenance IPT is monthly. Do not confuse IPT's three phases with Dreikurs's four phases of Adlerian therapy (Section 3.8) or with Mann's 12-session time-limited psychotherapy (Section 3.6), which is psychodynamic and organised around a central issue.

Evidence and adaptations

IPT has one of the strongest evidence bases of any psychotherapy for depression. In the famous NIMH Treatment of Depression Collaborative Research Program, reported by Irene Elkin and colleagues in 1989, IPT was one of the four treatments compared (with cognitive-behaviour therapy, imipramine plus clinical management and a placebo pill plus clinical management). All four groups improved and the overall differences were small, but among the more severely depressed patients IPT showed some evidence of effectiveness compared with placebo, which CBT did not; Chapter 8 teaches the trial as a landmark of research design. Many trials and several meta-analyses since have found IPT clearly better than control conditions for depression and roughly equal to CBT. The largest review, by Pim Cuijpers and colleagues (2011: 38 studies, 4,356 patients), found IPT well ahead of control conditions (an effect size of 0.63), no different from other psychotherapies, and, when combined with medication as a maintenance treatment, better at preventing relapse than medication alone; an earlier comparative review by the same group (2008) had found IPT slightly more effective than the other psychotherapies and dropout highest from CBT. Guidelines such as those of the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence in Britain list IPT among the high-intensity psychological treatments for depression (Chapter 5).

Key study: group IPT in rural Uganda (Bolton and colleagues, 2003)

The question. Can a psychotherapy designed in American university clinics work in a poor, rural African setting, delivered by local people without professional training? In the Masaka and Rakai districts of south-western Uganda, an area devastated by HIV, a survey in 2000 had found that about one adult in five met criteria for major depression. There is no single word for depression in Luganda, but villagers recognised two local depression-like syndromes. The method. Paul Bolton, Helen Verdeli, Myrna Weissman and colleagues ran a cluster randomised trial in 30 villages, 15 for men and 15 for women. In the intervention villages, depressed adults met in small single-sex groups for 90 minutes a week for 16 weeks, led by a local person of the same sex who had received two weeks of intensive training. Each week the leader reviewed every member's symptoms, members described the week's events and linked them to their mood, and the group offered support and suggestions within the IPT problem areas. The results. Attendance was high and fewer than one in twelve dropped out. After the groups, 6.5 per cent of the intervention participants still met criteria for major depression, against 54.7 per cent in the control villages (before treatment the figures had been 86 and 94 per cent), and everyday functioning improved too. The criticism. The control villages received no alternative treatment, the measures had to be adapted locally, and the follow-up was short (a later report found the gains held at six months). Group IPT has since spread widely in low-resource settings, and in 2016 the World Health Organization published a manual for group IPT delivered by trained lay workers.

As the evidence grew, Klerman and Weissman's colleagues adapted IPT for new groups, each time writing a new manual and testing it in a trial. Three adaptations are named in syllabuses. IPT-A, interpersonal psychotherapy for depressed adolescents, was developed by Laura Mufson, Weissman and colleagues (manual 1993). It keeps the time limit and the problem areas but adds work with parents, attention to developmental issues such as separation from parents, peer pressure and first romantic relationships, and, in its original form, a fifth problem area, single-parent families; the second edition of the manual (2004) dropped it and returned to the standard four areas. Group IPT, as in Uganda, turns the group itself into a source of interpersonal practice and support. And Ellen Frank's interpersonal and social rhythm therapy (IPSRT) for bipolar disorder combines IPT with a behavioural programme for stabilising daily routines. Frank started from the idea that daily social events (getting up, the first contact with another person, starting work, meals, going to bed) act as social zeitgebers, cues that keep the body's circadian rhythms on time, and that disrupting them (a night shift, a long journey, a new baby) can set off episodes of mania or depression in vulnerable people. Patients keep a social rhythm metric, a daily record of when these events happen, and work to keep them regular. IPSRT also adds a fifth problem area, grief for the lost healthy self: mourning the person the patient might have been without the illness. Table 3.14 lists these and the other main adaptations.

Table 3.14 Main adaptations of interpersonal psychotherapy.
AdaptationAssociated namesFor whomWhat is added or changed
IPT-A (1993)Laura Mufson, WeissmanDepressed adolescentsParents involved; developmental tasks; originally a fifth area, single-parent families
Group IPT (Uganda, 2003)Paul Bolton, Helen Verdeli, WeissmanDepressed adults in low-resource settingsGroups led by trained lay workers; WHO manual 2016
IPSRTEllen FrankBipolar disorderSocial rhythm metric; regular routines; grief for the lost healthy self
Maintenance IPT (1990)Ellen Frank, David KupferRecurrent depressionMonthly sessions after recovery
Other versionsMarkowitz and othersDysthymia, perinatal depression, bulimia, older adults, PTSDSame problem-area logic, new manual for each

IPT has limits too. It has done well against depression in pregnancy and after childbirth, and in Christopher Fairburn's Oxford trials for bulimia nervosa it caught up with CBT at follow-up, although more slowly. A trial led by John Markowitz in 2015 found it roughly as effective as prolonged exposure for chronic post-traumatic stress disorder. But it has been less successful with drug misuse, a 2011 trial found cognitive therapy better for social anxiety (Section 5.6), and its weakest area, interpersonal deficits, is exactly where many chronically depressed and personality-disordered patients sit. Critics also point out that IPT's techniques are largely borrowed, so its success may owe much to the common factors of a warm, structured, hopeful relationship (Chapter 8). Its defenders reply that the structure, the focus on a problem area and the medical framing are what make those common factors work.

Finally, the word "interpersonal" is used for so many different therapies and ideas that examiners can build a whole question out of the confusion. Table 3.15 sorts them.

Table 3.15 "Interpersonal" in names and concepts: who is who.
NamePersonWhat it is
Interpersonal psychotherapy (IPT)Gerald Klerman, Myrna WeissmanBrief, manualised therapy for depression; four problem areas
Interpersonal psychoanalysis, interpersonal psychiatryHarry Stack SullivanPsychiatry as the study of interpersonal relations (Section 3.8)
Interpersonal reconstructive therapy; Structural Analysis of Social BehaviorLorna Smith BenjaminTherapy for difficult, treatment-resistant patients built on her circumplex model of interpersonal behaviour
Interpersonal circle; interpersonal communication approachDonald KieslerMaps behaviour on dominance and friendliness; transactions between people
