Exam trap

A practice question asks which therapy is NOT commonly used for survivors of sexual assault, and keys interpersonal psychotherapy. Accept it: IPT was built for depression, and the first-line trauma treatments are trauma-focused CBT (Section 5.6) and EMDR (Chapter 4). But note Markowitz's 2015 PTSD trial, so never say IPT "cannot" treat trauma. Two further confusions: IPT is not a psychodynamic therapy in technique, even though Sullivan is among its ancestors, so "managing transference" is never an IPT answer; and IPT for bipolar disorder is IPSRT, Ellen Frank's, whose distinctive tool is the social rhythm metric. Finally, for an adult with a depressive disorder and marital discord, the keyed answer (IHBAS 2020) is marital and couple therapy, not psychodynamic, cognitive-behavioural or supportive therapy: treat what maintains the problem (see the Clinical link after Meena's case).

3.8 The Early Dissenters as TherapistsHigh yield · 6 Qs

In 1902 Freud began inviting a few interested doctors to his flat at Berggasse 19 in Vienna on Wednesday evenings to discuss psychology. The Wednesday Psychological Society grew into the Vienna Psychoanalytic Society, and within a decade the movement had produced its first great quarrels. Two of Freud's most gifted followers left him for good: Alfred Adler in 1911 and Carl Jung in 1913. A generation later, in America, Karen Horney and Harry Stack Sullivan rebuilt psychoanalysis around culture and relationships. Their theories of personality belong to the Personality book; this section asks what each one actually did with a patient, and which techniques belong to whom, and it ends with a table that sorts them.

Before the details, notice what the four dissenters share. All of them moved the therapist out from behind the couch: Jung, Adler and Sullivan to face-to-face conversation in chairs, Horney to a flexible arrangement in which the patient could lie, sit or walk about. All of them gave sexuality a smaller place than Freud did and gave society, relationships, goals or meaning a larger one. All of them made the therapist more active and the relationship more equal. And all of them looked forwards as well as backwards: towards the person the patient was trying to become, not only towards the child he had been.

Jung's analytical psychotherapy: aims, setting and stages

Carl Gustav Jung (1875 to 1961) was a Swiss psychiatrist who, from 1900, worked at the Burghölzli, the psychiatric hospital of the University of Zurich, under Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939), the psychiatrist who coined the term schizophrenia. Jung had made his name before he met Freud, with experimental studies of word association (described below). The two men met in Vienna in 1907 and talked for thirteen hours. Freud came to see Jung as his "crown prince", and in 1910 Jung became the first president of the International Psychoanalytical Association. But Jung could not accept that the libido was essentially sexual; for him it was a general psychic energy. His book on the transformations of the libido (1912; known in English as Psychology of the Unconscious and later Symbols of Transformation) made the split inevitable, and they broke in 1913. Jung called his own school analytical psychology.

Only as much of the theory as the therapy needs is given here (the full account is in the Personality book). Jung divided the unconscious into two layers. The personal unconscious contains forgotten and repressed experiences, organised into complexes: emotionally charged clusters of ideas and memories (a "mother complex", an "inferiority complex") that behave like small autonomous personalities and disturb conscious life. Beneath it lies the collective unconscious, a layer shared by all human beings and inherited rather than acquired, made up of archetypes: universal, inborn patterns or predispositions that shape how we perceive and respond to typical human situations, and which appear in myths, religions, fairy tales and dreams across cultures. Four archetypes matter most in therapy. The persona is the mask a person shows the world, the social role. The shadow holds the qualities a person refuses to recognise in himself, which he then tends to see in others. The anima is the unconscious feminine side of a man, and the animus the unconscious masculine side of a woman; each is projected onto real people of the other sex, which is part of why people fall in love with fantasies. The Self is the archetype of wholeness and the centre of the whole psyche, often symbolised in dreams by a circle or a mandala. Jung also described the attitudes of introversion and extraversion and four functions (thinking, feeling, sensation and intuition), which later inspired the Myers-Briggs Type Indicator (Assessment book).

The goal of Jungian therapy follows from this map. It is individuation: the lifelong process by which a person becomes a psychological "in-dividual", a separate, indivisible whole, by bringing the unconscious into a living relationship with consciousness. In practice this means seeing through the persona, owning the shadow, coming to terms with the anima or animus, and moving the centre of the personality from the narrow conscious ego towards the Self. Jung thought individuation was above all the task of the second half of life. He reported that about a third of his patients suffered from no clinically definable neurosis at all, but from the senselessness and aimlessness of their lives, and many of them were successful people in middle age. Where Freud's patient was a young person whose development had been blocked, Jung's typical patient was a middle-aged one whose life had lost its meaning.

The setting matched the aim. Jung sat face to face with his patients, usually in armchairs, and saw them less often than Freud did, typically once or twice a week, so that patients learned to work on their own dreams and images between sessions. He called psychotherapy a dialectical procedure, a dialogue between two people in which the doctor is as much "in the analysis" as the patient and may be changed by it. That is why Jung's Zurich school was the first to insist that every analyst must himself be analysed (the training analysis of Section 3.2), and why Jung spoke of the analyst as a "wounded healer", able to help others because he has been wounded himself and knows it.

In an essay of 1929, "Problems of Modern Psychotherapy", Jung described four stages that an analysis may pass through, and generously gave three of them to his rivals (Table 3.16). The stages are not a fixed sequence, and not every analysis reaches the last one.

Table 3.16 Jung's four stages of analytical therapy (1929).
StageWhat happensWhose idea Jung said it was
1 Confession (catharsis)Secrets and held-back feelings are told and releasedBreuer and Freud's cathartic method
2 ElucidationInterpretation, especially of the transference and of the patient's infantile wishesFreud
3 EducationThe patient is helped to adapt to society, with new habits and social responsibilityAdler
4 TransformationIndividuation; both patient and doctor are changedJung himself
Exam trap

The collective unconscious is Carl Jung's, not Freud's (personal, repressed unconscious), Adler's or Horney's. "Animus is the unconscious masculine side of a woman's psyche" is Jung's too; the anima is the feminine side of a man. Anna Freud gave the defences (Section 3.4) and Viktor Frankl the will to meaning (Chapter 6); neither wrote about the animus. Keep three schools apart by name: psychoanalysis is Freud's, analytical psychology is Jung's, individual psychology is Adler's. And do not confuse Jung's individuation (becoming a whole self, chiefly in the second half of life) with Margaret Mahler's separation-individuation (the toddler's psychological birth, Section 3.4).

Jung's methods: word association, amplification and active imagination

Jung's first clinical tool was a laboratory one. Word association had been studied by Francis Galton and in Wilhelm Wundt's laboratory, but from 1904 Jung and his Burghölzli colleague Franz Riklin turned it into a way of finding complexes. In Jung's word association test the examiner reads out a list of 100 stimulus words (head, green, water, to sing, death, long, ship, to pay, window, friendly, and so on), one at a time; the patient replies with the first word that comes to mind, and the examiner times each reply with a stopwatch. Most replies come quickly. But some words touch a complex, and then the reply betrays it: a long delay, no reply at all, repeating the stimulus word, an odd or very personal reply, a reply of several words, stammering or a slip of the tongue, giving the same reply to different words, or forgetting the reply when the list is repeated. Jung also measured changes in the skin's electrical resistance with a galvanometer, an early ancestor of the "lie detector". A cluster of disturbed replies around death, money and a particular name could reveal a hidden grief or guilt that the patient had not mentioned. The word association test is a projective technique in the Assessment book's sense; it is not free association, in which the patient talks continuously with no stimulus words at all (Section 3.2).

For Jung, as for Freud, dreams were central, but he read them differently. Jung rejected the distinction between a manifest and a latent content: a dream is not a disguise, he said, but a natural, undisguised expression of the unconscious in the language of images, and it "is its own interpretation". Most dreams, he argued, are compensatory: they balance the one-sidedness of the conscious attitude, showing the dreamer what he is ignoring. In one of his own cases Jung dreamed of a patient sitting high on the tower of a castle so that he had to crane his neck to look up at her; he concluded that in waking life he had been looking down on her, told her so, and the stalled treatment moved again. Dreams can also be prospective, sketching possible future developments. Jung interpreted dreams on two levels: on the objective level the people in a dream stand for the real people they resemble, while on the subjective level every figure represents a part of the dreamer himself. He preferred to work with a series of dreams over months rather than with one dream alone, and he distinguished ordinary "little" dreams, drawn from the personal unconscious, from rare "big" dreams full of archetypal images, which a person may remember for a lifetime.

His method of working with dream images was amplification. Freud's free association runs away from a dream element along a chain of memories, and Jung objected that it leads to the patient's complexes but away from the dream. Jung asked the patient to stay with the image itself and circle around it, returning to it again and again ("stick to the image"). In personal amplification the patient gives his own associations to that image. In archetypal (or objective) amplification the analyst enlarges the image with parallels from mythology, religion, folklore and art, so that the patient sees that his private dream of a descent into a cave, a flood or a divine child belongs to a story humanity has told many times. The archetypal parallels show what the image may mean and what it may be preparing the dreamer for.

Jung's most original method grew out of his own crisis. After the break with Freud he went through several years of disturbing visions and fantasies, and instead of suppressing them he deliberately entered them, wrote them down and painted them, in a large illustrated volume published only in 2009 as The Red Book. From these experiments came active imagination: a technique in which the patient, awake and attentive, focuses on an image from a dream or a mood, lets it move and develop of its own accord, and then engages with it actively, by talking to the figures, asking them questions and answering back, or by painting, sculpting, writing or dancing it. It differs from passive daydreaming because the conscious ego takes part. The Jungian analyst Gerhard Adler (no relation of Alfred) described a patient haunted by a recurrent fantasy of a veiled dark figure. Asked to concentrate on the figure instead of pushing it away, the man imagined removing veil after veil until he found, with a tremendous shock, that the face beneath was his mother's. Active imagination requires a fairly stable ego, so Jungians use it cautiously and rarely with patients at risk of psychosis. Jung also encouraged patients to paint their images; he noticed that circular, symmetrical designs, which he called by the Sanskrit word mandala, appeared spontaneously at times of inner reorganisation, and read them as symbols of the Self. Later, the Swiss Jungian Dora Kalff developed sandplay therapy, in which a child or adult builds scenes in a tray of sand with small figures.

Exam trap

Three "association" techniques are easily muddled. Free association (Freud) is the patient talking freely without stimulus words. The word association test (Jung, with Riklin, from 1904) uses a list of 100 stimulus words and reaction times to detect complexes. Amplification (Jung) stays with one dream image and enlarges it with personal and mythological parallels instead of drifting away from it. Active imagination is Jung's dialogue with inner images while awake; it is not Freud's pressure technique, not hypnosis and not Gestalt's empty chair, though it resembles the last. Jung, not Freud, held that dreams are compensatory and are not disguises.

Adler's individual psychology as a therapy

One of the earliest memories of Alfred Adler (1870 to 1937) was this: "I remember sitting on a bench, bandaged up on account of rickets, with my healthy elder brother sitting opposite me. He could run, jump, and move about quite effortlessly, while for me, movement of any sort was a strain and an effort." A sickly second son with a robust, successful elder brother (whose name, as it happens, was Sigmund), he decided as a small boy, after nearly dying of pneumonia, to become a doctor. You will see in a moment why an Adlerian therapist would find that memory so revealing. Adler joined Freud's Wednesday circle in 1902 and was president of the Vienna Psychoanalytic Society in 1910, but his growing emphasis on inferiority, power and social life rather than sexuality was intolerable to Freud. In 1911 Adler left with a group of supporters, and the following year he founded the Society for Individual Psychology. After the First World War he set up child guidance clinics in Vienna, where he counselled children, parents and teachers in front of audiences; he later moved to New York and died in 1937 on a lecture tour in Scotland.

The name individual psychology does not mean a psychology of the isolated individual. "Individual" comes from the Latin for indivisible: Adler insisted that a person is a unified whole who must be understood as a whole, moving purposefully towards goals in a social world. Again, only the theory the therapy needs is given here (the rest is in the Personality book).

  • Adler began, in Study of Organ Inferiority (1907), with organ inferiority: a constitutionally weak organ or body part produces a sense of inferiority, which the person strives to make up for, sometimes overcompensating until the weakness becomes a strength (the stammering boy who becomes an orator, the sickly boy who becomes a physician).
  • He then generalised the idea. Every child, small and dependent among large and capable adults, feels inferior, and this is normal and useful, because inferiority feelings drive the lifelong striving for superiority, which Adler later called striving for perfection or completion: not dominating others, but overcoming, growing and mastering. When inferiority feelings become overwhelming, the person develops an inferiority complex: a settled conviction of inadequacy that stops him from facing life's problems. Some cover it with a superiority complex, an exaggerated show of importance (boasting, arrogance, belittling others) that hides the inferiority beneath.
  • People strive towards goals that are partly imagined. Adler borrowed from the philosopher Hans Vaihinger (1852 to 1933), whose The Philosophy of "As If" (1911) argued that people live by useful fictions (such as "all people are created equal") that they treat as if they were true. Adler's fictional finalism is the idea that behaviour is guided by such fictional, often unconscious final goals ("I must be first", "I must never be at anyone's mercy"), so that a person is pulled by the future more than pushed by the past.
  • Each person develops, by about the age of five, a unique style of life (or lifestyle): his characteristic way of striving towards his goal and meeting life's problems, built on his private logic, his personal, partly mistaken beliefs about himself and the world.
  • The measure of mental health is social interest (Adler's Gemeinschaftsgefühl, "community feeling"): an innate potential, which must be developed, for feeling part of humanity and contributing to the common good. Every person must meet three life tasks, work, friendship (society) and love, and later Adlerians added self-development and spirituality. Neurosis, crime and psychosis are, for Adler, styles of life low in social interest: attempts to gain superiority on the "useless side of life" while avoiding the life tasks.

From this theory comes a distinctive view of symptoms. A symptom is not the disguised expression of a repressed wish; it is a purposeful strategy, serving the person's goal, often as an excuse ("I could achieve great things, if only I did not have these headaches") or as a way of controlling others. Therapy therefore asks not "What caused this?" but "What is it for?"

Adlerian therapy and counselling, as later systematised by Adler's most influential follower Rudolf Dreikurs (1897 to 1972), who carried the school to Chicago, is usually described in four phases, which overlap (Figure 3.9). The first is the relationship: a cooperative, equal relationship of mutual respect and trust, with the goals of client and therapist aligned, since misaligned goals look like resistance. The second is assessment, the investigation of the lifestyle. The third is insight, through interpretation, which Adlerians offer tentatively ("Is it possible that...?", "Could it be that...?") and which concerns goals and purposes rather than causes. The fourth is reorientation, in which the client acts on the insight, takes risks and tries new behaviour, with a toolbox of active techniques. Encouragement runs through all four: restoring the client's courage and faith in himself, focusing on assets rather than deficits. Adler thought discouragement was at the root of most problems, and Adlerians regard encouragement as the most important single ingredient of their work.

Lifestyle assessment: family constellation, birth order and early recollections

Lifestyle assessment is the systematic investigation of a client's style of life, usually through a semi-structured interview or questionnaire (Dreikurs devised the first formal versions, and Bernard Shulman and Harold Mosak later published a widely used Life Style Inventory). It has three main sources of data, plus dreams and the client's assets.

The first is the family constellation: the pattern of relationships in the family of origin, including each sibling's age gap, character and alliances, the parents' values and their relationship with each other and with each child, and the child's own perceived place in the family. Clients are often asked to rate their siblings on traits such as the hardest worker, the most rebellious, the best at school, the most punished, the most spoiled. Within the constellation Adler gave special attention to birth order (Table 3.17). What matters, he stressed, is the psychological position, how the child experiences his place, not the bare ordinal number. In a family with children aged 14, 13 and 1, the youngest is in many ways an only child.

Table 3.17 Adler's birth order positions (tendencies, not rules).
PositionSituationPossible strengthsPossible problems
First-bornSole attention, then "dethroned" by a siblingResponsible, organised, protective of othersBids to regain power; anxious about being overtaken; conservative
Second (middle)Always has a pacesetter aheadCompetitive, ambitious, optimisticFeels squeezed or gives up if the elder excels
YoungestNever dethroned; often the family's petFast to develop; may surpass allPampered, dependent, expects others to provide
Only childCentre of attention among adultsMature early; comfortable with adultsHas not learned to share or compete; disappointed when not the centre

The second source is early recollections: the client's earliest specific memories, usually from before about the age of eight or ten. Adler argued that memory is selective in the service of the lifestyle. Out of thousands of childhood events, a person keeps the few that confirm his view of himself and of life, so early recollections are a compact statement of the lifestyle, whether or not they happened exactly as remembered. Adlerians distinguish a true recollection (a single, specific incident the person can picture: "I was four; I was standing at the gate and my father lifted me onto his scooter") from a report ("We always went to my grandmother's in the summer"), and they ask for the most vivid moment and the feeling attached to it. Read Adler's own memory again in this light: a boy watching his healthy elder brother from a bench, feeling that every movement is an effort. It is a portrait of the inferiority he spent his life overcoming, and of the theory he built from it. Dreikurs often collected ten or more recollections from a client and expected them to change, in the telling, as the lifestyle changed during therapy.

From these data the therapist identifies basic mistakes, the self-defeating convictions of the private logic. Mosak grouped them into five kinds: overgeneralisations ("Everyone is against me"), false or impossible goals of security ("I must please everyone"), misperceptions of life and its demands ("Life never gives me a break"), minimising or denying one's own worth ("I am stupid"), and faulty values ("You have to cheat to get ahead"). Assessment also lists the client's assets, because encouragement needs something to build on. Adler read dreams as forward-looking rehearsals that create the mood a person needs for a course of action he has already half chosen, not as disguised wishes from the past. And early in assessment many Adlerians ask the question: "What would you do, or what would be different, if you were well?" The answer reveals what the symptom is helping the client avoid ("If I didn't have these panic attacks, I'd go back to college"). Dreikurs added a diagnostic use: if the client answers that nothing would be different except that the pain would be gone, the symptom may well be organic rather than psychological.

Clinical link

Rohit, 24, the middle of three sons in a Mysuru family, comes for help with procrastination that has cost him two attempts at a bank recruitment examination. His elder brother is an engineer in Pune; the youngest is the family's favourite. Rohit's earliest recollection: "I was five. My brother won a prize at school and everyone was clapping. I hid behind the curtain." His sibling ratings put the elder brother first for "hardest working" and "best at studies", the younger first for "most liked", and himself first for nothing. An Adlerian formulation might read his procrastination as a safeguard: if he never fully tries, he can never be shown to be less than his brother ("I could have cleared it if I had studied"). The basic mistake is "I count only if I am first". The work would aim at encouragement, at a goal of contribution rather than comparison, and at small tasks he can complete.

Adlerian reorientation: acting as if, spitting in the soup and the push-button

Adlerians are known above all for their lively action techniques, used mainly in reorientation. Several have colourful names, and each belongs to a specific person, which is exactly what examiners test.

Acting as if asks the client to behave, for a limited time, as if he were already the person he wishes to be, or as if a feared action will work. It comes from Vaihinger's "as if" philosophy. A student afraid to tell her professor about a marking error is asked to "act as if" she were confident, and simply go and do it. Dreikurs and Mosak compared it to trying on a new suit: the suit does not change who you are, but it can make you feel and act differently, and the experience then changes the belief.

Spitting in the client's soup takes its name from a trick of boarding-school boys, who spat in someone's soup so that they could have it themselves; the eater may go on eating, but it will not taste the same. The therapist identifies the hidden purpose or payoff of a self-defeating behaviour and points it out, so that the behaviour becomes less attractive. A mother who describes how much she sacrifices for her grown children might hear, "It is a pity that all that sacrifice leaves you no life of your own; and I wonder whether it also keeps them a little in your debt." She may continue, but she cannot do it innocently any more.

The push-button technique, developed by Harold Mosak, teaches clients that they create their own feelings. The client closes his eyes and recalls a pleasant memory in detail, noticing the feelings that come with it; then an unpleasant memory of hurt, failure or anger, noticing how the feelings change; then the pleasant scene again. The lesson is that, like pressing a button, he can choose what to think about and therefore, to a large degree, how he feels. (Its resemblance to cognitive therapy is not an accident; Albert Ellis acknowledged Adler as a forerunner, Chapter 5.)

Catching oneself asks the client to notice, without self-blame, the moment he slips into the old pattern. At first he catches himself afterwards ("I did it again"), then during, and finally before, at which point he can choose differently. A wife who always walks out of the room when her husband gets angry learns to catch the urge to flee and say, instead, "I can see you're getting angry and I'm getting scared; can we talk?"

Several other techniques complete the box. Paradoxical intention, which Adler called "prescribing the symptom" and Dreikurs "antisuggestion", asks the client to do deliberately, even exaggerate, the very thing he is struggling against, so that the symptom loses its purpose and its power; Viktor Frankl (1905 to 1997), who passed through Adler's circle in the 1920s before being expelled in 1927, later made paradoxical intention a central technique of logotherapy (Chapter 6). Task setting and homework turn decisions into small, achievable steps. Creating images gives the client a mental picture of himself succeeding. Avoiding the trap (older texts call it "avoiding the tar baby") means refusing to behave as the client's mistaken beliefs predict, for instance refusing to be irritated by a client who expects to annoy everyone. Notice the contrast with psychoanalysis. Adlerians use the relationship and comment on it, but they do not foster a transference neurosis or analyse the transference as a repetition of childhood; the relationship is a meeting of equals working on goals.

Dreikurs deserves a pointer of his own. In Chicago he turned Adler's child guidance work into a system of parent education (Children: The Challenge, 1964, with Vicki Soltz), arguing that a misbehaving child is a discouraged child pursuing one of four mistaken goals, attention, power, revenge or display of inadequacy, to be met with encouragement and natural and logical consequences rather than rewards and punishments. He also introduced multiple therapy (two therapists seeing one client together). His parenting work belongs with child and family work in Chapters 7 and 10.

Exam trap

Which technique would not be used by an Adlerian counsellor? Analysis of transference (psychoanalysis). Acting "as if", the push-button technique, encouragement, spitting in the client's soup, catching oneself, the question, paradoxical intention, lifestyle assessment, early recollections and birth order are all Adlerian. The push-button is Mosak's; the four goals of misbehaviour are Dreikurs's; acting "as if" goes back to Vaihinger. The proponent of individual psychology is Adler, not Horney, Jung or Erikson. The inferiority complex and striving for superiority are Adler's, not Jung's, Frankl's or Erikson's, and so is organ inferiority (not Sullivan's, Klein's or Anna Freud's). Fixed-role therapy, in which a client lives as a scripted new character, is George Kelly's, not Adler's acting "as if" (Section 5.5).

Horney: neurotic needs, the tyranny of the should and self-analysis

Karen Horney (1885 to 1952) trained in medicine in Germany, was analysed by Freud's loyal follower Karl Abraham (an analysis she found of little help), and taught at the Berlin Psychoanalytic Institute before moving to the United States in 1932, first to Chicago and then to New York. Her early papers attacked Freud's view of women, arguing that "penis envy" reflected women's real social disadvantages and that men might as well envy women's capacity to bear children ("womb envy"; the theory is in the Personality book). Her books, The Neurotic Personality of Our Time (1937), New Ways in Psychoanalysis (1939), Self-Analysis (1942), Our Inner Conflicts (1945) and Neurosis and Human Growth (1950), placed the roots of neurosis in culture and in disturbed human relationships rather than in instinct. In 1941 the New York Psychoanalytic Society stripped her of her status as a training analyst, and she founded the Association for the Advancement of Psychoanalysis and the American Institute for Psychoanalysis.

Horney's starting point is basic anxiety: the child's feeling of being isolated and helpless in a potentially hostile world, produced not by instinct but by parents who fail to give real warmth and security (through domination, indifference, inconsistency, lack of respect and so on), along with the basic hostility the child dares not express towards the people on whom he depends. To cope, the child adopts protective strategies that, if they become rigid and compulsive, turn into what Horney called neurotic needs. She listed ten in Self-Analysis (1942), and in Our Inner Conflicts (1945) grouped them into three neurotic trends: moving towards people, moving against people and moving away from people (Table 3.18). Healthy people use all three flexibly, as the situation demands; the neurotic is locked into one and cannot help using it everywhere, and the conflict between the trends he suppresses is the core of his neurosis.

Table 3.18 Horney's ten neurotic needs grouped into three neurotic trends.
TrendPersonality typeNeurotic needs
Moving towards peopleCompliantAffection and approval; a dominant partner
Moving against peopleAggressivePower; exploitation; prestige; admiration; achievement or ambition
Moving away from peopleDetachedSelf-sufficiency and independence; perfection; narrow limits to life

In her last book Horney described how the neurotic person, unable to bear his real self, constructs an idealised self-image, a glorified picture of who he is or should be (perfectly generous, perfectly strong, perfectly self-sufficient), and spends his energy on a "search for glory" to make it real. The idealised image issues commands, and Horney's name for them is famous: the tyranny of the should. "I should be the perfect daughter, the perfect doctor, always calm, never tired, liked by everyone, top of every list." Because the shoulds are impossible, every failure produces self-hatred and contempt for the actual self, and the person drifts ever further from the real self, the central inner source of growth. One escape is externalisation, experiencing the inner conflict as if it were outside (blaming others, institutions or fate). A student who must top the university and collapses into self-loathing at a second rank is living under the tyranny of the should. Cognitive therapists will recognise a cousin of Albert Ellis's "musturbation" (Chapter 5), and Ellis acknowledged Horney's influence.

As a therapist Horney kept free association and dream analysis but changed their use. She worked from the present before the past: first the patient's current attitudes, defences and conflicts, and only then their history, uncovering layer below layer like peeling an onion. She paid close attention to the patient's visible emotional reactions to the analyst, as a sample of his attitudes to others. She read dreams as attempts to solve problems, constructively or neurotically, with the dream feeling as the safest clue. She kept a couch but let patients sit, lie or walk as they wished, and she described her attitude as constructive friendliness: analysis was to be "an exquisitely cooperative enterprise". The goal was to help the patient give up the idealised image and the shoulds, a painful disillusionment, and find his way back to the real self and to self-realisation, which Horney believed every person naturally strives for if obstacles are removed.

Horney's most practical contribution was self-analysis. Disappointed by her own analysis, she analysed herself for the rest of her life, and in Self-Analysis (1942) she argued that people with mild or moderate neurotic problems could, with effort, understand themselves without an analyst or between sessions. She distinguished occasional self-analysis (working out why one reacted badly on a particular occasion) from systematic self-analysis (a sustained exploration of one's personality), and illustrated the second with the long case of "Clare", a woman who worked her way out of a morbid dependency on a lover. Horney was honest about the limits: the self-analyst has blind spots and resistances that an analyst would see. Self-analysis is an ancestor of every self-help book and of the modern idea that clients can carry on therapeutic work on their own.

Exam trap

The "tyranny of the should", basic anxiety, the ten neurotic needs, the three neurotic trends (towards, against, away from people), the idealised self-image, womb envy and self-analysis are all Karen Horney's. Do not give the "shoulds" to Ellis (his term is "musts" or "musturbation") or to Rogers (conditions of worth, Chapter 6), though all three ideas are relatives. Horney's "basic anxiety" is not Freud's "signal anxiety" or Sullivan's anxiety transmitted from the mother. "Moving against people" is the aggressive type, not the detached one.

Sullivan: the psychiatrist as participant observer

Harry Stack Sullivan (1892 to 1949) was an American psychiatrist who, unlike the European dissenters, did his formative work not with the anxious middle classes but with young men with schizophrenia. In the 1920s, at the Sheppard and Enoch Pratt Hospital near Baltimore, he set up a small ward for young male patients with early schizophrenia, staffed by attendants he chose and trained himself for their warmth and their ability to treat patients as fellow human beings. He reported results far better than was usual at the time. From that work came his conviction that even severe mental illness is played out, and can be changed, in relationships, and his most quoted sentence, the "one-genus postulate": "We are all much more simply human than otherwise." His major books were published after his death from lectures and notes: The Interpersonal Theory of Psychiatry (1953) and The Psychiatric Interview (1954).

For Sullivan, psychiatry is the study of interpersonal relations, and personality exists only in a person's relationships with others, real or imagined. Anxiety, which he believed the infant first catches from an anxious mother through a kind of emotional contagion he called empathy, is the great disrupter of relationships. To avoid it, the developing person builds a self-system and uses security operations, interpersonal manoeuvres that reduce anxiety at the cost of accurate awareness. The most important for therapy is selective inattention: simply not noticing those aspects of one's own behaviour, or of other people's, that would provoke anxiety. A man who keeps losing jobs because he quietly mocks his superiors genuinely does not notice that he does it, and is honestly puzzled each time. Sullivan's term for transference-like distortion is parataxic distortion: perceiving and reacting to a person in terms of an earlier relationship or a fantasy, not as he actually is. His three modes of experience (prototaxic, parataxic and syntaxic), his personifications of the self ("good-me", "bad-me" and the dreaded "not-me") and his stress on preadolescent "chumship" belong to the Personality book.

The clinical consequence is Sullivan's best-known idea. The psychiatrist cannot be the detached observer of Freud's mirror, because the moment two people are in a room, each is part of what the other is responding to. The psychiatrist is a participant observer: he takes part in the interaction and observes it at the same time, including his own contribution to it, and he uses what happens between himself and the patient as evidence about the patient's patterns with others. Sullivan described the psychiatrist as an "expert in interpersonal relations", and the interview as the psychiatrist's main instrument. In The Psychiatric Interview he divided an interview into four stages: the formal inception (why the person has come and the interviewer's first impressions), the reconnaissance (a rough social and personal history, who this person is), the detailed inquiry (the longest stage, in which the interviewer explores the person's development, relationships and difficulties in detail and tests the hypotheses formed so far, noticing exactly where the patient becomes anxious and evasive), and the termination (a summary of what has been learned, a prescription for action and a formal leave-taking). Throughout, the interviewer tries to keep the patient's anxiety at a level that allows him to keep talking.

In therapy, Sullivan's aim was to help the patient notice what he selectively ignores, correct his parataxic distortions by checking them against reality, a process he called consensual validation, and so live in more satisfying relationships with less anxiety. The style was active and conversational: questions more than interpretations, close attention to concrete recent interactions ("What exactly did he say? And then what did you do?"), and little interest in reconstructing infantile sexuality. You can hear the ancestor of IPT's communication analysis in that last question. With Erich Fromm (1900 to 1980), the social philosopher and analyst of Escape from Freedom (1941), Clara Thompson and Frieda Fromm-Reichmann (1889 to 1957), Sullivan founded the William Alanson White Institute in New York in the 1940s, the home of "interpersonal psychoanalysis". Fromm-Reichmann carried his approach into the intensive psychotherapy of schizophrenia at Chestnut Lodge in Maryland (Principles of Intensive Psychotherapy, 1950), a treatment one of her patients later fictionalised in I Never Promised You a Rose Garden (1964). Sullivan's influence runs on through interpersonal psychotherapy (Section 3.7), Strupp and Binder's time-limited dynamic psychotherapy (Section 3.6) and the relational analysts (Section 3.5).

Exam trap

The psychiatrist as participant observer, the detailed inquiry, selective inattention, security operations, parataxic distortion and "we are all much more simply human than otherwise" are Sullivan's. Selective inattention is not repression in Freud's sense (Sullivan's term concerns not noticing in the moment, to avoid anxiety in a relationship) and not the cognitive psychologist's selective attention. Parataxic distortion is Sullivan's word for something close to transference; do not confuse the parataxic mode with Piaget's preoperational stage, although both describe a child's prelogical thinking.

Who uses which technique

Because the dissenters borrowed from Freud and from one another, and because their followers borrowed from everyone, exam options mix their techniques with those of later therapies. Table 3.19 is the sorting table for this chapter and its neighbours. Learn it as pairs: technique and person.

Exam trap

Three pairs cause most errors. Free association is Freud's, but the word association test is Jung's. Acting "as if" is Adler's, but fixed-role therapy is Kelly's and role reversal is Moreno's. Paradoxical intention is best known as Frankl's, but Adler ("prescribing the symptom") and Dreikurs ("antisuggestion") used the same move earlier, so in an Adlerian list it is not the odd one out. The odd one out in any Adlerian list is a psychoanalytic technique: analysis of transference, free association on the couch or interpretation of resistance.

3.9 Transactional AnalysisMedium yield · 2 Qs

In 1956 a psychiatrist in his mid-forties applied for membership of the San Francisco Psychoanalytic Institute. He had been in analytic training for fifteen years, first in New York and then in San Francisco, and he expected to be admitted. He was turned down: he should have several more years of analysis, the Institute told him, and then apply again. The rejection hurt, and it also set him free. Within two years he had published the first papers on a method of his own, and within eight he had written a runaway best-seller with the odd title Games People Play. His name was Eric Berne (1910 to 1970), and his method was transactional analysis (TA): a theory of personality and a form of psychotherapy built on the observation that people shift between distinct states of mind, each with its own way of speaking, and that much human misery is played out in the exchanges between those states.

TA belongs in this chapter because it is a rebellious child of psychoanalysis. Berne kept the analyst's interest in childhood, in repetition and in hidden motives, but he threw away the couch, the jargon and the open-ended time scale. He wanted concepts so plain that patients could use them on themselves, and treatment so clearly contracted that both parties would know when it had succeeded. This section follows his ideas in the order he built them: the ego states of a single person, the transactions between two people, the strokes and games that keep people locked together, and the life scripts and positions that lie beneath it all.

Berne and the ego state

Berne was born Eric Bernstein in Montreal, the son of a doctor, and qualified in medicine at McGill University in 1935. He trained in psychiatry in the United States, served as an army psychiatrist during the Second World War, and took the shorter surname Berne. His own analysts are worth remembering, because both appear elsewhere in this chapter. From 1941 he was analysed in New York by Paul Federn, the Viennese analyst who had written about ego states and the shifting boundaries of the ego (Sections 3.1 and 3.4); from 1947 he was analysed in San Francisco by Erik Erikson, the ego psychologist of the identity crisis (Section 3.4). Federn's idea that the ego can exist in several distinct states, each one a whole way of experiencing oneself, became the seed of TA.

The seed sprouted in the consulting room. One of Berne's patients, a successful lawyer, remarked during a session: "I'm not really a lawyer. I'm just a little boy." In court he was a sharp and competent professional; at other moments he felt and behaved like the small boy he had once been. Berne began to ask, at different moments, which of the two was talking, the lawyer or the little boy, and found that the distinction made immediate sense to the patient. Later he identified a third state, in which patients thought, spoke and behaved like their own parents. Over the next few years Berne published his first papers, "Ego States in Psychotherapy" (1957) and "Transactional Analysis: A New and Effective Method of Group Therapy" (1958), followed by the full statement, Transactional Analysis in Psychotherapy (1961). Games People Play (1964) took the ideas to the general public, the International Transactional Analysis Association was founded in the same year, and Berne's last book, What Do You Say After You Say Hello?, appeared in 1972, two years after his death.

Berne defined an ego state as a consistent system of feelings accompanied by a related set of behaviour patterns: a whole way of thinking, feeling and acting that a person moves into and out of. He identified three, and wrote them with capital letters to distinguish them from real parents, adults and children. Because an ego state is a pattern of behaviour as well as of experience, it can be recognised, and Berne listed four ways of doing so. The behavioural diagnosis uses words, tone of voice, gestures, posture and facial expression. The social diagnosis notes which ego state the person tends to draw out in others (a person who sounds like a scolding parent tends to provoke a sulking child). The historical diagnosis asks where the pattern came from ("Who used to say that?"). The phenomenological diagnosis is the person's own re-experiencing of the state, as when a grown woman, describing a humiliation, suddenly feels as small as she did at six.

Students often map the three ego states onto Freud's superego, ego and id, and Berne himself acknowledged a family resemblance. But he insisted on the difference. Freud's agencies are theoretical constructs that no one ever meets; ego states are observable realities, whole states of a real person. The Child ego state is not a cauldron of drives but the actual child that this person once was, with that child's feelings, logic and ways of coping. The Parent is not merely a conscience but a recording of the real parents' behaviour, their kindness included. Berne gave them formal names as "psychic organs": the exteropsyche (Parent, borrowed from outside), the neopsyche (Adult, dealing with the present) and the archaeopsyche (Child, the archaic, preserved past).

Exam trap

The idea of ego states came to Berne from his analyst Paul Federn; Berne was also analysed by Erik Erikson, which is perhaps why Berne's name turns up as a distractor in questions about the "identity crisis" (Erikson's term, not Berne's). Do not equate the Parent, Adult and Child with the superego, ego and id: ego states are observable patterns of behaviour and experience, not hypothetical agencies. And "persona", offered as a fourth ego state in some questions, is Carl Jung's archetype of the social mask (Section 3.8), not a TA term.

Structural analysis: Parent, Adult and Child

The analysis of a single personality into its ego states Berne called structural analysis, the first of TA's four levels of analysis. (The other three, in order, are the analysis of transactions between people, the analysis of games and the analysis of life scripts; this section follows the same order.) Figure 3.10 shows Berne's familiar diagram of three stacked circles.

The Parent ego state consists of the behaviours, thoughts and feelings that a person has copied, or taken in whole, from parents and other parent figures (grandparents, elder siblings, teachers), and that the person replays much as the originals performed them. When you wag a finger at a younger cousin exactly as your grandfather wagged his at you, or announce that "in my day students respected their teachers", you are in Parent. Later TA writers divided the Parent by function into two faces. The Controlling Parent (also called the Critical Parent) sets rules and limits, criticises, commands and protects: "Don't touch that", "How many times must I tell you?", "Look both ways before you cross". The Nurturing Parent comforts, cares, helps and gives permission: "You look exhausted, beta; sit, I'll make you some tea". Each face has a helpful and a harmful version. Controlling Parent protects a child from traffic but can also persecute; Nurturing Parent soothes but can also smother, doing for a person what he could do for himself.

The Adult ego state deals with present reality. It gathers information, estimates probabilities and solves problems in the here and now, and Berne compared it to a computer processing data. The Adult is not defined by age (a four-year-old working out how to reach the biscuit tin with a stool is using her Adult) and it is not emotionless; it simply responds to the situation as it actually is rather than as a parent would have seen it or as a frightened child once felt it. You are in Adult when you check the bus timetable, read a laboratory report or calmly weigh two job offers.

The Child ego state consists of the feelings, thoughts and behaviours replayed from the person's own childhood. By function it too has divisions. The Free Child (or Natural Child) is spontaneous, playful, curious, affectionate and self-centred: the part that whoops when India wins a test match, or wants pani puri this minute. The Adapted Child is the part that learned to fit in with the parents' demands, and it comes in two forms: compliant (polite, apologetic, guilty, withdrawn: "Sorry, sir, it won't happen again") and rebellious (sulking, defiant, procrastinating: "Fine, then I won't eat"). Rebellion counts as adaptation because it is still a reaction to the Parent. Finally, Berne gave a name to the shrewd, intuitive, creative thinking of the young child, the part of a five-year-old that works out which parent will say yes and how to ask: the Little Professor. In Berne's second-order diagram the Child is itself divided into three, and the Little Professor is the "Adult in the Child" (labelled A1), sitting between an early Parent in the Child (P1) and the most primitive, bodily Child in the Child (C1).

Healthy functioning, in TA terms, does not mean living in the Adult. It means that all three ego states are available and that a person can move between them as the moment requires, with the Adult acting as an executive that knows which state is in charge. The Free Child provides joy, creativity and intimacy; the Parent provides values, care and the protection of others; the Adult keeps both in touch with reality.

In 1972 Berne's student, the psychiatrist John Dusay, turned the functional model into a picture of a whole personality, the egogram: a bar chart showing how much psychological energy a person puts into each of the five functional ego states (Controlling Parent, Nurturing Parent, Adult, Free Child and Adapted Child). A dutiful, joyless manager might show a tall Controlling Parent bar and a stunted Free Child bar. Dusay's constancy hypothesis held that the total energy stays constant, so that when one bar rises, another must fall.

Two disorders of the boundaries between ego states have clinical importance, and Figure 3.10 shows both. In contamination the Adult mistakes the contents of another ego state for reality. When Parent material intrudes, the result is prejudice held as fact: "People from that community cannot be trusted", "Girls are not good at mathematics". When Child material intrudes, the result is a fear or fantasy held as fact: "Everyone in the canteen is staring at me", "If I fail this exam my life is over", and at the extreme, delusion. A double contamination involves both. Part of TA treatment is decontamination: helping the Adult test the belief against evidence, a procedure close in spirit to the cognitive therapist's questioning of automatic thoughts (Chapter 5).

In exclusion one or two ego states are rigidly shut out, so that a person lives in a single state whatever the situation. The constant Parent excludes Adult and Child: the dutiful, moralising person who cannot play and cannot think freshly about a problem. The constant Adult excludes Parent and Child: dry, efficient, robot-like, without fun or warmth or strong values. The constant Child excludes Parent and Adult: impulsive, charming perhaps, but unable to think ahead or care for anyone. A person who says "I don't remember anything about my childhood" and never plays may have an excluded Child. Table 3.20 gathers the clues by which ego states are recognised in a session.

Table 3.20 Recognising the functional ego states (behavioural diagnosis).
Ego stateTypical wordsVoice and bodyExample
Controlling Parentshould, must, never, alwaysStern, pointing finger, frown"How many times must I tell you?"
Nurturing Parentdon't worry, let me helpSoothing, arm round shoulder"You must be tired; sit down."
Adultwhat, how, probably, I thinkEven tone, level eye contact"The train leaves at 6.10, so we leave by 5.30."
Free Childwow, I want, let'sLaughter, excitement, tears"Let's go for pani puri!"
Adapted Child (compliant)sorry, I'll try, pleaseDowncast eyes, whining"Sorry, sir, it won't happen again."
Adapted Child (rebellious)won't, so what, laterSulking, slammed door"Fine, then I won't eat."
Little Professorwhat if, I know a waySly grin, quick glanceA child asking the softer parent first
Exam trap

Behaviours, thoughts and feelings "copied from parents or parental figures" define the Parent ego state; present-reality information processing defines the Adult; feelings and behaviour "replayed from one's own childhood" define the Child. The Little Professor is the Adult in the Child (A1), not a fourth ego state and not the Adult itself. Controlling (Critical) Parent, Nurturing Parent, Free Child and Adapted Child are TA's functional names; the "modes" with similar names in schema therapy are Jeffrey Young's (Chapter 5), and "topdog" and "underdog" are Fritz Perls's Gestalt terms (Chapter 6). Contamination (an ego state leaking into the Adult) is not exclusion (an ego state walled off). The egogram, a bar chart of the five functional ego states, is John Dusay's (1972), not Berne's.

Transactions and the rules of communication

Once a single personality has been mapped, the next question is what happens when two of these three-part people meet. Berne called the unit of social intercourse a transaction: one person offers a transactional stimulus (a remark, a look, a gesture) from one of his ego states, addressed to one of the other person's ego states, and the other person gives a transactional response from one of hers. Transactional analysis proper, the second level of analysis, means diagramming single transactions by drawing an arrow, which TA calls a vector, from the ego state that speaks to the ego state addressed. Figure 3.11 shows the four basic kinds.

In a complementary transaction the response comes from the ego state that was addressed and goes back to the ego state that sent the stimulus, so the two vectors are parallel. "Do you know where my keys are?" "On the shelf by the door" is a complementary Adult-to-Adult transaction. "You're late again!" (Controlling Parent to Child) answered by "Sorry, it won't happen again" (Adapted Child to Parent) is complementary too, and so is "Let's skip the lecture and watch the match!" "Yes, let's!" (Child to Child). Notice that complementary does not mean pleasant or healthy. Two retired uncles agreeing from their Parents that "the younger generation has no discipline" can keep a complementary exchange going all evening.

In a crossed transaction the response comes from a different ego state from the one addressed, or is aimed at a different ego state from the one that spoke, so the vectors cross on the diagram. A husband asks from his Adult, "Do you know where my keys are?" His wife replies from her Child to his Parent: "Why do you always blame me for everything?" Communication on the original topic stops dead; nobody is now discussing keys. Berne called this commonest form of crossing (Adult stimulus, Child-to-Parent response) "crossed transaction type I", and he pointed out that it is exactly the transference reaction of psychotherapy (Section 3.3): the patient answers the therapist's reasonable question as if it came from a critical parent. The reverse crossing ("type II"), in which an Adult question such as "Do you think I'm improving?" draws a Parent-to-Child reply such as "You must stop worrying so much", he called the countertransference reaction. Crossing is not always bad. A therapist who answers a whining appeal from the Child with a calm Adult question ("What would you like to do about it?") crosses the transaction deliberately, to invite the patient out of an old pattern.

The third kind is the most interesting. An ulterior transaction carries two messages at once: an overt message at the social level, usually Adult to Adult, and a covert message at the psychological level, conveyed by tone, timing, facial expression or context. An angular transaction involves three ego states. Berne's example is a salesman who says, "This model is better, but you probably can't afford it." At the social level it is Adult information; at the psychological level it is a dare aimed at the customer's Child. If the customer's Child answers, "That's the one I'll take", the salesman has made his sale, whatever the customer's Adult would have advised. A duplex transaction involves four ego states, two at each level. "Would you like to come up and see my new paintings?" "I'd love to; I've always been interested in art" is, at the social level, a conversation about art between two Adults, and at the psychological level a flirtation between two Children. Both people know which level counts.

From these diagrams Berne drew three rules of communication, which are among the most examined statements in TA:

  1. As long as transactions remain complementary, communication can proceed indefinitely.
  2. When a transaction is crossed, a break in communication results, and one or both people must shift ego states for communication to be re-established.
  3. The behavioural outcome of an ulterior transaction is determined at the psychological level, not at the social level.

Push a real exchange through the three rules and they come alive. Ravi, 24, is preparing for postgraduate entrance examinations and living at home. At breakfast his father, without looking up from the newspaper, asks, "Have you filled in the IHBAS form yet?" Ravi snaps, "Stop treating me like a child!", and leaves the table. On paper the father's words are an Adult question. But he asked it with a sigh and a raised eyebrow, and it was the third time that week; at the psychological level the message was Controlling Parent to Child: "You are careless, as usual." Ravi responded to the hidden message, from his rebellious Adapted Child, exactly as the third rule predicts. The response crossed the social-level transaction, so, by the second rule, talk about the form stopped. Either party could restore communication by shifting ego state. Ravi could answer the Adult question from his own Adult ("Not yet; I'll do it tonight; the deadline is Friday"), which leaves the father's hidden Parent nothing to push against. Or the father could ask a genuinely Adult question ("Is there anything in the form you need help with?"). In family and couples work TA therapists teach people to diagram their own quarrels in exactly this way, and Table 3.21 summarises the types.

Table 3.21 Kinds of transaction.
TransactionEgo states involvedArrowsWhat follows
ComplementaryTwo (or the same state in both)ParallelTalk can continue indefinitely (rule 1)
CrossedThree or fourCrossBreak in communication until someone shifts (rule 2)
Ulterior, angularThreeSocial plus one hiddenOutcome decided at hidden level (rule 3)
Ulterior, duplexFourTwo social, two hiddenOutcome decided at hidden level (rule 3)
Exam trap

Complementary means the vectors are parallel (the response returns from the ego state addressed), not that the exchange is Adult to Adult or friendly; a scolding answered by an apology is complementary. Crossed means the vectors cross and communication breaks down. Ulterior means a hidden psychological message lies under the social one: angular involves three ego states, duplex four. The rule "the behavioural outcome is determined at the psychological level" belongs to ulterior transactions. Berne linked crossed transaction type I to transference and type II to countertransference.

Strokes and the structuring of time

Why do people keep transacting at all, even when the transactions hurt? Berne's answer began with infants. He opened Games People Play with the work of René Spitz (1887 to 1974), who in 1945 described babies in foundling homes who were fed, clean and medically cared for but seldom handled or played with, and who nevertheless wasted away, physically and emotionally, a condition Spitz called hospitalism. Human beings, Berne concluded, have a biological stimulus hunger, a need for sensation and contact, which in adults becomes recognition hunger, the need to be noticed by others, and a further structure hunger, the need to fill time in a way that brings recognition.

The currency of recognition Berne called the stroke, defined as a unit of recognition: any act that acknowledges another person's presence. A smile, a nod, "Good morning", a pat on the back, a frown and a slap are all strokes. Strokes can be verbal or non-verbal, and positive (pleasant to receive) or negative (painful). They can also be conditional, given for what a person does ("That was a beautiful cover drive"), or unconditional, given for what a person is ("I love you"; at the other extreme, "I wish you had never been born", which is the most destructive stroke of all). The principle that makes strokes clinically important is simple and unsettling: any stroke is better than no stroke. A child who cannot get positive strokes will work hard for negative ones, because a scolding at least proves that somebody noticed. Many adult patterns that look self-defeating, such as provoking criticism again and again, make sense once you see them as ways of collecting the only strokes a person has learned to expect.

The Californian transactional analyst Claude Steiner extended the idea into social criticism. In "The Stroke Economy" (1971) he argued that families and societies teach a set of unwritten rules that keep strokes artificially scarce, so that people remain stroke-hungry and easy to control:

  1. Do not give strokes when you have them to give.
  2. Do not ask for strokes when you need them.
  3. Do not accept strokes when you want them.
  4. Do not reject strokes when you do not want them.
  5. Do not give yourself strokes.

The stroke economy is the name for this enforced scarcity. Many families, in India as elsewhere, withhold praise in the belief that a praised child will become "over-smart", and many adults would be embarrassed to say to a spouse, "I need you to tell me that I did well today." TA groups deliberately break all five rules: members practise giving strokes freely, asking for them, accepting the ones they want, refusing the ones they do not want (such as the backhanded "You're quite clever for a girl") and stroking themselves.

Structure hunger leads to Berne's account of time structuring, the six ways in which people fill time when they are together. They are arranged in Table 3.22 in order of increasing stroke intensity, and also of increasing psychological risk.

Table 3.22 Berne's six ways of structuring time.
WayWhat it isExampleStrokes
WithdrawalPhysically present, mentally elsewhereDaydreaming in a crowded busSelf-strokes only
RitualsFixed, predictable exchanges"Namaste, all well at home?" "All well, by God's grace."Low, safe, expected
PastimesSemi-ritual chat around a topicCricket scores, petrol prices, "children these days"Mild, mostly positive
ActivitiesWork directed at a goalTwo nurses changing a dressingConditional, for doing
GamesRepeated ulterior transactions with a payoff"Why don't you, yes but" (below)Intense, usually negative
IntimacyCandid, game-free sharing of feelings and wants"I was frightened when you didn't call."Most intense, most rewarding

Intimacy in TA has nothing specifically to do with sex. It means a straight, game-free exchange in which people say what they feel and want without hidden agendas, and because it offers no protection from rejection it is the riskiest way of spending time. That is the reason, Berne thought, why so many people settle for games: games give intense strokes while keeping real closeness at a safe distance. The capacity for intimacy was one of the three marks of what Berne called autonomy, the goal of TA, alongside awareness (seeing and hearing the world directly rather than as one was taught to) and spontaneity (free choice among the ego states).

Exam trap

A stroke is Berne's "unit of recognition", not a unit of reinforcement or affection; negative strokes count, and are preferred to none. The stroke economy and its five rules are Claude Steiner's (1971), not Berne's. The six ways of structuring time run withdrawal, rituals, pastimes, activities, games, intimacy. A pastime differs from a game because it has no concealed motive and no painful payoff; "intimacy" means game-free candour. Berne drew his "stimulus hunger" from René Spitz's studies of hospitalised infants.

Games and the drama triangle

Games are what made Berne famous, and they are the most misunderstood part of TA, because the everyday word suggests something light-hearted and deliberate. Berne meant neither. He defined a game as an ongoing series of complementary ulterior transactions progressing to a well-defined, predictable outcome; more colloquially, "a series of moves with a snare, or gimmick". Three features matter. A game is repetitive: the same person plays it again and again with different partners. It has a concealed motive: at the social level something ordinary seems to be going on, while the real business happens at the psychological level. And it ends in a payoff, usually a familiar bad feeling (TA calls these racket feelings) that confirms the player's old beliefs about himself and others. Crucially, games are played outside Adult awareness. A con man who knowingly manipulates is not playing a game in Berne's sense; he is running a manoeuvre.

In his last book Berne summarised the structure of every game in a formula, often called Formula G:

Con + Gimmick = Response → Switch → Cross-up → Payoff

The con is the bait, the opening move with its hidden message. The gimmick is the weak spot in the other player that the con hooks (a need to be helpful, a readiness to feel guilty). The response is the string of apparently ordinary transactions that follows. At the switch one player suddenly changes role, and there is a moment of cross-up, of confusion in which both feel "What just happened?" Then comes the payoff: each walks away with the bad feeling the game was designed to deliver.

The game Berne said had first given him the idea of games is "Why Don't You, Yes But". In his example, a woman tells a group, "My husband always insists on doing our own repairs, and he never builds anything right." Someone asks, "Why doesn't he take a course in carpentry?" "Yes, but he doesn't have time." "Why don't you buy him some good tools?" "Yes, but he doesn't know how to use them." "Why don't you have your building done by a carpenter?" "Yes, but that would cost too much." Every suggestion is met with "yes, but", until the advisers fall silent, irritated and useless. At the social level this is Adult to Adult: a problem and some solutions. At the psychological level it is a Child challenging a row of Parents: "See if you can find a solution I can't fault." The payoff for the player is proof that nobody can tell her anything (and so that she need not change); the payoff for the advisers is the familiar feeling that their help is never good enough. You will recognise the game in any outpatient clinic. The mother of a teenager who will not study is offered advice by everyone, from the psychiatrist to the neighbour, and every idea has a "yes, but". The antithesis Berne recommended was not a better suggestion but no suggestion at all, a reply such as "That's a difficult problem. What are you going to do about it?", a question that addresses her Adult and offers the Child nothing to defeat.

In "Kick Me" the player behaves in ways that invite others to put him down: he arrives late, forgets the assignment, makes the provocative joke at the wrong moment, and then asks plaintively, "Why does this always happen to me?" Berne described the player as wearing an invisible sign that reads "Please don't kick me", which others somehow cannot resist. The payoff is a supply of negative strokes and fresh confirmation that he is not OK. Table 3.23 lists some of the other games most often cited.

Table 3.23 Some of Berne's games.
GameThe movesHidden payoff
Why Don't You, Yes ButAsks for help, rejects every suggestion"No one can help me"; advisers feel useless
Kick MeInvites put-downs, then laments themNegative strokes; "Why does this always happen to me?"
Now I've Got YouWaits for a mistake, then pouncesJustified anger; "You're not OK"
If It Weren't for YouBlames partner for limits one secretly wantsAvoids a feared activity without admitting the fear
Ain't It AwfulCollects and shares misfortunesSympathy without action
Wooden Leg"What do you expect from someone with ...?"Excuse from responsibility
I'm Only Trying to Help YouHelper's advice keeps failing"People are ungrateful"; helper feels unappreciated
UproarEscalating quarrel, slammed doorAvoids intimacy (often sexual)

"If It Weren't for You" is Berne's example of a marital game: a wife complains bitterly that her domineering husband will not let her go out dancing, when in fact she chose him, without knowing it, because he protects her from a fear of social situations she would rather not face. "Wooden Leg" is common in clinics: "What do you expect from someone who comes from a broken home?" or "from someone with depression?" "I'm Only Trying to Help You" is the helping professions' own game, and it fits neatly with "Why Don't You, Yes But". Berne also graded games by their severity. A first-degree game is socially acceptable and could be described at a dinner party. A second-degree game does no permanent damage, but the players would rather keep it hidden. A third-degree game is played "for keeps" and, in Berne's phrase, ends in the surgery, the courtroom or the morgue.

In 1968 Stephen Karpman, a young doctor who attended Berne's San Francisco seminars, published "Fairy Tales and Script Drama Analysis" in the Transactional Analysis Bulletin. Using fairy tales such as Little Red Riding Hood, he showed that every drama, on the stage or in a family, needs only three roles: the Persecutor, the Rescuer and the Victim. The figure he drew, the drama triangle (Figure 3.12), became one of the most widely used diagrams in all of counselling. Karpman's insight was that the drama lies in the switch: the moment when players change corners. This is the switch of Formula G. In "Why Don't You, Yes But" the player begins as a Victim ("Poor me, my husband builds nothing right") and the advisers line up as Rescuers. With each "yes, but" the player moves into the Persecutor's corner, defeating the Rescuers, who end as Victims, feeling useless, or turn Persecutor themselves: "You obviously don't want to be helped!"

Notice that the three roles are not the same as real social positions. A real victim of a crime is not necessarily in the Victim role, and a real doctor helping a patient is not necessarily a Rescuer. In TA terms a Rescuer is someone who helps without being asked, does more than half the work and needs the other person to stay helpless; a Victim invites rescue or persecution by acting more helpless than he is; a Persecutor puts others down from a position of righteous superiority. Helping professionals are especially prone to the Rescuer corner, and you have met this danger before as the "wish to rescue" of positive countertransference (Table 3.4). Later TA writers proposed an antidote. In 1990 Acey Choy described a "winner's triangle" in which the Victim becomes vulnerable (owning a real problem and asking straightforwardly for help), the Rescuer becomes caring (helping only when asked, and no more than is needed) and the Persecutor becomes assertive (stating needs and limits without punishing).

Clinical link

A trainee counsellor at a college counselling centre in Delhi finds that her sessions with one student leave her exhausted. Each week he describes his failing grades, she suggests a study timetable, a peer group, a meeting with his professor, and he explains why none of them will work. In supervision she diagrams the sessions and recognises "Why Don't You, Yes But", with herself in the Rescuer corner and her irritation as the switch approaching. The next week she declines the role: "You've thought about this a great deal, and none of the usual answers fits. What do you want to do?" The student is silent, then angry, then, for the first time, talks about how frightened he is of disappointing his father. Stepping off the triangle has made room for intimacy in Berne's sense.

Exam trap

The drama triangle is Stephen Karpman's (1968), not Berne's, and its roles are Persecutor, Rescuer and Victim (not Parent, Adult and Child). The "winner's triangle" is Acey Choy's (1990). "Why Don't You, Yes But" means rejecting every suggestion offered; "Kick Me" means inviting put-downs; "Now I've Got You" means pouncing on a mistake. A game is played outside Adult awareness and ends in a payoff of bad feelings, which separates it from a pastime (no payoff) and from deliberate manipulation. Berne's games have nothing to do with mathematical game theory.

Scripts, injunctions and drivers

If games are the scenes, the script is the whole play. Berne defined a life script as a life plan made in childhood, reinforced by the parents, justified by subsequent events, and culminating in a chosen alternative. Every phrase of the definition matters. The plan is made by the child, early, on the basis of a child's limited understanding. The parents keep reinforcing it. Later events seem to justify it, partly because the person selects and provokes the events that confirm it (the games of the last subsection are the means). And it heads towards an ending that the child, in a sense, chose. Berne liked to compare scripts to myths and fairy tales: someone with a "Never" script is like Tantalus, standing in water he can never drink, forever wanting what he never allows himself to have; someone with an "Almost" script is like Sisyphus, who pushes his rock almost to the top of the hill before it rolls down again. By outcome, Berne divided scripts into those of winners (who achieve what they set out to do), losers and non-winners (who neither win nor lose badly, but plod through what Steiner called a "banal" script).

How are scripts transmitted? Claude Steiner's script matrix (1966) answered with a diagram of two parents and a child, each drawn as three ego states, and arrows showing which parental ego state sends which kind of message to which ego state of the child:

  • Counterinjunctions pass from the parent's Parent to the child's Parent. They are the spoken rules and slogans of the family: "Work hard", "Be a good girl", "Big boys don't cry", "Think of the family first". They are mostly conscious and often admirable.
  • The programme passes from the parent's Adult to the child's Adult. It is the "here's how" of the script, taught mostly by example: here is how to hide your feelings, here is how to work until you collapse.
  • Injunctions pass from the parent's Child to the child's Child. They are the most powerful messages and mostly unspoken, conveyed through the parent's face, tone, absence or neglect, and born of the parent's own fears, anger and unmet needs. A depressed mother who cannot bear her baby's noise may convey "Don't feel" or "Don't exist" without ever saying a word.

The best-known list of injunctions comes from the husband-and-wife team Robert Goulding and Mary Goulding, who in Changing Lives Through Redecision Therapy (1979) set out twelve: Don't Be (Don't Exist), Don't Be You (for example, "don't be the sex you are"), Don't Be a Child, Don't Grow Up, Don't Make It (Don't Succeed), Don't (do anything), Don't Be Important, Don't Belong, Don't Be Close, Don't Be Well (or Don't Be Sane), Don't Think and Don't Feel. The Gouldings added a crucial point. Injunctions do not write the script by themselves; the child decides how to respond to them. One child who receives "Don't Be Close" decides "I'll never trust anyone again"; a sibling who receives the same injunction decides "I'll make people need me so they can't leave". Because the script rests on a decision, the adult can make a new one, and that idea gave their therapy its name.

Counterinjunctions often take the form of what Taibi Kahler (1975) called drivers, five compulsive messages that can be seen in behaviour within seconds: Be Perfect (over-precise speech, endless qualifications), Be Strong (a flat face and voice, no admission of need), Try Hard (trying rather than doing, "I'll try to finish it"), Please Others (eager nodding, questions in a rising voice) and Hurry Up (fidgeting, interrupting, rushing). Each driver carries a hidden condition: I am OK only if I am perfect, strong, trying, pleasing or fast.

Take Kavya, 27, a software engineer and the eldest of three daughters, who comes to a Bengaluru clinic with exhaustion, headaches and crying spells. Her script analysis shows a counterinjunction ("Think of the family first; a good daughter never complains") and the drivers Please Others and Be Strong. Beneath them lies an injunction she can trace to the years when her mother was overwhelmed by illness and her father by debts: "Don't Be a Child", with the early decision, "My needs don't matter; I will look after everyone." Her adult life confirms the decision daily: she takes on her colleagues' work, sends half her salary home, never asks for help and plays a quiet game of "Look How Hard I've Tried". Therapy in TA terms would help her Adult see the script (decontamination), offer her the permission her Child never received ("You can have needs, and you can ask"), and help her make a new decision.

Exam trap

"Injunctions, counterinjunctions and games" are core components of transactional analysis, not of Gestalt therapy, REBT or person-centred therapy. Gestalt therapy (Fritz Perls, Chapter 6) speaks of unfinished business, topdog and underdog and the empty chair; REBT (Albert Ellis, Chapter 5) speaks of irrational beliefs, "musts" and the ABC model; person-centred therapy (Carl Rogers, Chapter 6) speaks of conditions of worth, which are close in spirit to injunctions but are Rogers's term. The trap within the trap: the Gouldings' redecision therapy borrowed Gestalt techniques, but the injunction concept is TA's. The script matrix is Steiner's (1966); the twelve injunctions are the Gouldings'; the five drivers are Kahler's.

The four life positions

Underneath the script lies something simpler still: the child's basic verdict on the worth of himself and of other people. Berne called this a life position and described four of them in a short paper, "Classification of Positions", in the Transactional Analysis Bulletin in 1962, using them again in Games People Play (1964). The positions are usually written in TA shorthand, with "I" for oneself, "U" for you (others), plus for OK and minus for not OK.

  • I'm OK, you're OK (I+U+): the healthy position, in which a person values both himself and others and expects problems to be solvable. It is the position from which intimacy and genuine cooperation are possible, and the goal of TA therapy.
