  • I'm OK, you're not OK (I+U-): the position of superiority and blame, which Berne called the paranoid or projective position. Others are seen as stupid, untrustworthy or bad; the person gets rid of them, attacks them or keeps them at a distance.
  • I'm not OK, you're OK (I-U+): the depressive position of inferiority, in which others are seen as more capable and the person withdraws, defers or seeks rescue.
  • I'm not OK, you're not OK (I-U-): the futile position, in which nothing and nobody is worth anything. At its extreme Berne linked it to schizoid withdrawal and to despair that can become suicidal or homicidal.

The positions became famous through someone else. Thomas Anthony Harris (1910 to 1995), a psychiatrist and colleague of Berne's, wrote I'm OK, You're OK (1967), a practical guide to TA that became one of the best-selling self-help books ever published. Harris added a claim of his own that Berne did not share. Every infant, Harris argued, begins life in the position I'm not OK, you're OK, because the small child is helpless and dependent while the big people around him seem all-powerful; the healthy position is not given but must be reached later, by a conscious decision of the Adult. Berne and most later TA writers took the opposite view, that people begin life OK and are taught otherwise. Berne liked to say that people are born princes and princesses, until their parents turn them into frogs.

In 1971 the transactional analyst Franklin Ernst placed the four positions on a two-by-two grid, which he called the OK Corral, and linked each to an "operation" that a person in that position habitually uses (Table 3.24). Ernst's grid makes one point clearer than Berne's list: the positions are not fixed types. Most people move between them during a day, and therapy aims to increase the time spent in I'm OK, you're OK. Each person, though, has a favourite position under stress, and the payoffs of the person's games keep confirming it. "Kick Me" ends with I'm not OK; "Now I've Got You" ends with you're not OK.

Table 3.24 The four life positions and Ernst's OK Corral (1971).
PositionShorthandErnst's operationTypical stance
I'm OK, you're OKI+U+Get on withHealthy; problems are solvable together
I'm OK, you're not OKI+U-Get rid ofSuperior, blaming, suspicious (paranoid)
I'm not OK, you're OKI-U+Get away fromInferior, withdrawing, dependent (depressive)
I'm not OK, you're not OKI-U-Get nowhere withHopeless, futile (schizoid)
Exam trap

Who gave the four life positions? Eric Berne first described them (1962) and used them in Games People Play (1964); Thomas Harris popularised them in I'm OK, You're OK (1967), which is a book title, not a fifth position. Harris, not Berne, claimed that every child starts at "I'm not OK, you're OK". The OK Corral grid and the operations "get on with, get rid of, get away from, get nowhere with" are Franklin Ernst's (1971). If a question offers only Berne and Harris for "life positions", choose Berne for the concept and Harris for the book.

Transactional analysis as a therapy: contracts, redecision and evidence

TA's distinctive tool in the consulting room is the treatment contract, which Berne defined as an explicit bilateral commitment to a well-defined course of action. The contract is made Adult to Adult at the start of therapy and states, in observable terms, what the client wants to change and what each party will do. "I want to be more confident" is a wish, not a contract; "I will speak at least once in every team meeting, and I will stop apologising before I give my opinion" is a contract, because both client and therapist will know when it has been met. Berne distinguished the administrative level of a contract (fees, times, place), the professional level (the agreed goal of treatment) and the psychological level (what is really going on between the two, which may include an invitation to play a game). Steiner added four requirements borrowed from the law of contracts: mutual consent, a valid consideration (each party gives something), competence to make the agreement, and a lawful object. The contract method puts I'm OK, you're OK into practice, since it treats the client as a responsible partner rather than a patient to be cured, and it anticipated the collaborative goal setting of cognitive-behavioural therapy (Chapter 5) and the treatment contract of Kernberg's transference-focused psychotherapy (Section 3.4).

Classical TA treatment then moves through Berne's four levels. The client learns to recognise his own ego states (structural analysis) and to free the Adult from contamination; examines his transactions, often in a group, which was Berne's preferred setting; identifies his games, their cons and switches, and stops playing them; and finally uncovers the script and the early decisions beneath it. In 1966, in a short paper titled "Permission and Protection" in the Transactional Analysis Bulletin, Pat Crossman named two things the therapist must provide if a client is to defy an old injunction: permission (an explicit message such as "You can think for yourself" or "You can enjoy your success") and protection (enough support that the client's Child feels safe in disobeying the parental message). TA trainers usually add a third, potency: the therapist's permission must be felt as strong enough to outweigh the parent's.

After Berne's death TA divided into schools. The classical school kept Berne's emphasis on analysis and contracts. The cathexis school of Jacqui Lee Schiff worked with severely disturbed young adults using "reparenting", in which clients regressed and were parented again by therapists; its methods became highly controversial. The most influential was the redecision therapy of the Gouldings, who combined TA's analysis of scripts with the experiential techniques of Gestalt therapy (Chapter 6). In a redecision session the client returns in imagination to an early scene in which a script decision was made, re-experiences it in the present tense, often speaking to the parent in an empty chair, and then, from the Free Child and with the therapist's support, makes a new decision: "I'm going to live", "I'll think for myself", "I can be close". The Gouldings stressed that the power to change lies with the client, who made the original decision and can make a new one; the therapist provides the setting, not the cure.

How good is the evidence? TA's great strength is its accessibility: its concepts can be taught in an afternoon, used by clients on themselves, and applied to couples, families, classrooms, hospital teams and companies, which is why it has spread so widely through counselling and management training, in India as elsewhere. Its weaknesses are the other side of the same coin. Ego states, games and scripts were derived from clinical observation and have rarely been defined precisely enough to be measured, so the theory is hard to test; controlled trials of TA as a psychotherapy are few, small and mostly old, and it has nothing like the evidence base of cognitive-behavioural or even psychodynamic therapy (Section 3.11). Popularisation brought a further risk, that the vocabulary is turned into a weapon ("You're in your Child again!") or into a substitute for change. TA's lasting contributions are probably its diagrams (the transaction diagram and the drama triangle are used by therapists of every school), the contract method, and the insistence that a patient's own Adult can understand and use the therapist's theory.

Exam trap

Berne's contract is "an explicit bilateral commitment to a well-defined course of action", made Adult to Adult. Redecision therapy is Robert and Mary Goulding's integration of TA and Gestalt; reparenting belongs to Jacqui Lee Schiff's cathexis school. Berne's goal of therapy is autonomy (awareness, spontaneity, intimacy), not mere insight. Transactional analysis is also not cognitive analytic therapy (Anthony Ryle's integration of cognitive and object relations ideas, Chapter 5), even though both use diagrams of roles.

3.10 Supportive Psychotherapy, Hypnotherapy and NarcoanalysisLower yield · 1 Q

Every method so far in this chapter asks something of the patient: the capacity to look inward, to bear the anxiety that an interpretation stirs up, to attend sessions for months or years, or at least to learn a theory about oneself. Many patients cannot give these things, at least not now. Think of the woman whose husband died last week, the young man recovering from his third episode of schizophrenia, or the farmer who has travelled four hours by bus to a district hospital and will see the doctor for ten minutes. For them clinicians fall back on the oldest psychological methods of all, methods that work less by insight than by influence: support, suggestion and, for a few decades of the twentieth century, drugs that loosen the tongue. This section explains when each is used and where each stops working. The history of hypnosis, from Mesmer to Freud's abandonment of it, was told in Section 3.1; here you meet hypnosis as it is practised today.

Supportive psychotherapy: aims and indications

Support is part of every therapy: the regular appointment, the attentive and reliable therapist, the working alliance of Section 3.3. Supportive psychotherapy is what results when these features are moved to the foreground and become the treatment itself, while interpretation recedes. The most quoted definition comes from the American psychiatrists Henry Pinsker and Richard Rosenthal (1988), refined in Arnold Winston, Rosenthal and Pinsker's Introduction to Supportive Psychotherapy (2004): a dyadic treatment characterised by the use of direct measures to ameliorate symptoms and to maintain, restore or improve self-esteem, ego function and adaptive skills. Each phrase is worth unpacking. Dyadic means it takes place between two people. Direct measures means the therapist acts openly, reassuring, advising, praising and helping, rather than interpreting and waiting for insight. And the aims (to maintain, restore or improve) are deliberately modest: the patient's existing strengths are the material, and no reconstruction of personality is attempted.

For most of the psychoanalytic era supportive therapy was what analysts offered, almost apologetically, to patients judged unsuitable for analysis. The American psychiatrist Lewis Wolberg gave it a recognised place in The Technique of Psychotherapy (1954), where he sorted all psychotherapies into three classes by their goals. Supportive therapy aims to restore the patient's equilibrium by strengthening existing defences and relieving symptoms, using guidance, environmental manipulation, reassurance, persuasion, suggestion and similar measures. Re-educative therapy aims to change attitudes and behaviour through a new understanding of current patterns, without digging into the unconscious; counselling, many group methods and the behaviour therapies fall here. Reconstructive therapy aims to reorganise the personality through insight into unconscious conflict; psychoanalysis and its variants are the examples. Wolberg's three classes are still a useful way to place any treatment you meet.

Supportive psychotherapy has, then, a characteristic set of aims. For a previously well-functioning person overwhelmed by stress, the aim is restoration: return to the previous level of functioning. For a person with a chronic, disabling condition, the aim is maintenance: the best possible adaptation, the prevention of relapse and decompensation, a treatment that is palliative rather than radical. In both cases the therapist works to relieve symptoms and anxiety, raise self-esteem, strengthen adaptive defences and coping skills, and keep the patient functioning in work and relationships. The technical stance follows from these aims. Defences are strengthened, not interpreted: a frightened patient's useful denial or intellectualisation is left in place. The transference is kept positive and is used, not analysed; the trusting, mildly idealising attachment that Section 3.3 called the unobjectionable positive transference is the engine of the treatment. The focus is on conscious, present problems. And the therapist behaves as a real person: answers questions, gives opinions, speaks conversationally and, with care, discloses a little of herself.

Who is it for? The Indian Psychiatric Society's clinical practice guidelines for supportive psychotherapy (Sandeep Grover, Ajit Avasthi and Mukesh Jagiwala, 2020) list the main indications, which line up with the assessment of ego strength you met in Section 3.4:

  • People overwhelmed by stressful circumstances such as bereavement, divorce, job loss, menopause, physical illness or academic difficulties. People in acute crisis belong here too, and for them supportive measures are the first response (crisis intervention is taught in Chapter 9).
  • People with severely impaired ego strength, such as those with chronic schizophrenia, chronic and severe mood disorders or severe personality disorders, for whom the goal is the best possible adaptation.
  • People who lack curiosity about themselves or the capacity for self-directed psychological work, but want their symptoms to change.
  • People for whom other therapies are not feasible because of cost, distance, the frequency of visits required or a shortage of trained therapists.
  • People in insight-oriented therapy whose anxiety temporarily exceeds their capacity to cope, for whom a supportive phase is a temporary expedient.
  • People who need their ego strength built up first, for whom supportive work is an ego-building measure that prepares them to commit to more searching psychotherapy later.

The same guidelines stress that supportive psychotherapy can be used across diagnoses and age groups, in outpatient clinics, wards, emergency departments and consultation-liaison work with the medically ill, and that cognitive deficits and low psychological sophistication are not contraindications, although they shape how the therapist works.

Exam trap

"Which therapy is mostly needed in crisis management?" Supportive therapy (IHBAS 2020): ventilation, realistic reassurance, guidance and environmental manipulation come first in a crisis, before CBT, acceptance and commitment therapy or expressive therapies. But do not choose supportive therapy by reflex. For "a male adult with depressive disorder and marital discord" (IHBAS 2020) the keyed answer is marital and couple therapy, because the formulation points to the marriage as the main maintaining factor; and "the treatment of choice for adjustment disorder" (RML 2024) is crisis intervention (Chapter 9). Supportive psychotherapy has no single founder. Indian lists that pair "supportive psychotherapy" with Franz Alexander (1891 to 1964) are oversimplifying: Alexander is the analyst of the "corrective emotional experience" and of flexible, shortened psychoanalytic therapy; if a list forces one name, it probably wants Alexander. Wolberg (1954) classified supportive, re-educative and reconstructive therapy, and Pinsker, Rosenthal and Winston wrote the modern definition and manuals.

The techniques of support

The techniques of supportive psychotherapy sound like common sense, and in a sense they are. What makes them therapy is that they are used deliberately, in the right order and at the right moment, by someone who has assessed the patient and knows what each technique can and cannot do. Table 3.25 lists the classic set, most of which go back to Wolberg's list.

Table 3.25 The techniques of supportive psychotherapy.
TechniqueWhat the therapist doesExample
VentilationInvites the patient to talk out pent-up feelings and worries, and listens without judging"Tell me what these weeks since the accident have been like."
ReassuranceRemoves unfounded fears with honest, specific, fact-based statements"Your heart tests are normal; these are panic attacks, frightening but not dangerous."
GuidanceGives information and advice on practical matters"Take the tablets after food and don't stop them on your own."
SuggestionPlants an expectation that the patient accepts on the therapist's authority"You will find your sleep improving within a week."
PersuasionAppeals to reason and will with facts and argument"Look at the evidence: you have passed every exam you prepared for."
Environmental manipulationChanges the patient's circumstances to reduce stress or add supportFamily meeting; letter to the college asking for leave
Externalisation of interestsEncourages activities and contact that pull the patient out of withdrawalRejoining the temple choir or a women's self-help group
Praise and encouragementReinforces healthy efforts, in terms of the patient's own values"Coming every month, despite the journey, shows how much you want to get well."
Strengthening defencesLeaves useful defences in place instead of interpreting themAllowing a frightened patient's hopeful denial before surgery
Psychoeducation and problem solvingExplains the illness; rehearses how to handle coming difficultiesPlanning the first day back at work

Ventilation (sometimes called confession) means encouraging the patient to pour out feelings, worries and guilt to a listener who neither judges nor hurries. It relieves tension, lets the therapist hear and correct misconceptions, and often makes a problem feel smaller once it has been spoken aloud. It is a cousin of Breuer's catharsis (Section 3.1) but a tamer one: there is no hypnosis and no hunt for repressed memories, only the talking out of feelings about present events. It has limits. Unrestricted ventilation can disorganise a very disturbed or psychotic patient, and with some patients it turns into repetitive rumination, so the therapist gently gives it a shape and an end.

Reassurance, the restoration of confidence by removing unfounded doubts and fears, is the most used and most misused technique in medicine. Effective reassurance is realistic and specific, it rests on the facts of the case, and it comes after the patient's fears have been heard and understood. Premature reassurance ("Don't worry, everything will be fine"), offered before the therapist knows what the worry is, tells the patient that his fears are unwelcome and closes the conversation; false reassurance destroys trust the moment it is proved wrong. And in health anxiety and obsessive-compulsive disorder, repeated reassurance-seeking is part of the problem, because each reassurance brings brief relief that strengthens the urge to seek more (the behavioural logic is in Chapter 4). The helping skills behind good listening and reassurance are taught in Section 6.10.

Guidance is the giving of information and advice on practical matters: medication, work, study, money, relationships. It helps a patient who is too anxious or too impaired to think a problem through to find a realistic course of action. Its dangers are that the therapist's own values slip in under cover of advice (the opposite of the analyst's neutrality in Section 3.2), that advice outside the therapist's competence is given with false authority, and that the patient learns to bring every decision to the clinic.

Suggestion is the planting of an idea, belief or expectation that the patient accepts without critical examination, because of his trust in the therapist. When the therapist deliberately uses the full weight of professional status, the technique is called prestige suggestion. Suggestion is the mechanism the Nancy school championed (Section 3.1); it is part of every placebo response, and it can be given in the waking state or under hypnosis (below). It works best when a symptom serves little defensive purpose. Persuasion, by contrast, appeals to the patient's reason and will, using facts and argument to talk him into more realistic attitudes. Its best-known champion was the Swiss neurologist Paul Dubois (1848 to 1918) of Bern, whose Les psychonévroses et leur traitement moral (1904) argued that nervous symptoms rest on mistaken ideas, which a patient can be argued out of in calm, rational conversation; his "persuasion" or "rational therapy" competed with psychoanalysis in the early twentieth century and is a distant ancestor of the cognitive therapies (Chapter 5). The difference is easy to remember: suggestion bypasses the patient's critical judgement, persuasion engages it.

Environmental manipulation means changing the patient's circumstances, rather than the patient, so as to reduce stress or increase support: a family meeting, a letter to an employer or a college, arranging leave or a change of hostel room, day care, admission to hospital, or linking the family with a social worker, a welfare scheme or a self-help group. Externalisation of interests means encouraging the patient to resume old activities or take up new ones, so that attention turns outwards and withdrawal and brooding are interrupted; occupational and recreational therapies are its formal versions. Praise and encouragement, psychoeducation, problem solving, anticipatory guidance (rehearsing how to cope with a predictable difficulty) and, in emergencies only, firm limit setting complete the repertoire.

See how the techniques fit together in a single short contact. Lakshmamma, 52, from a village near Mysuru, has been attending the psychiatry outpatient department of a district hospital for two months with depression that began after her husband's stroke left him bedridden. The clinician has fifteen minutes. She begins by letting Lakshmamma talk about the past month, the sleepless nights and her anger at relatives who never visit (ventilation). She notes, and says, that sleep and appetite are better than at the first visit, and explains that this kind of depression usually goes on improving with treatment (realistic reassurance and psychoeducation). She checks how the tablets are being taken and explains why they must not be stopped when Lakshmamma feels better (guidance). With Lakshmamma's permission she calls in the son who has brought her, and they agree that he will take over the husband's care on two evenings a week, and that the hospital's social worker will help the family arrange physiotherapy (environmental manipulation). She asks about the women's self-help group Lakshmamma used to attend and encourages her to go once before the next visit (externalisation of interests). She ends by telling Lakshmamma that coming regularly, despite the journey and the cost, shows real determination (praise). No interpretation has been offered, no childhood has been explored, and the treatment has been delivered.

Exam trap

Ventilation is the supportive talking out of current feelings; abreaction is reliving a repressed memory with its emotion; catharsis is the emotional release itself (Section 3.1). Suggestion works through the therapist's authority and bypasses critical judgement (Bernheim, the Nancy school); persuasion works through reason (Paul Dubois). Supportive therapy strengthens defences and keeps the positive transference unexplored; interpreting defences and transference belongs at the expressive end. Reassurance must be realistic and specific; premature or false reassurance is an error, and in health anxiety and OCD reassurance-seeking maintains the problem.

The expressive-supportive continuum

At the opposite pole from supportive therapy stands expressive psychotherapy (also called exploratory or insight-oriented psychotherapy): psychodynamic therapy that aims at insight into unconscious conflict, interprets defences and transference, tolerates and even provokes some anxiety in the service of understanding, and hopes for lasting change in the structure of the personality. Classical psychoanalysis lies at the far expressive end. But modern psychodynamic clinicians, following writers such as Glen Gabbard, do not treat supportive and expressive therapy as two separate boxes. They speak of an expressive-supportive continuum, a single line along which every intervention, every session and every treatment can be placed. Gabbard arranges the therapist's interventions along it, from the most expressive to the most supportive, roughly as in Table 3.26, which follows the classic version of his list. In his later books Gabbard also places observation (pointing out a pattern without trying to explain it) just after interpretation, and psychoeducation towards the supportive end, next to advice and praise.

Table 3.26 Interventions along the expressive-supportive continuum (after Gabbard).
InterventionWhat it doesExample
Interpretation (most expressive)Links feeling or behaviour to an unconscious meaning or the past"Perhaps it feels safer to be angry with me than to feel how frightened you are of losing him."
ConfrontationPoints to something the patient avoids or does not notice"You say you don't mind, but your fists are clenched."
ClarificationSharpens or reformulates what the patient has said"So the panic began after your manager's remark, not before?"
Encouragement to elaborateInvites the patient to say more"Tell me more about that evening."
Empathic validationConveys that the feeling is understandable"Anyone in your place would feel let down."
Advice and praiseRecommends a course of action; reinforces a healthy one"I think you should take the leave. Asking for help was a good step."
Affirmation (most supportive)Briefly endorses the patient's statement or action"Yes, that makes sense."

Where a patient should be placed on the line depends mainly on the ego strength you met in Section 3.4. Patients with good reality testing, a capacity to tolerate anxiety and frustration, mature defences, reasonably good relationships, psychological mindedness and a wish to understand themselves can use expressive work. Patients whose functioning is fragile, who are in crisis, or whose reality testing is shaky, need the supportive end, at least for now. Most patients sit in the middle, and most real treatments are mixtures (Lester Luborsky's manualised "supportive-expressive" psychotherapy, Section 3.6, is named for exactly this blend). The position also shifts within a single treatment and even a single session: a therapist working expressively with a capable patient moves towards support on the day the patient arrives shattered by a bereavement, and moves back when the patient has steadied.

The classic evidence for the continuum comes from the Menninger Psychotherapy Research Project in Topeka, Kansas, in which Robert Wallerstein's team followed 42 patients through psychoanalysis or psychoanalytic psychotherapy; it is told as a Key study in Section 3.11. Supportive elements turned up in every kind of treatment, the analyses included, did far more of the work than anyone had predicted, and produced changes often as stable as those reached through insight, so the neat hierarchy of "real change by interpretation, mere patching by support" blurred.

Later research has kept pointing the same way. In the borderline trial of Clarkin and colleagues (Section 3.4), the supportive psychodynamic condition, intended as a comparison, did surprisingly well. In a meta-analysis of 31 trials in adult depression, Pim Cuijpers and colleagues (2012) found that non-directive supportive therapy was clearly better than no treatment and only slightly less effective than other psychotherapies, and that even this small gap disappeared once the researchers' allegiance to the rival therapy was taken into account. The fact that supportive therapy is so often used as the "placebo" condition in psychotherapy trials, and so often performs respectably, is one of the strongest arguments for the common factors you will meet in Chapter 8.

Supportive therapy has real limits, all of them the shadow side of its strengths. Because the therapist gratifies the patient's need for help instead of analysing it, the patient may become dependent, and ending treatment can be hard; some chronically ill patients reasonably need support for years, but the therapist should know whether dependence is a choice or a drift. Advice may carry the therapist's own values into the patient's life. Reassurance may be premature or false. A negative transference that is never acknowledged can build up until it erupts in missed appointments or an abrupt dropout. Without clear goals, "support" can drift into pleasant, aimless conversation, or collude with avoidance by soothing a patient who needs to face something. And because it does not aim at structural change, supportive therapy alone will not alter a lifelong pattern that a capable patient wants to change; for such a patient it can feel patronising.

Exam trap

"Expressive therapy" has two meanings, and both appear in option lists. In psychodynamic writing it means the exploratory, insight-oriented end of the expressive-supportive continuum (interpreting defences and transference). In other contexts it means the expressive arts therapies (art, music, dance and movement, drama; Chapter 9). In either sense it is not the first response to an acute crisis; supportive therapy is. On the continuum, interpretation is the most expressive intervention and affirmation the most supportive; clarification and confrontation lie in between. Patient selection depends on ego strength: the weaker the ego, the more supportive the approach.

Support in the Indian clinic

Section 3.2 raised a question that Indian clinicians have argued over for decades: how well does a long, individual, insight-oriented therapy suit patients whose lives are embedded in joint families and whose treatment takes place in crowded public hospitals? The practical answer, in most government hospitals, is that the psychological help most patients actually receive is supportive. The reasons are not hard to see. Outpatient departments are very large and consultations short; many patients travel long distances, cannot afford frequent visits and may have little formal education; families usually come along, and decisions about treatment are often family decisions. In that setting a psychiatrist, clinical psychologist, psychiatric social worker or nurse who combines medication with ventilation, reassurance, guidance, family involvement and environmental manipulation, as in the case of Lakshmamma, is delivering an evidence-informed psychological treatment, not a second-best substitute for one.

Indian psychiatrists also argued that the expectations patients bring differ from those the Western model assumes. In an influential paper of 1973 the Indian psychiatrist Jaswant Singh Neki (1925 to 2015) proposed the guru-chela (teacher-disciple) relationship as a therapeutic paradigm suited to Indian culture. Many Indian patients, Neki observed, expect the healer to be an active, caring guide rather than a neutral, egalitarian mirror, and the guru-chela bond offers a culturally familiar model: the disciple accepts guidance and a phase of dependence, and the guru, through discipline and care, leads him gradually towards independence. Dependence, on this view, is a stage to be grown through, not a pathology to be interpreted away. Later Indian commentators welcomed the cultural fit and warned of its danger, that the paradigm can become an excuse for paternalism. Both points describe supportive therapy well.

Indian context

The Indian Psychiatric Society's clinical practice guidelines on supportive psychotherapy (Grover, Avasthi and Jagiwala, 2020) recommend it in outpatient, inpatient, emergency and consultation-liaison settings, and note that it fits situations in which resources, cost or the frequency of visits rule out more intensive therapy. Educated urban clients increasingly seek, and benefit from, expressive and structured therapies, so the Indian answer to the question of Section 3.2 is not "insight therapy does not suit Indians" but "match the position on the expressive-supportive continuum to the patient, the family and the setting".

Hypnosis and hypnotherapy today

Section 3.1 left hypnosis at the moment Freud gave it up, in the 1890s, because many patients could not be hypnotised, the cures did not last and the method went round resistance instead of through it. Hypnosis did not disappear. It survived in medicine, dentistry and psychology, attracted a good deal of laboratory research in the twentieth century, and is now defined and studied with some precision. In 2014 the American Psychological Association's Division of Psychological Hypnosis (Division 30) agreed a set of definitions, published by Gary Elkins and colleagues in 2015, that are worth learning word for word:

  • Hypnosis: a state of consciousness involving focused attention and reduced peripheral awareness, characterised by an enhanced capacity for response to suggestion.
  • Hypnotic induction: a procedure designed to induce hypnosis.
  • Hypnotisability: an individual's ability to experience suggested alterations in physiology, sensations, emotions, thoughts or behaviour during hypnosis.
  • Hypnotherapy: the use of hypnosis in the treatment of a medical or psychological disorder or concern.

The last definition contains an important point. Hypnosis is not itself a therapy; it is a procedure that can be added to a therapy. A clinician uses hypnosis within cognitive-behavioural, psychodynamic or medical treatment, to deepen relaxation, sharpen imagery, alter the experience of pain or strengthen a suggestion. Someone who has learned to hypnotise but has no training in diagnosis or psychotherapy is not qualified to treat disorders with it, a point worth making in India, where "hypnotherapy" is widely advertised by people with no clinical training at all.

A clinical session usually runs through recognisable stages. In preparation the clinician explains what hypnosis is and is not, corrects myths and agrees the goal. The induction follows: the patient may fix the eyes on a point until the lids grow heavy, attend to slow breathing, relax the body muscle group by muscle group, or follow the therapist's count downwards. Deepening procedures, such as imagining going down a staircase, intensify absorption. Then come the therapeutic suggestions: direct ("your hand is becoming numb, as if wrapped in snow") or, in the indirect, permissive style associated with the American psychiatrist Milton Erickson (1901 to 1980), indirect ("you may begin to notice a pleasant coolness, perhaps in one hand, perhaps in both"). Erickson's use of metaphor and of whatever the patient brings to the session strongly influenced the brief therapies (Chapter 6). A post-hypnotic suggestion is a suggestion to be carried out after the session, usually in response to a cue: "Whenever you feel the urge to smoke, you will take three slow breaths and notice the urge passing." Many sessions add ego-strengthening suggestions of confidence and calm. The session ends with re-alerting, and patients are usually taught self-hypnosis so that they can use the method at home.

People differ enormously in hypnotisability, and the differences are remarkably stable. Roughly 10 to 15 per cent of adults are highly hypnotisable, a similar proportion respond very little, and most fall in between. When Carol Piccione, Ernest Hilgard and Philip Zimbardo (1989) retested people whose hypnotisability had been measured 25 years earlier, the correlation between the two scores was about 0.7, as stable as many measures of intelligence. Hypnotisability is measured by standardised scales in which a hypnotic induction is followed by a graded series of suggestions, from easy ones (eyes closing, an arm feeling heavy) to hard ones (hallucinating a voice, failing to see an object that is present, forgetting the session afterwards), with the score being the number passed. The classic scales are the individually administered Stanford Hypnotic Susceptibility Scales (Forms A and B by André Weitzenhoffer and Ernest Hilgard, 1959; Form C, 1962), the Harvard Group Scale of Hypnotic Susceptibility (Ronald Shor and Emily Orne, 1962) and the brief clinical Hypnotic Induction Profile of Herbert Spiegel. (The scales themselves belong to the Assessment book.) Hypnotisability is not gullibility, weakness of will or a wish to please; it correlates instead with absorption, the capacity to become deeply immersed in imagination, music, reading or nature. It matters clinically because, for some uses such as pain relief, highly hypnotisable patients benefit most.

What is hypnosis? Two families of theory have argued for decades. State theories hold that hypnosis is a special state of consciousness. The most influential is the neodissociation theory of Ernest Hilgard (1904 to 2001), set out in Divided Consciousness (1977), which holds that hypnosis divides consciousness, so that part of the mind continues to register what the hypnotised part does not experience. Socio-cognitive (non-state) theories, developed by Theodore Sarbin, Theodore Barber and Nicholas Spanos, hold that hypnotic behaviour is ordinary, goal-directed behaviour shaped by expectations, motivation, attention and the social role of the hypnotised subject, with no special state required. The Key study shows how each side read the same phenomenon.

Key study: Hilgard's hidden observer (1970s)

During a classroom demonstration Hilgard hypnotised a blind student and suggested that he would be completely deaf; the student stopped reacting to loud noises. Someone in the class asked whether "some part" of the student might still be hearing, and Hilgard quietly told the student that if there was such a part, his index finger should rise. The finger rose. Hilgard then studied the effect systematically with hypnotic analgesia: highly hypnotisable subjects held an arm in iced water (the cold pressor test) and, after suggestions of analgesia, reported little pain, yet when the "hidden observer" was asked to report, through automatic writing or a key press, it reported pain close to the levels felt without hypnosis. Hilgard took this as evidence of divided consciousness. Spanos and his colleagues replied (1980) that the hidden observer could be made to report more pain or less pain simply by changing the instructions about what a hidden part would experience, which suggests it is created by expectations. Most researchers now accept elements of both views. Imaging supports the reality of the effect: in 1997 Pierre Rainville and colleagues showed that hypnotic suggestions that made pain more or less unpleasant changed activity in the anterior cingulate cortex but not in the somatosensory cortex, so hypnosis altered how much the pain bothered the subjects rather than how intense it felt.

Several popular beliefs about hypnosis are wrong, and patients need to hear the corrections in the preparation stage. A hypnotised person is not asleep (the brain's electrical activity is that of a waking person), usually remains aware of what is happening and can come out of hypnosis at will. Hypnosis cannot reliably make people act against their deeply held values, although it does make them more willing to go along with what the situation seems to demand, which is why stage hypnosis is ethically dubious. Hypnosis does not give people superhuman strength. And, most important for clinicians, it does not improve the accuracy of memory, a fact so important that it has its own subsection below.

Exam trap

Hypnosis is defined (APA Division 30, 2014 to 2015) by focused attention, reduced peripheral awareness and enhanced response to suggestion; hypnotherapy is the use of hypnosis to treat a disorder; hypnotisability is a stable trait, not gullibility. Hilgard's hidden observer supports the neodissociation (state) theory; Sarbin, Barber and Spanos are the socio-cognitive theorists. Milton Erickson is the hypnotherapist of indirect suggestion and utilisation, not Erik Erikson the ego psychologist. A post-hypnotic suggestion is carried out after the session; it is not the same as post-hypnotic amnesia, which is forgetting the session.

What hypnosis is used for

The oldest and best-supported clinical use of hypnosis is the relief of pain, and its most remarkable demonstration took place in India. In April 1845 James Esdaile (1808 to 1859), a Scottish surgeon of the East India Company in charge of a hospital at Hooghly, near Calcutta, tried mesmerism on a patient before a painful operation and found that the man felt nothing. Over the following months Esdaile performed dozens of operations on mesmerised patients, many of them for huge scrotal tumours, reporting that patients felt little or no pain and claiming that fewer of them died after surgery than was usual. A government committee investigated, and in November 1846 he was given a small mesmeric hospital in Calcutta. But ether had been demonstrated as an anaesthetic in Boston in October 1846, and chloroform followed in 1847; chemical anaesthesia was quicker and worked on everyone, and Esdaile's method faded. It had shown, nonetheless, that suggestion could block the experience of severe pain.

Modern trials confirm the point for procedural and surgical pain. In a trial reported by Guy Montgomery and colleagues in 2007, 200 women about to undergo breast surgery were randomly assigned to a 15-minute hypnosis session before the operation or to an attention-control session of the same length. The hypnosis group needed less anaesthetic during surgery, reported less pain, nausea, fatigue and emotional upset afterwards, and cost the hospital about 770 dollars less per patient, mainly because surgery took less time. Hypnosis is also used for burns dressings, childbirth, dental work and painful procedures in children, and, with more modest results, for chronic pain, where patients are taught self-hypnosis as a coping skill.

Key study: Whorwell's trial of hypnotherapy for irritable bowel syndrome (1984)

Irritable bowel syndrome (IBS), with its abdominal pain, bloating and disturbed bowel habit, is common and often resistant to treatment. In Manchester, the gastroenterologist Peter Whorwell and his colleagues took 30 patients with severe IBS that had not responded to any treatment and randomly assigned them to hypnotherapy or to sessions of psychotherapy with placebo tablets. The hypnotherapy, later called gut-directed hypnotherapy, combined relaxation with suggestions and imagery aimed at the gut itself: the patient placed a warm hand on the abdomen and imagined the bowel working smoothly, like a calmly flowing river. The psychotherapy group improved slightly in pain, distension and well-being; the hypnotherapy group improved dramatically on every measure, including bowel habit, and had not relapsed three months later (Lancet, 1984). The criticism: a small sample and an unblinded comparison. Many later trials have confirmed the benefit, and British national guidance (NICE, 2008) recommends considering hypnotherapy, along with cognitive-behavioural therapy, for people whose IBS has not responded to drug treatment after 12 months.

For habit control the evidence is weaker than advertisements suggest. A Cochrane review of hypnotherapy for smoking cessation (2019) concluded that there was not enough evidence to say whether it works better than other forms of behavioural support or than quitting unaided. For weight loss, a meta-analysis by Irving Kirsch and colleagues (1995) suggested that adding hypnosis to cognitive-behavioural treatment improved results; reanalyses of the weight-loss studies found the added benefit to be smaller than first reported, though still present in some analyses. In anxiety and phobias hypnosis is used as an adjunct, to deepen relaxation and imagery during exposure-based or cognitive treatment, rather than as a treatment on its own. It is not a first-line treatment for obsessive-compulsive disorder (exposure and response prevention is, Chapter 4) or for depression. It is generally avoided in active psychosis and approached with great caution in severe dissociative and borderline conditions. Table 3.27 summarises the evidence.

Table 3.27 Clinical uses of hypnosis and the strength of the evidence.
UseWhat hypnosis addsEvidence
Surgical and procedural painAnalgesic suggestion; less distress and drug needGood (randomised trials)
Chronic painSelf-hypnosis as a coping skillModerate
Irritable bowel syndromeGut-directed suggestion and imageryGood; recommended for refractory IBS
SmokingPost-hypnotic suggestions against urgesInconclusive (Cochrane, 2019)
Weight lossAdjunct to CBTSmall added effect
Anxiety and phobiasRelaxation and imagery within CBTAdjunct only
Recovering memoriesNothing reliableHarmful: creates false memories
Clinical link

When a patient asks for "hypnosis to cure my smoking" or "hypnosis to find out what happened to me as a child", the clinician's reply follows from this subsection. For pain, IBS and some anxiety problems, hypnosis is a respectable adjunct, best delivered by a trained clinician within a wider treatment. For habit control, it may help some people but is not a proven cure. And for uncovering the past it should not be used at all, for the reasons that follow.

Hypnosis and memory: the false memory problem

The most dangerous belief about hypnosis is that it unlocks memory: that under hypnosis a witness can recall a car's number plate, a patient can recover forgotten childhood events, and, in the version broadcast on Indian television, a person can be "regressed" to a past life. The research tells a different story. Hypnosis does often increase the amount a person reports, a phenomenon called hypnotic hypermnesia. But in 1983 Jane Dywan and Kenneth Bowers showed that the extra recall consists of new correct items and even more new errors, while the person's confidence rises for both. The witness under hypnosis does not remember better; he remembers more, believes it more and is harder to shake in cross-examination, a process sometimes called memory hardening. That combination of inaccuracy and certainty is precisely what a court, or a therapist, should fear.

Key study: Laurence and Perry's hypnotically created memory (1983)

Jean-Roch Laurence and Campbell Perry hypnotised 27 highly hypnotisable volunteers, took each back in imagination to a night of the previous week, and asked whether they had heard some loud noises during that night. Many said yes. After hypnosis, 13 of the 27, about half, stated that the noises had really happened, and some held to the memory even after being told that the hypnotist had suggested it. A pseudomemory, a false memory experienced as real, had been implanted with a single question. The criticism is that the volunteers were selected for high hypnotisability and the memory was trivial; but the finding, published in Science, showed how easily a suggestive procedure can create conviction.

These findings became urgent in the "memory wars" of the late 1980s and 1990s, chiefly in the United States. Some therapists, convinced that a patient's depression or eating disorder must stem from forgotten childhood sexual abuse, used hypnosis, guided imagery, dream interpretation and persistent suggestive questioning to "recover" repressed memories, an approach critics called recovered memory therapy. Some patients produced detailed accounts of abuse, families were broken apart and parents were prosecuted; some patients later retracted their memories and sued their therapists. The cognitive psychologist Elizabeth Loftus (born 1944), already known for showing how misleading questions alter eyewitness memory, demonstrated with Jacqueline Pickrell (1995) that about a quarter of adults could be led, by suggestion from relatives, to "remember" an entirely false childhood event: being lost in a shopping mall. (Memory research belongs to the General Psychology book.) Real abuse is common and real survivors must be believed and helped; the lesson is about method, not about whether abuse happens. Professional bodies since the American Medical Association's report of 1985 have concluded that hypnotically refreshed memories are no more accurate than ordinary recall and may be less reliable, and guidance in many countries advises against using hypnosis to recover memories or with anyone who may give evidence in court.

Notice the irony. Freud's demonstration from Bernheim, that forgotten experiences could be brought back by insistence (Section 3.1), was half right: some forgotten things can be recalled. What neither Bernheim nor Freud could see was that insistence and suggestion can also manufacture memories that feel exactly the same.

Exam trap

Hypnosis increases the quantity of recall and the confidence attached to it, not its accuracy; it creates pseudomemories in suggestible people (Laurence and Perry, 1983). "The use of hypnosis and relaxation to access repressed memories" is not what the bilateral stimulation of EMDR means (EMDR uses back-and-forth eye movements while the client holds a memory in mind; Chapter 5). Past-life regression has no scientific support. The misinformation effect and the "lost in the mall" study are Elizabeth Loftus's.

Narcoanalysis: the amytal interview

If suggestion and hypnosis loosen a patient's control, why not use a drug to do it more reliably? That was the idea behind narcoanalysis: an interview conducted after the slow intravenous injection of a sedative-hypnotic drug, in a dose that produces a drowsy, relaxed, talkative state without sleep. The classic drug was the barbiturate sodium amobarbital, sold as Amytal, which is why the procedure is also called the amytal interview; thiopentone (sodium pentothal) was also widely used, and benzodiazepines such as diazepam and lorazepam later replaced the barbiturates. Under the drug, inhibitions fall, speech flows more freely and emotionally charged material may pour out. In Indian psychiatric practice the procedure has long been known simply as "abreaction", because releasing pent-up emotion was its main clinical purpose, a direct descendant of Breuer's hypnotic abreaction (Section 3.1).

The method has a short and colourful history. In 1922 Robert House, an obstetrician in Dallas, Texas, who had noticed how freely women talked under the scopolamine "twilight sleep" then used in childbirth, interviewed two prisoners in the Dallas county jail under scopolamine. Both denied the charges and both were later acquitted, and House concluded that a person under the drug "cannot create a lie". A newspaper report of his experiment is thought to have coined the phrase "truth serum", and the myth was launched. Psychiatry's version began in 1930, when William Bleckwenn at the University of Wisconsin found that intravenous sodium amytal could produce a brief "lucid interval" in mute, rigid catatonic patients, who would talk and eat for a while before relapsing. In 1936 the British psychiatrist J. Stephen Horsley, writing in The Lancet, called the technique of interviewing patients under such drugs narco-analysis, and he later set it out at length in his book Narco-Analysis (1943). During the Second World War the American psychiatrists Roy Grinker and John Spiegel used intravenous pentothal with soldiers suffering acute combat neuroses in North Africa. Under the drug, soldiers relived the battle with terrifying vividness, and the therapist helped them to integrate the experience into ordinary consciousness, a procedure Grinker and Spiegel called narcosynthesis.

After the war the amytal interview settled into a modest set of clinical uses. It was given to patients in catatonic stupor or mutism, both to help diagnosis (a patient with a functional stupor often became able to talk, while a patient whose stupor had an organic cause usually became more confused) and to allow a brief interview. It was used in conversion and dissociative states, such as hysterical mutism, paralysis, amnesia and fugue, to help the patient recall the precipitating events and to suggest recovery. And it was occasionally used to help very inhibited patients speak about painful material.

Clinical use has since faded almost everywhere, for several reasons that together make a useful answer to an interview question:

  • Safety. Intravenous barbiturates depress breathing and can cause laryngeal spasm, a fall in blood pressure or stopped breathing, so the procedure needs a doctor, resuscitation equipment and monitoring; it is dangerous in people with respiratory disease or porphyria.
  • Truth. The drug does not make people truthful; it makes them disinhibited and more suggestible. People can keep up a lie under amytal, and what they do say mixes memory, fantasy and the interviewer's suggestions (see the Key study). The material is, at best, psychologically meaningful rather than factually reliable.
  • Better alternatives. For catatonia, a safer benzodiazepine, lorazepam, given as a "lorazepam challenge", now serves the same diagnostic and therapeutic purpose; for conversion and dissociative disorders, psychological treatment and rehabilitation have replaced the drug.
  • The drug adds little. Grinker and Spiegel themselves concluded that, given enough time, they could obtain essentially the same material and the same emotional release without drugs.
  • Ethics and evidence. Patients may disclose things they did not intend to, the consent of a frightened or mute patient is hard to secure, and there have never been good controlled trials showing lasting benefit.
Key study: Redlich, Ravitz and Dession, "Narcoanalysis and Truth" (1951)

Could a person under sodium amytal keep a secret? At Yale, the psychiatrist Frederick Redlich and his colleagues asked nine volunteers, students and professionals, to reveal shameful, guilt-producing episodes from their past and then to invent false, self-protective cover stories for them. Each volunteer was then given sodium amytal and cross-examined about the cover story by a second investigator who did not know the truth. Normal individuals with good defences stuck to their invented stories and refused to confess. Neurotic individuals with strong self-punishing tendencies confessed more easily, and they also substituted fantasy for truth, confessing to offences they had never committed. The conclusion: the "truth drug" can neither guarantee the truth nor prevent false confessions. The criticism is the tiny sample and the artificial secrets, but every later review has reached the same conclusion.

Exam trap

There is no truth serum. Barbiturates and benzodiazepines disinhibit and increase suggestibility; they neither compel truth nor prevent lies or false confessions. Narcoanalysis (the amytal interview) is a drug-assisted interview; narcosynthesis is Grinker and Spiegel's wartime use of it to relive and integrate combat trauma; drug-assisted abreaction is the Indian clinical name. Narcoanalysis is not a psychological test, and it is not the same as a polygraph (which records autonomic arousal) or "brain mapping" (which records brain electrical responses to words or pictures).

Narcoanalysis and Indian law: Selvi v. State of Karnataka (2010)

Just as psychiatry was abandoning narcoanalysis, Indian police investigators took it up. From the early 2000s suspects in many high-profile cases were sent to state forensic science laboratories, notably in Bengaluru and Gandhinagar, where they were given intravenous sodium pentothal and questioned by forensic psychologists with an anaesthetist in attendance; video recordings of the sessions sometimes reached television news. Polygraph examinations and the brain electrical activation profile (BEAP, popularly called "brain mapping") were used alongside. The practice was challenged in a group of appeals that reached the Supreme Court of India, heard together as Selvi and Others v. State of Karnataka.

On 5 May 2010 a three-judge bench led by Chief Justice K. G. Balakrishnan (with Justices R. V. Raveendran and J. M. Panchal) gave its judgment. Its central holdings are these:

  1. No individual may be forcibly subjected to narcoanalysis, polygraph examination or BEAP, whether in a criminal investigation or otherwise. Compulsory administration violates the right against self-incrimination in Article 20(3) of the Constitution ("No person accused of any offence shall be compelled to be a witness against himself"), because the results are testimonial in character, conveying personal knowledge; and it violates the right to personal liberty in Article 21, which the Court held to include a person's mental privacy.
  2. The tests may be administered voluntarily, but only with safeguards. The person's consent must be recorded before a Judicial Magistrate, with the person's lawyer present, after the person has been told of the physical, emotional and legal consequences, and the National Human Rights Commission's guidelines for the administration of polygraph tests must be strictly followed, with similar safeguards for narcoanalysis and BEAP.
  3. Even when the person consents, the test results by themselves cannot be admitted as evidence, because the subject does not exercise conscious control over the responses during the test.
  4. However, any information or material subsequently discovered with the help of voluntarily administered test results can be admitted, in accordance with Section 27 of the Indian Evidence Act, 1872 (the provision on facts discovered in consequence of information received from an accused).

For a psychologist the judgment has two lessons. The first is legal: taking part in an involuntary narcoanalysis, polygraph or BEAP examination in India is unconstitutional, and voluntary examinations are lawful only within the Court's safeguards. The second is ethical and scientific: the Court's reasoning echoed what clinical psychiatry had already learned, that a drugged person's statements are neither reliably true nor freely given, and that consent is the foundation of any procedure done to a person's mind. The professional ethics of consent are set out in Chapter 1 and forensic practice in Chapter 10.

Exam trap

Selvi v. State of Karnataka (Supreme Court, 5 May 2010) covers three techniques: narcoanalysis, polygraph and the brain electrical activation profile (BEAP). Involuntary use violates Article 20(3) (self-incrimination) and Article 21 (personal liberty, including mental privacy). Voluntary use is allowed with consent recorded before a Judicial Magistrate and a lawyer present; the results are not admissible as evidence, but facts later discovered with their help may be admitted under Section 27 of the Evidence Act. Do not confuse it with K. S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India (2017), the nine-judge judgment that declared privacy a fundamental right.

Table 3.28 brings the three methods of this section together, which is the comparison an examiner or an interviewer is most likely to ask for.

Table 3.28 Supportive psychotherapy, hypnotherapy and narcoanalysis compared.
MethodHow it worksUsed today forMain limits
Supportive psychotherapyDirect measures: ventilation, reassurance, guidance, environmental manipulationCrises; weak ego strength; chronic illness; busy public clinicsDependence; no structural change; risk of false reassurance and imposed values
HypnotherapyFocused attention and enhanced response to suggestionPain, IBS, adjunct in anxiety and habit controlVaries with hypnotisability; weak evidence for habits; creates false memories
NarcoanalysisIntravenous sedative disinhibits speechRarely: catatonia (now lorazepam), some conversion statesRespiratory risk; no truth; suggestibility; illegal if involuntary in India

Each method in this chapter, from the couch and the interpretation to the contract, the reassurance and the syringe, carries a claim about what helps people change. Section 3.11 asks how well the psychodynamic claims stand up to the evidence.
