3.11 Does Psychodynamic Therapy Work?Medium yield · 3 Qs

Freud was never in doubt that psychoanalysis went to the roots of a neurosis instead of painting over its surface. Yet for most of the twentieth century the evidence he and his followers offered was of one kind only: the case history, written by the treating analyst about a treatment no outsider had watched. Stories like those of Anna O. and Dora can persuade, but they cannot tell you how many patients improved, how many would have improved anyway and how many got worse. This last section asks the question a sceptical colleague or an interview panel will put to you: does psychodynamic therapy work, for whom, and why?

The question has three layers, and much confusion comes from mixing them. Is psychoanalytic theory the kind of theory that evidence could ever refute? Do patients get better, more than they would without treatment and as much as with other treatments? And if they do, is it because of insight into unconscious conflict, as the theory says, or because of something every good therapy shares? The critics attacked each layer in turn, and this section follows them in that order.

Is psychoanalysis a science? Popper and Grünbaum

In 1919 a young Viennese student named Karl Popper (1902 to 1994) was helping with Alfred Adler's social work among the children of working-class Vienna (you meet Adler as a therapist in Section 3.8). One day Popper described to Adler a case that did not seem to him particularly Adlerian. Adler analysed it at once in terms of his theory of inferiority feelings, although he had never seen the child. Slightly shocked, Popper asked how he could be so sure. "Because of my thousandfold experience," Adler replied. Popper could not resist: "And with this new case, I suppose, your experience has become thousand-and-one-fold."

The exchange stayed with Popper, and it became the seed of his most famous idea. What had first impressed him about the theories of Freud, Adler and Karl Marx was their explanatory power: whatever happened, they could explain it. Later he came to see this as their weakness. His standard illustration involved two men. One pushes a child into the water intending to drown it; the other sacrifices his own life trying to save it. Freud, Popper wrote, would explain the first man by repression (of some component of his Oedipus complex) and the second by sublimation; Adler would explain both by feelings of inferiority, the first man needing to prove to himself that he dared to commit a crime, the second that he dared to rescue the child. Popper could not think of any human behaviour that either theory could not explain. A theory that fits every possible outcome, he argued, forbids nothing, and so tells you nothing about the world; Albert Einstein's general relativity, by contrast, predicted a definite bending of starlight near the sun, which the eclipse observations of 1919 could have refuted and did not.

Popper's criterion of falsifiability, set out in The Logic of Scientific Discovery (German 1934, English 1959) and turned on psychoanalysis in the essays collected in Conjectures and Refutations (1963), holds that a theory is scientific only if it specifies observations that would show it to be false. On this test psychoanalysis was not a false science but a pseudo-science: not refuted, but irrefutable. Critics found a vivid example in the consulting room itself. If the patient accepts an interpretation, the interpretation is confirmed; if the patient rejects it, the rejection is resistance (Section 3.2), and the interpretation is confirmed again. Freud knew the jibe. In "Constructions in Analysis" (1937) he quoted a "well-known man of science" who said that analysts treat their patients on the principle of "Heads I win, tails you lose", and replied that analysts take neither the patient's "yes" nor the patient's "no" at face value: a construction is a conjecture, to be confirmed or dropped according to what the further course of the analysis brings up, such as new memories and associations.

The most searching critic of psychoanalysis turned Popper's argument upside down. Adolf Grünbaum (1923 to 2018), a philosopher of science at the University of Pittsburgh, argued in The Foundations of Psychoanalysis: A Philosophical Critique (1984) that Popper had misjudged the problem. Many Freudian claims are perfectly testable; Freud himself revised his ideas when the evidence went against them (he abandoned his seduction theory of hysteria in 1897); and some of the claims, once tested, look false. The real trouble, Grünbaum said, is not that the theory cannot be tested but that the couch is the wrong place to test it. Everything an analyst observes in a session (the patient's associations, recovered memories, dreams and agreement with interpretations) is produced inside a relationship with an authority figure whose theory the patient soon learns. The clinical data are contaminated by suggestion at their source, so they cannot be used to confirm the theory that shaped them. Bernheim and the Nancy school of Section 3.1 would not have been surprised.

Freud had an answer ready, and Grünbaum's dissection of it is his most quoted argument. In the Introductory Lectures (1916 to 1917) Freud granted that the analyst influences the patient, but insisted that the patient's conflicts will only be solved, and the resistances overcome, if the ideas the analyst offers "tally with what is real" in the patient. Suggestion can produce a passing improvement; only a true interpretation can produce a lasting cure; so durable cures vouch for the truth of the interpretations. Grünbaum named this the tally argument and pointed out what it needs in order to work: that neuroses are hardly ever cured by any other means, and that they rarely clear up by themselves. Both premises are false. Other therapies relieve the same conditions, and many neurotic problems remit without treatment (the claim Hans Eysenck pressed in 1952, which you meet below). Once those facts are admitted, a cure on the couch no longer shows that the interpretations were true. It could be the work of suggestion, hope, the relationship or time.

Defenders replied in two ways. Some argued that Grünbaum had misread Freud. The more productive reply was to move the testing out of the consulting room. Experimental and developmental research has since supported several broadly psychodynamic propositions: that much of mental life is unconscious, that emotional reactions can be triggered by cues outside awareness, that people use defensive processes to keep painful information out of mind, and that early relationships lay down templates for later ones (the attachment research of Section 3.5); the American psychologist Drew Westen surveyed this evidence in 1998 in "The scientific legacy of Sigmund Freud". Other claims, such as the universal Oedipus complex or dreams as disguised wish fulfilments, have fared poorly. For therapy the lesson was plain: whether the treatment works would be settled not by analysts' reports of their own cases but by controlled comparisons of outcome.

Exam trap

Keep the three classic critics apart. Karl Popper's charge is that psychoanalytic theory is unfalsifiable: it can explain any outcome, so no observation could refute it. Adolf Grünbaum's charge is almost the reverse: the theory is testable, but the clinical evidence for it is contaminated by suggestion, and Freud's tally argument fails because other therapies and spontaneous remission also cure. Hans Eysenck's charge (1952) is about outcome: patients in psychoanalysis improved no more than people who had no formal therapy. "Heads I win, tails you lose" is the critics' summary of interpretation plus resistance, and Freud quoted it himself in 1937.

Cost, length and a founding legend

Practical critics made simpler objections, and they are still the ones a hospital manager raises first. Classical analysis means four or five sessions a week for several years (Section 3.2), and even once- or twice-weekly psychodynamic psychotherapy is often open-ended. Few people can afford it, no public health service can offer it widely, and long treatments are slow and costly to test, which is one reason, as you will see, that the evidence on long-term therapy is thin.

The patients whom analysis suited were also a select group. Freud himself wrote in "On Psychotherapy" (1905) that people near or above the age of fifty usually lack the elasticity of mind on which the treatment depends, and that a suitable patient should have a reasonable degree of education and a fairly reliable character. Classical analysts looked for patients who were psychologically minded, articulate and motivated. The American psychologist William Schofield later gave the favoured client of psychotherapy a famous acronym, YAVIS: young, attractive, verbal, intelligent and successful (Section 8.6). A treatment that selects the healthiest applicants will look effective whether or not it is, and it leaves out the poor, the less educated, the old and the severely ill, who make up most of the people who need help.

Then there was the evidence itself, or rather its absence. For decades psychoanalytic institutes, especially in the United States, treated research with suspicion and maintained that only analysts were competent to judge analysis. Even Jonathan Shedler, the American psychologist whose defence of psychodynamic therapy you will meet below, conceded in 2010 that the "indifference to empirical research of earlier generations of psychoanalysts" was a failing that "continues to haunt the field". When Hans Jürgen Eysenck (1916 to 1997) pooled the published outcome figures in 1952, psychoanalysis came off worst of all: about 44 per cent of its patients improved, against about 64 per cent for eclectic therapy and about 72 per cent for people who received no formal psychotherapy, although Eysenck counted every patient who broke off analysis as a failure. Chapter 8 (Section 8.1) tells that story and the replies to it in full.

Even the founding case did not survive scrutiny. Studies on Hysteria (1895) presented Anna O. as a woman freed of her symptoms by talking. In 1972 the psychiatrist and historian Henri Ellenberger (1905 to 1993), author of The Discovery of the Unconscious (1970), traced the records of the Bellevue sanatorium at Kreuzlingen in Switzerland and showed that Bertha Pappenheim had been admitted there in July 1882, within weeks of the end of Breuer's treatment, still ill and dependent on the morphine she had been given for facial pain. The prototype of the cathartic cure, Ellenberger concluded, had been neither a cure nor a catharsis. What became of her afterwards is part of the answer to the question this chapter opened with, and the chapter's closing pages give it in full. For now, notice that the first evidence ever offered for the talking cure was a case history that left out its ending.

Figure 3.13 gathers the classic lines of criticism and the kind of evidence that eventually answered each of them.

Exam trap

YAVIS (young, attractive, verbal, intelligent, successful) is William Schofield's acronym (1964), not Freud's; Freud's own warning was about patients near or above fifty. Eysenck's 44 per cent for psychoanalysis counted dropouts as failures, which is one of the standard objections to his review. The "symptom substitution" that analysts predicted when symptoms are treated directly was largely not found (Section 3.2 and Chapter 4). And the historian who found the Bellevue records on Anna O. is Henri Ellenberger (1972), not Ernest Jones, whose 1953 biography of Freud first made her real name public.

The Menninger project: forty-two lives in treatment

If case histories cannot settle the question, what can? The first serious attempt to study psychoanalytic treatment systematically, with records kept and predictions made by people other than the treating analyst, began in 1954 at the Menninger Foundation in Topeka, Kansas, then one of the great centres of American psychiatry. Its principal investigator was Robert Wallerstein (1921 to 2014), a psychiatrist and analyst who later became president of the International Psychoanalytical Association. The final report, Forty-two Lives in Treatment: A Study of Psychoanalysis and Psychotherapy, appeared in 1986, more than thirty years after the first patient was enrolled.

Key study: The Menninger Psychotherapy Research Project (1954 to 1982)

The question. What changes take place in psychoanalysis and in psychoanalytic psychotherapy, and which features of the patient, the therapist and the treatment account for them? The method. Forty-two adults, most of them seriously disturbed and many of whom had travelled to Topeka from across the United States and abroad, were followed through their treatment: roughly half in classical psychoanalysis and half in psychoanalytic psychotherapy that mixed expressive (interpretive) and supportive techniques. Researchers who were not treating them made detailed predictions at the outset about how each patient would respond and why, gathered case records and clinical ratings during treatment, and reassessed the patients at termination and again at follow-up some years later; Wallerstein kept track of many of them for decades.

The result. Three findings surprised the investigators. First, the treatments were less distinct than intended: the analyses contained supportive elements, and the psychotherapies contained more support than planned. Second, the supportive elements did far more of the work than anyone had predicted, and the changes they produced were often as stable and as deep ("structural", in analytic language) as the changes reached through interpretation and insight. Third, many patients changed through what Wallerstein called a "transference cure": in his words, the patient in effect says, I make the agreed-upon changes for you, the therapist, in order to earn and keep your support, your esteem and your love. Proportionally, psychoanalysis did no better than psychotherapy, and several of the sickest patients, placed in full analysis in the hope that only analysis could reach them, did worse than predicted; Wallerstein recorded the failure of these "heroic indications" for analysis. The criticism. There was no randomisation and no untreated comparison group, the sample was small and unusual, and outcome was judged by clinical ratings rather than standard measures. The project could describe change in great depth, but it could not show that the treatment, rather than time, had caused it.

The Menninger findings cut against the central assumption of classical technique (Section 3.2): that lasting change comes through insight, while support merely props the patient up. If encouragement, advice, strengthened defences and the wish to please the therapist could produce durable change, the line between "deep" and "superficial" treatment was not where analysts had drawn it. Wallerstein concluded that supportive techniques deserved far more respect, one reason supportive psychotherapy (Section 3.10) is now taught as a treatment in its own right. And the transference cure, change made for the sake of the relationship with the therapist rather than through insight, is a psychoanalytic name for what Chapter 8 calls common factors: improvement carried by the relationship and the patient's attachment to the helper.

The same data were also read another way. Otto Kernberg (Section 3.4), who led the project's quantitative analysis (reported in 1972), concluded that patients with weak egos did best in a skilled, expressive psychotherapy that worked on the transference in the here and now, the reading that later grew into transference-focused psychotherapy. One project thus yielded two lessons, Wallerstein's (support does more than analysts believed) and Kernberg's (the most disturbed patients need a structured, transference-focused therapy), and both reappear in the borderline trials below.

Exam trap

The Menninger Psychotherapy Research Project (Topeka, 1954 to 1982) is Robert Wallerstein's; its book is Forty-two Lives in Treatment (1986), and 42 is the number of patients. Its famous finding is that supportive elements did much of the work and produced change as durable as insight did, often through a "transference cure". It was a naturalistic study, not a randomised controlled trial. Do not confuse it with Eysenck's 1952 review (a reanalysis of published figures), Smith and Glass's 1977 meta-analysis (Section 8.2) or the NIMH depression trial of CBT, interpersonal psychotherapy and imipramine (Section 8.8).

Short-term psychodynamic therapy: the meta-analyses

Since the 1990s the question has been asked through randomised controlled trials and meta-analyses, the methods of Chapter 8 (Sections 8.2 and 8.7). An effect size expresses the difference between a treated group and a comparison group in standard deviation units; by Jacob Cohen's rule of thumb 0.2 is small, 0.5 moderate and 0.8 large, and Smith, Glass and Miller (1980) put the average effect of psychotherapy of all kinds, against no treatment, at 0.85.

To judge psychodynamic therapy you need one further distinction, because nearly every quarrel below turns on it. A between-group effect size compares patients who received the therapy with patients randomly assigned to something else (a waiting list, usual care or another therapy) at the same point in time. A within-group effect size, also called a pre-post effect size, compares the same patients before and after treatment. The second is almost always larger, because it also counts everything that would have improved the patients anyway: the passage of time, spontaneous remission, the tendency of people who seek help at their worst to drift back towards their usual level (regression to the mean), and the hope that comes with starting any treatment. A within-group effect of 1.4 tells you nothing about whether the therapy beats an alternative; a between-group effect of 1.4 would be remarkable.

Short-term psychodynamic psychotherapy (STPP), the time-limited family described in Section 3.6, was the first to be tested in numbers. In 2004 the German psychotherapy researcher Falk Leichsenring, with Sven Rabung and Eric Leibing, pooled 17 high-quality randomised trials of STPP (an average of 21 sessions) in the Archives of General Psychiatry. Compared with control conditions, the effect on the patients' target problems was 1.17; within the treated groups, the pre-post effect was 1.39 at the end of therapy and 1.57 at follow-up an average of 13 months later.

Two years later came a review for the Cochrane Library, the database of systematic reviews that medicine treats as its most rigorous summary of evidence. Its lead author was the Canadian psychiatrist Allan Abbass of Dalhousie University in Halifax, a leading researcher in Davanloo's intensive short-term dynamic psychotherapy (Section 3.6). Abbass and his colleagues (2006) pooled 23 randomised trials with 1,431 patients who had common mental disorders (non-psychotic depression, anxiety and somatoform disorders, often mixed with personality problems) and who received under 40 hours of STPP, compared with waiting-list, minimal-treatment or usual-care controls. The effect size for general symptom improvement was 0.97, rising to 1.51 at follow-up more than nine months after treatment. The effects for anxiety (1.08, rising to 1.35) and somatic symptoms (0.81, rising to 2.21) were also large; the effect on depressive symptoms was more modest (0.59, rising to 0.98).

The reviewers attached two warnings themselves. The trials were few, of variable quality and very different from one another; and most compared STPP with waiting lists, minimal treatment or usual care, which shows that it beats little or nothing, not that it matches the best alternative. The 2014 Cochrane update (Abbass with Steve Kisely, Joel Town, Leichsenring and others: 33 studies, 2,173 participants) reached the same conclusion with the same caution: gains over controls in the short and medium term that generally appeared to grow in the long term. A review by Abbass, Kisely and Kurt Kroenke (2009) found STPP helpful for somatic symptom conditions as well, often with a fall in the patients' use of health care.

Remember

Before any effect size impresses you, ask two questions. Compared with what? (A waiting list is an easy opponent; an established therapy is a hard one.) And measured how? (A pre-post change within one group includes natural recovery.) Cohen's benchmarks are 0.2 small, 0.5 moderate, 0.8 large.

Long-term therapy and the quarrel over 1.8

Short-term therapy is the easy case. The harder question, and the one that matters for psychoanalysis proper, is whether long, open-ended treatment adds anything. In 2008 Leichsenring and Rabung published "Effectiveness of long-term psychodynamic psychotherapy" in the Journal of the American Medical Association (JAMA). They defined long-term psychodynamic psychotherapy (LTPP) as psychodynamic therapy lasting at least a year or 50 sessions, and they asked about "complex" mental disorders (personality disorders, chronic disorders and multiple co-occurring disorders), for which short treatments most often fail. Pooling 23 studies with 1,053 patients (11 randomised trials and 12 observational studies), they reported that in the controlled comparisons LTPP was significantly better than shorter psychotherapies in overall effectiveness, target problems and personality functioning. The headline figure was a between-group effect size of 1.8 for overall effectiveness: after LTPP, the authors wrote, patients with complex disorders were on average better off than 96 per cent of the patients in the comparison groups.

The number 1.8 caused a storm. An effect that size is far larger than almost anything else in the psychotherapy literature, and critics, among them Brett Thombs and Aaron Beck (Chapter 5), soon argued that it was an artefact. It had not been calculated as a direct difference between randomised groups. Instead the pre-post effects of the LTPP arms had been compared with the pre-post effects of the comparison arms, treating treatment conditions rather than studies as the units of analysis, which throws away the protection that randomisation provides. The comparison treatments were also often short, weak or not genuine psychotherapies, so "better than shorter therapy" partly meant "better than a smaller dose of something lesser". Even Shedler, who cited the result in 2010, added a footnote warning that the atypical method might have inflated it.

Leichsenring and Rabung responded to the methodological point and in 2011 published an update in the British Journal of Psychiatry, computing proper between-group effect sizes from ten controlled studies with 971 patients (all but one of them randomised trials). LTPP still came out ahead of less intensive psychotherapies for complex disorders, but the advantage was now moderate, from 0.44 to 0.68 depending on the outcome. In 2012 Smit and colleagues (among them the meta-researcher John Ioannidis and the schema therapy researcher Arnoud Arntz) analysed eleven trials in Clinical Psychology Review, using recovery as the main outcome, and found no difference between LTPP and the control treatments: the evidence, they concluded, was "limited and at best conflicting". Leichsenring's group replied that many of Smit's controls were themselves long-term therapies, so the finding showed that LTPP equals other long treatments, not that length adds nothing. The honest summary is that long-term psychodynamic therapy has some controlled support for complex and chronic problems, from a small number of trials, with effects whose true size is still argued over.

Two trials show what long treatment can and cannot do. In the Helsinki Psychotherapy Study (Paul Knekt and colleagues, 2008), 326 outpatients with depressive or anxiety disorders were randomly assigned to up to three years of long-term psychodynamic psychotherapy, to short-term psychodynamic psychotherapy or to solution-focused therapy (Chapter 6): the short therapies were ahead in the first year, the groups were level in the second, and the long-term group was ahead at three years. In the Tavistock Adult Depression Study in London (Peter Fonagy and colleagues, 2015), 129 patients whose depression had failed at least two treatments received usual care with or without 18 months of weekly psychoanalytic psychotherapy; the difference in full remission was small at the end of treatment but widened over the next two years.

Gains that keep growing after therapy has stopped, sometimes called the sleeper effect, are the most distinctive claim made for psychodynamic treatment; the rising follow-up figures of Leichsenring (2004) and Abbass (2006) above are examples. Defenders explain it by the theory: a patient whose inner capacities have changed goes on using them. Critics point to duller explanations: the patients who stay in follow-up are not a random sample, chronic conditions fluctuate, and further treatment muddies the comparison (in the ten-year Helsinki follow-up the long-term advantage had shrunk, possibly because more short-term patients had gone on to other therapy). The effect is plausible and repeatedly reported, but it rests on fewer and smaller studies than its fame suggests.

Exam trap

Leichsenring and Rabung's JAMA meta-analysis (2008) is about long-term therapy (at least a year or 50 sessions) for complex disorders; its famous figure is an effect size of 1.8 ("better off than 96 per cent"), and its weakness is the method that produced it, corrected to a moderate effect in the 2011 update. Abbass and colleagues' Cochrane review (2006, updated 2014) is about short-term therapy (under 40 hours) for common mental disorders; its famous figure is 0.97 for general symptoms, rising to 1.51 at follow-up. Do not swap the two, and do not treat a pre-post effect size as evidence that a therapy beats anything.

Shedler's case for the defence

The paper that carried these findings to a wide audience was Shedler's review "The efficacy of psychodynamic psychotherapy", published in 2010 in American Psychologist, the flagship journal of the American Psychological Association. Its abstract made three claims. First, effect sizes for psychodynamic therapy "are as large as those reported for other therapies that have been actively promoted as 'empirically supported' and 'evidence based'". Second, patients who receive psychodynamic therapy maintain their gains and appear to continue to improve after treatment ends. Third, non-psychodynamic therapies may be effective partly because their more skilled practitioners use techniques long central to psychodynamic practice. The common impression that psychodynamic approaches lack empirical support, he added, might reflect "selective dissemination of research findings", fed by a lingering distaste in the mental health professions for past psychoanalytic arrogance and authority.

To make the first claim concrete, Shedler set the psychodynamic figures beside reference points from other treatments, and Figure 3.14 plots a selection of them. Psychotherapy in general, in Smith, Glass and Miller's 1980 meta-analysis, had an effect size of 0.85. CBT for depression, panic disorder and generalised anxiety disorder, in a 2006 meta-analysis by Haby and colleagues, had 0.68. Antidepressant medication, in Erick Turner and colleagues' 2008 analysis of all the trials submitted to the United States Food and Drug Administration (FDA), published and unpublished, had 0.31. Against these, STPP's 0.97 and 1.17 looked excellent, and LTPP's 1.8 looked extraordinary.

The replies came quickly, several of them in the same journal in 2011, among them one by Joye Anestis, Michael Anestis and Scott Lilienfeld. The critics' four main points are worth being able to state.

  • Like was not compared with like. Pre-post effects, effects against waiting lists and one atypical calculation sat beside reference figures from other kinds of comparison, exactly the mixture that Figure 3.14 displays.
  • The evidence base was small. Shedler himself conceded that some of his most striking findings rested on only two studies and that there are far more outcome studies of CBT.
  • Many psychodynamic trials were small, used weak controls, mixed diagnoses and were run by researchers committed to the therapy (the allegiance effect of Section 8.6).
  • The process evidence for the third claim, described below, was correlational.

Shedler answered that his critics held psychodynamic research to a stricter standard than the treatments they favoured. The fair verdict sits between them: short-term psychodynamic therapy clearly beats no treatment or minimal treatment for common mental disorders, with effects in the same broad range as other psychotherapies, but the comparisons that made the paper famous were less secure than they looked.

Exam trap

Shedler's "The efficacy of psychodynamic psychotherapy" appeared in American Psychologist in 2010. Its three claims: effect sizes as large as those of "empirically supported" therapies; gains maintained and growing after treatment; other therapies work partly through psychodynamic techniques. Its reference numbers: 0.85 for psychotherapy in general (Smith, Glass and Miller, 1980), 0.68 for CBT (Haby and colleagues, 2006), 0.31 for antidepressants (Turner and colleagues, 2008), 0.97 for STPP (Abbass and colleagues, 2006). Do not confuse this review with Smith and Glass's 1977 meta-analysis or Eysenck's 1952 review (Chapter 8), and do not confuse Shedler's review with the Shedler-Westen Assessment Procedure, a personality assessment instrument (Assessment book).

Borderline personality disorder: MBT and TFP on trial

The strongest single body of evidence for modern psychodynamic treatment concerns borderline personality disorder, the pattern of unstable relationships, identity and emotion, with impulsivity and frequent self-harm, that classical analysis had found so hard to help (the disorder itself belongs to the Psychiatric Disorders book). Two psychodynamic treatments built for it have been tested in randomised trials. Mentalisation-based treatment (MBT), Anthony Bateman and Peter Fonagy's therapy for strengthening the patient's capacity to understand behaviour in terms of mental states, is taught in Section 3.5; transference-focused psychotherapy (TFP), Kernberg's treatment, in Section 3.4. Here the only question is how well they work.

MBT's trials are told as a Key study in Section 3.5: the small day-hospital trial (1999), its eight-year follow-up (2008) and the outpatient trial (2009) against structured clinical management, a well-organised, supportive, generic treatment. Read as evaluation, those trials have two weaknesses: they were run by the treatment's developers, and the comparison treatment in the first trial was weak. Independent replications have been more mixed; a Danish trial reported in 2013, for example, found intensive MBT better than a less intensive supportive group therapy on only one main outcome, global functioning.

TFP's main trials you have already met. In Clarkin and colleagues' New York trial of 2007 (Key study, Section 3.4), TFP, dialectical behaviour therapy (DBT) and supportive psychodynamic therapy all helped, with TFP changing the most domains and alone increasing reflective function; in Giesen-Bloo and colleagues' Dutch trial of 2006, schema therapy did better than TFP. A third trial adds an important piece. In 2010, in the British Journal of Psychiatry, a German and Austrian team led by Stephan Doering randomly assigned 104 women with borderline personality disorder to a year of TFP or to treatment by experienced community psychotherapists. Far fewer patients dropped out of TFP (about 39 per cent against 67 per cent), fewer attempted suicide, and TFP did better on borderline symptoms, psychosocial functioning, personality organisation and psychiatric admissions, although depression and anxiety improved equally in both groups.

Three lessons follow, and they echo the Menninger project. Specialised psychodynamic treatments for borderline personality disorder work, and some benefits last for years. They are not clearly better than the other specialised treatments, DBT (Section 5.8) and schema therapy (Section 5.7); the 2020 Cochrane review of psychological therapies for the disorder found that disorder-specific psychotherapies reduce its severity compared with usual treatment, with DBT and MBT the most studied, but judged much of the evidence to be of low certainty. And well-structured generic or supportive care does surprisingly well, which suggests that what the successful treatments share (a clear structure, a coherent model, a consistent team and steady attention to the relationship) matters as much as what divides them.

Exam trap

MBT is Bateman and Fonagy's: the day-hospital trial (1999), the eight-year follow-up (2008: 13 per cent against 87 per cent still meeting criteria) and the outpatient trial against structured clinical management (2009), not against DBT. TFP is Kernberg's: Clarkin and colleagues (2007, against DBT and supportive therapy) and Doering and colleagues (2010, against community psychotherapists). In Giesen-Bloo's 2006 trial it was schema therapy (Jeffrey Young) that beat TFP. DBT is Marsha Linehan's and is a cognitive-behavioural therapy, not a psychodynamic one.

Psychodynamic therapy versus CBT

The comparison everyone wants is the head-to-head race with CBT, the most researched psychotherapy in the world (Section 5.11 surveys its evidence). Once you separate the disorders, the results form a clear pattern.

For depression the two have repeatedly come out roughly level. In Amsterdam, Ellen Driessen and colleagues (American Journal of Psychiatry, 2013) randomly assigned 341 outpatients with major depression to 16 sessions of CBT or of short-term psychodynamic supportive therapy, and found no significant differences on any outcome. The sobering detail is that only about 23 per cent of patients were in remission at the end of treatment, a reminder that sixteen sessions are not enough for many clinic patients, whatever the brand. In an American community mental health setting, Mary Beth Connolly Gibbons and colleagues (JAMA Psychiatry, 2016) randomly assigned 237 outpatients and found dynamic psychotherapy no worse (statistically noninferior) than cognitive therapy when both were delivered by community clinicians.

For social anxiety disorder, the largest trial of psychodynamic therapy ever run, led by Leichsenring at several German centres and published in 2013, randomly assigned 495 patients to manualised CBT (209), manualised psychodynamic therapy (207) or a waiting list (79). Both therapies beat the waiting list. At the end of treatment CBT was ahead on remission (36 per cent against 26 per cent, with 9 per cent on the waiting list) but not significantly on response (60 against 52 per cent). By the two-year follow-up the remission rates of the two therapies had converged at about 40 per cent. Notice who ran the trial: a team led by psychodynamic researchers that reported a CBT advantage plainly, the kind of result that builds trust in a research programme. For panic disorder, Barbara Milrod's panic-focused psychodynamic psychotherapy beat applied relaxation in a small New York trial (2007: 49 patients; response 73 per cent against 39 per cent), although in a larger two-site trial (2016, 201 patients) CBT gave the more consistent results across the two sites.

The meta-analyses tell the same story at a larger scale. David Tolin (Clinical Psychology Review, 2010) found CBT modestly superior to psychodynamic therapy (an effect size of about 0.28) for anxiety and depressive disorders, though not to interpersonal or supportive therapy, and a reanalysis by Baardseth and colleagues (2013) disputed even that advantage. The most careful test so far, by Christiane Steinert, Thomas Munder, Sven Rabung, Jürgen Hoyer and Leichsenring (American Journal of Psychiatry, 2017), tested formal equivalence rather than merely the absence of a significant difference. Across 23 randomised trials with 2,751 patients, comparing psychodynamic therapy with established treatments (mostly CBT) for mood, anxiety, eating, personality and substance use disorders and PTSD, psychodynamic therapy was equivalent in efficacy at the end of treatment and at follow-up; to reduce allegiance bias the team deliberately included researchers from both camps.

Where does CBT keep a clear lead? Mainly where exposure is the active ingredient and psychodynamic therapy has hardly been tested: obsessive-compulsive disorder, specific phobias and post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), for which exposure-based treatments (Chapter 4), trauma-focused CBT and eye movement desensitisation and reprocessing (EMDR) remain first-line because they have by far the most trials. Psychodynamic therapy is widely used with trauma survivors, including survivors of sexual assault, but it is not a first-line treatment for PTSD (Section 5.11 has the trap). In schizophrenia, the Boston Psychotherapy Study (John Gunderson and colleagues, 1984) found exploratory, insight-oriented psychotherapy no better overall than reality-adaptive supportive therapy, which had the edge in practical adaptation. And for a short-lived reaction to a stressor, such as an adjustment disorder or an acute crisis, crisis intervention and supportive therapy (Section 3.10 and Chapter 9) are the treatments of choice. Table 3.29 summarises the picture.

Table 3.29 Where psychodynamic therapy stands, condition by condition.
ConditionKey evidenceWhere psychodynamic therapy stands
DepressionDriessen 2013; Steinert 2017Short-term therapy roughly equal to CBT
Treatment-resistant depressionTavistock trial, 2015Long-term therapy helps; the benefit shows late
Social anxiety disorderLeichsenring 2013Both work; CBT ahead at the end, level at two years
Panic disorderMilrod 2007Beat relaxation; CBT more consistent
Somatic symptomsAbbass, Kisely and Kroenke 2009Short-term therapy helps; fewer trials
Borderline personality disorderMBT and TFP trialsEffective; rivals DBT and schema therapy
Complex and chronic disordersLeichsenring and Rabung 2008, 2011; Smit 2012Long-term therapy probably better than short; disputed
PTSD, OCD, specific phobiaFew psychodynamic trialsExposure-based and trauma-focused CBT, EMDR first
SchizophreniaBoston Psychotherapy Study, 1984Insight-oriented therapy not supported
Adjustment disorder, acute crisisClinical consensusCrisis intervention and supportive therapy first
Exam trap

Three treatment-selection questions recur. The treatment of choice for an adjustment disorder is crisis intervention, not psychoanalytic psychotherapy, group therapy or CBT, because the problem is a time-limited reaction to a stressor. A depressed man whose marital discord is maintaining the depression benefits more from marital and couple therapy (Chapter 7) than from individual psychodynamic therapy, CBT or supportive therapy: match the treatment to the problem that keeps the disorder going. And for survivors of sexual assault the evidence-based core is trauma-focused CBT and EMDR; interpersonal psychotherapy, built for depression (Section 3.7), is the expected answer to "not commonly used". A practice question keys "psychodynamic psychotherapy, trauma-focused CBT and EMDR" as the most effective set: choose it, because it is the only option that contains both guideline treatments, but know that psychodynamic therapy itself is not a first-line treatment for PTSD. Remember too that pharmacotherapy is a treatment but not a psychotherapy.

What survives: the distinctive features of psychodynamic process

If brand names race to a dead heat, perhaps the useful question is not which therapy wins but what happens inside the sessions that help. In 2000 two American psychologists, Blagys and Mark Hilsenroth, searched the research literature for studies that had compared recordings and transcripts of actual sessions of manualised short-term psychodynamic-interpersonal therapy with sessions of manualised CBT, and asked which therapist activities reliably told the two apart. They found seven, published in Clinical Psychology: Science and Practice as "Distinctive features of short-term psychodynamic-interpersonal psychotherapy" (Table 3.30). Two years later, in Clinical Psychology Review ("Distinctive activities of cognitive-behavioral therapy"), they did the same for CBT and found six distinctive activities of its own: assigning homework and between-session activities, directing the session's activity, teaching skills for coping with symptoms, emphasising the patient's future experiences, giving information about the treatment, the disorder or the symptoms, and an intrapersonal, cognitive focus on thoughts and beliefs. These lists matter because they describe each therapy by what the therapist actually does rather than by its theory, which gives researchers something they can rate in a recording and relate to outcome.

Table 3.30 The seven distinctive features of psychodynamic-interpersonal therapy (Blagys and Hilsenroth, 2000).
What the therapist doesIn theoretical termsTaught in
Focuses on affect and the expression of emotionEmotional, not merely intellectual, insightSection 3.2
Explores attempts to avoid distressing thoughts and feelingsDefence and resistanceSection 3.2
Identifies recurring themes and patternsRepetition; the core conflictual relationship themeSections 3.2, 3.6
Discusses past experience (a developmental focus)The past living on in the presentSections 3.2, 3.4
Focuses on interpersonal relationsObject relations and attachmentSections 3.4, 3.5
Focuses on the therapy relationshipTransference and countertransferenceSection 3.3
Explores fantasy life: wishes, dreams, daydreamsFree association; dream analysisSection 3.2

What makes the list more than a description is a set of process-outcome studies. The American psychologist Enrico Jones built the Psychotherapy Process Q-set, 100 items that trained raters use to describe a recorded session. With Stacy Ablon (1998) he had expert psychodynamic and cognitive-behavioural therapists describe an ideal session of each kind, built prototypes from their ratings, and measured how closely real sessions from archived trials resembled each prototype, whatever therapy the therapists believed they were delivering. Adherence to the psychodynamic prototype predicted good outcome in both psychodynamic and cognitive therapy; adherence to the CBT prototype showed little relation to outcome in either. Louis Castonguay and colleagues (1996), studying 30 depressed clients in Beck's cognitive therapy (with or without medication), found that the working alliance and the patient's depth of emotional "experiencing" predicted improvement, while the therapist's focus on the impact of distorted thoughts on the depressive symptoms predicted poorer outcome, apparently because some therapists pushed the cognitive model rigidly when the alliance was strained.

Do not over-read these findings, and do not let an interviewer catch you doing so. They are correlational: therapists who explore feelings and patterns may simply have patients who are easier to work with. Other studies, by Robert DeRubeis and his colleagues among others, have found that competent use of cognitive techniques predicts improvement in CBT. And the brand labels are leaky: when Ablon and Jones (2002) rated transcripts from the NIMH depression trial, the CBT and interpersonal therapy sessions overlapped so much that the premise of two distinct treatments seemed doubtful. The defensible conclusion is the cautious form of Shedler's third claim: skilled therapists of every school do some of what psychodynamic therapists do, and the processes psychodynamic theory describes are among the ingredients of good therapy in general. The working alliance itself, now the most researched common factor (Section 8.5), began as a psychoanalytic idea (Section 3.3).

Psychodynamic ideas have also survived by being absorbed into other therapies, often without their label. Aaron Beck trained as a psychoanalyst before he built cognitive therapy (Chapter 5); schema therapy and cognitive analytic therapy (Section 5.7) reach back into childhood and work through the therapeutic relationship; and the case formulations of Chapter 2 routinely ask the psychodynamic questions of what the patient avoids, what keeps repeating and where it began.

Exam trap

Blagys and Hilsenroth (2000) listed seven distinctive activities of psychodynamic-interpersonal therapy; their 2002 review listed six for CBT. In an option list, assigning homework, setting the session agenda, teaching coping skills, giving information and focusing on the future are CBT; exploring avoidance, focusing on emotion, linking past and present, and exploring the therapy relationship, wishes and dreams are psychodynamic. The process-outcome findings that favour psychodynamic features are correlational, and other studies find that CBT techniques predict outcome too.

Psychodynamic thinking in Indian training and practice

Psychoanalysis came to India early, through Girindrasekhar Bose and the Indian Psychoanalytical Society he founded in Calcutta in 1922 (Section 3.2), but it never became the dominant clinical practice that it was for a time in the United States. Indian clinical psychology grew up largely in psychiatric hospitals and medical colleges, where the demand was for brief, practical treatments for very large numbers of patients, and its research base, as Section 5.11 and Chapter 9 describe, lies overwhelmingly in behavioural, cognitive and community-delivered interventions. Randomised trials of psychodynamic therapy in India are few.

Yet psychodynamic thinking runs through Indian training. Clinical psychology syllabi teach psychodynamic theory and therapy alongside behavioural, cognitive, humanistic and systemic approaches, and trainees learn to write psychodynamic formulations, to recognise defences and transference, and to use supervision to examine their countertransference, even when the treatment they finally offer is eclectic. In everyday practice psychodynamic ideas usually appear in modified form: weekly or less frequent sessions, a shorter and more focused course, a more active and supportive therapist, and the family drawn into the work rather than kept outside it. Long-term, open-ended psychoanalytic psychotherapy is available mainly in private practice in the larger cities, to the minority who can afford it.

Indian clinicians have also asked whether the theory itself travels. The psychoanalyst Sudhir Kakar (born 1938), in The Inner World: A Psycho-analytic Study of Childhood and Society in India (1978), argued that Indian childhood, with its long and intense closeness between mother and child and its embedding in the extended family, produces a self organised more around relationships and the family's expectations than around separateness. The American psychoanalyst Alan Roland, who worked with patients in India and Japan, called this a "familial self" (In Search of Self in India and Japan, 1988), in contrast to the "individualised self" that Western therapy assumes and tries to strengthen. If separation-individuation (Mahler, Section 3.4) and autonomy are the unspoken goals of Western psychodynamic therapy, an Indian therapist may need to redefine success as a better-negotiated interdependence. In the same spirit Jaswant Singh Neki's guru-chela (teacher-disciple) paradigm of 1973 (Section 3.10) asks the therapist to be an authoritative, caring guide rather than the neutral, opaque listener of Section 3.2; the debate about the dependence it permits, and the related ideas of Surya and Jayaram, return in Section 8.11.

Clinical link

Meera, a 31-year-old schoolteacher in Mysuru, has had two episodes of moderate depression. In assessment a pattern stands out: she gives endlessly to her in-laws and colleagues, says nothing, and then feels crushed and worthless when her efforts go unnoticed, much as she once tried to win a distant, critical father's approval. What does the evidence let her clinician offer? For depression of this severity, a focused short-term psychodynamic therapy organised around that repeated relationship pattern (Luborsky's core conflictual relationship theme, Section 3.6), CBT and interpersonal psychotherapy (role disputes, Section 3.7) have broadly similar evidence, so Meera's own preference and the formulation can guide the choice. The evidence also says what not to promise: a cure through insight alone, or a guarantee that sixteen sessions will be enough. Her clinician rates her depression every few sessions and changes course, adding medication or another approach, if she is not improving.

Weighing it up

Put the pieces together and the verdict is more interesting than either side allows. As a scientific theory, classical psychoanalysis earned its critics: its clinical evidence was contaminated by suggestion, its founding case was not the cure it was claimed to be, and its institutions were slow to test their claims. As a family of treatments, psychodynamic therapy has done far better than that history predicted. What it has not shown is that its own theory of change, insight into unconscious conflict, is the reason it works. From the Menninger project onwards, support, the relationship and the patient's attachment to the therapist have done much of the work, and the dodo bird verdict of Section 8.3, that very different bona fide therapies tend to produce similar results, applies here as much as anywhere. Psychodynamic therapy's most lasting contributions may be the ones every therapist now uses: attention to emotion and avoidance, to repeated patterns and their history, and above all to the relationship in the room.

Remember

A thirty-second answer to "Does psychodynamic therapy work?": Yes, better than no treatment and, for depression and several anxiety disorders, about as well as CBT in the best trials (Steinert and colleagues, 2017); specialised forms such as MBT and TFP help in borderline personality disorder. The evidence base is smaller and weaker than CBT's, the claims for long-term therapy (Leichsenring and Rabung, 2008) are disputed, and exposure-based treatments remain first choice for PTSD, OCD and phobias. As for why it works, the Menninger project and the process studies suggest that support and the relationship matter as much as insight.

The case of Anna O., revisited

Go back to the sickroom in Vienna, and to Bertha Pappenheim pushing away the glass of water she longed for. You can now name everything that happened on the evening she drank again. Her angry account of the companion's dog drinking from a glass was an abreaction, the reliving of a blocked memory with its full feeling, and the relief that followed was catharsis (Section 3.1). The trance in which she talked was hypnosis, and the procedure was Breuer's cathartic method. Freud's reasons for giving that method up are already visible in the story: such cures seemed to depend on the patient's attachment to her physician, and they went round the patient's resistance instead of through it. By replacing hypnosis with free association, and by learning from Dora that the patient's feelings for the analyst were the heart of the work rather than an interruption of it, Freud turned the talking cure into psychoanalysis. Out of that change grew almost every therapy in this chapter, from Klein's play technique and Kohut's selfobject transferences to Malan's triangles, Klerman and Weissman's interpersonal psychotherapy and Berne's ego states.

Was she cured by talking? The question the chapter opened with has a plain answer: no. Henri Ellenberger's search of the Bellevue sanatorium records (1972) showed that she was admitted at Kreuzlingen in July 1882, within weeks of the end of Breuer's treatment, still ill and dependent on the morphine she had been given for facial pain. During the next decade she was institutionalised several times with severe returns of her symptoms before a slow recovery began. She then became a pioneering social worker and a campaigner against the sexual exploitation of women and the traffic in women forced into prostitution; she founded a league of Jewish women in 1904 and a home for unmarried mothers in 1907, and after her death in 1936 West Germany honoured her with a commemorative postage stamp. That is the instructive fact. The first case ever offered as evidence for the talking cure left out its ending, and no one can now say how much of a recovery that took years owed anything to the talking. Section 3.11 drew the lesson the field took most of a century to learn: case histories written by the treating clinician cannot show that a therapy works, but controlled comparisons can. They show that psychodynamic therapy does help, about as much as other therapies for depression and several anxiety disorders, while the Menninger project and the process studies suggest that support and the relationship carry as much of the change as insight does. Bertha Pappenheim's real story fits that verdict better than Breuer's version of it ever did.

Chapter summary

3.1 From Hypnosis to the Talking Cure. Psychodynamic therapies assume that symptoms come from mental forces in conflict, often outside awareness. Their roots lie in Franz Anton Mesmer's animal magnetism, whose effects a French royal commission (1784) traced to belief, and in James Braid's "hypnotism" (1843). Charcot at the Salpêtrière held that only hysterics could be deeply hypnotised; Bernheim's Nancy school answered that hypnosis is normal suggestibility. Josef Breuer's treatment of Anna O. (Bertha Pappenheim) produced the "talking cure", her own phrase, and the cathartic method of Studies on Hysteria (1895): abreaction relives a repressed memory with its emotion, and catharsis is the release that follows. Pierre Janet explained hysteria by dissociation, a weakness of integration; Freud by repression, an active, motivated exclusion from awareness, which is why repression is the psychodynamic answer for dissociative symptoms. Freud then abandoned hypnosis and the pressure technique for free association.

3.2 Classical Psychoanalysis as a Treatment. Freud's aims were to make the unconscious conscious ("Where id was, there ego shall be") and to turn hysterical misery into common unhappiness, through insight and structural change. The patient lies on a couch with the analyst out of sight, in modern classical analysis four or five times a week, and every analyst first undergoes a training analysis. The fundamental rule asks the patient to say whatever comes to mind (free association), while the analyst keeps evenly suspended attention, neutrality and abstinence. Dreams are the "royal road" to the unconscious: the dream-work turns latent into manifest content. Resistance is analysed rather than overpowered, and Freud classified five kinds (1926). Greenson's sequence runs confrontation, clarification, interpretation; Strachey's mutative interpretations address the live transference; working through repeats insight until change lasts. Libido, cathexis, fixation, sublimation and the defence mechanisms complete the analyst's vocabulary.

3.3 Transference, Countertransference and the Alliance. Transference is the patient's unconscious redirection onto the therapist of feelings and expectations that belonged to figures from the past. Freud learned its importance when Dora broke off treatment (published 1905), and came to see it as both the strongest resistance and the only place where the old conflict can be changed. It may be positive, negative or erotic, and in full analysis the illness is re-staged as a transference neurosis. Countertransference, named by Freud in 1910, is the therapist's emotional reaction arising from his own unresolved conflicts; Winnicott (1949) and Heimann (1950) turned it into an instrument, which Racker refined, and negative countertransference shows as irritation, boredom or punitive remarks. Zetzel, Greenson and Bordin (goals, tasks, bond; 1979) described the working alliance, and Greenson the real relationship. Boundary crossings differ from violations, and managing transference is the hallmark of psychodynamic models.

3.4 Ego Psychology and Object Relations. Freud's structural model of 1923 opened two roads. Anna Freud's The Ego and the Mechanisms of Defence (1936) made the defences the analyst's business and added identification with the aggressor; Heinz Hartmann, the father of ego psychology, described the conflict-free ego sphere (1939), and the assessment of ego strength came to guide the choice of treatment. Erik Erikson coined the identity crisis. Object relations are internalised representations of self and others and the relationships between them; Fairbairn held that libido is object-seeking. Melanie Klein's play technique led to the paranoid-schizoid position, with splitting and projective identification, and the depressive position, with guilt and reparation; splitting is her answer for paranoia. Winnicott described the good-enough mother, the holding environment, the transitional object and the false self. Mahler traced separation-individuation and linked schizophrenia to the autistic phase, and Kernberg described borderline personality organisation and transference-focused psychotherapy.

3.5 Self Psychology, Relational Analysis, Attachment and Mentalisation. Heinz Kohut's self psychology treats narcissistic disorders as a deficit: the self needs selfobjects for mirroring, idealisation and twinship, and when these fail it fragments, empties and rages. A client who feels "empty" and "no one" reflects a failure of parental mirroring and idealising. In the mirror, idealising and twinship transferences the therapist serves as the missing selfobject, and empathy (vicarious introspection), optimal frustration and transmuting internalisation build structure. Greenberg and Mitchell's relational psychoanalysis and Stolorow's intersubjectivity make therapy a two-person psychology. John Bowlby's attachment theory gave the therapist five tasks, beginning with a secure base, and Mary Main's Adult Attachment Interview scores the coherence of a narrative. Peter Fonagy's reflective function grew into mentalisation, and Bateman and Fonagy's mentalisation-based treatment for borderline personality disorder uses a not-knowing stance, differing from Kernberg's TFP as deficit differs from conflict.

3.6 Brief Psychodynamic Psychotherapies. Ferenczi's active technique, Rank's fixed end date and Alexander and French's corrective emotional experience (1946) prepared the way for brief dynamic work, which shares selection, a focus, a time limit, an active therapist, early transference work and attention to ending. David Malan's Tavistock focal therapy rests on the triangle of conflict (defence, anxiety, hidden feeling) and the triangle of person (other, therapist, past). Peter Sifneos's short-term anxiety-provoking psychotherapy has the strictest selection criteria and an Oedipal focus, and he coined alexithymia. Habib Davanloo's ISTDP uses pressure, challenge and the head-on collision with resistance. James Mann's time-limited psychotherapy lasts exactly twelve sessions around a central issue. Lester Luborsky's supportive-expressive therapy works on the core conflictual relationship theme (wish, response of other, response of self), and Strupp and Binder's time-limited dynamic psychotherapy on the four-part cyclical maladaptive pattern.

3.7 Interpersonal Psychotherapy. Gerald Klerman and Myrna Weissman built interpersonal psychotherapy (manual 1984) to be tested like a drug: a time-limited, manualised treatment, originally for major depression, that links symptoms to a current interpersonal problem. Its roots are Adolf Meyer, Harry Stack Sullivan, John Bowlby and research on life events. In the initial phase of a 12 to 16 session course the therapist diagnoses the depression, grants Talcott Parsons's sick role and takes an interpersonal inventory. The four problem areas are grief, interpersonal role disputes (at renegotiation, impasse or dissolution), role transitions and interpersonal deficits. Communication analysis is the signature technique, and the transference is not interpreted. The evidence includes the NIMH collaborative trial (1989), Cuijpers's meta-analyses, which put IPT roughly level with other psychotherapies, and Bolton's group IPT trial in Uganda (2003); adaptations include IPT-A, maintenance IPT and Ellen Frank's interpersonal and social rhythm therapy for bipolar disorder.

3.8 The Early Dissenters as Therapists. Carl Gustav Jung's analytical psychology, after his break with Freud in 1913, aims at individuation through the personal and collective unconscious, complexes and archetypes (persona, shadow, anima, animus, Self). Jung sat face to face, described four stages (confession, elucidation, education, transformation) and used the word association test, amplification and active imagination. Alfred Adler's individual psychology rests on organ inferiority, the inferiority complex, striving for superiority, fictional finalism, the style of life and social interest. Dreikurs's four phases run from relationship to reorientation, with lifestyle assessment (family constellation, birth order, early recollections), encouragement, acting as if, spitting in the client's soup and Mosak's push-button technique; analysing the transference is not Adlerian. Karen Horney described basic anxiety, ten neurotic needs, three neurotic trends, the tyranny of the should and self-analysis, and Harry Stack Sullivan the participant observer, selective inattention and parataxic distortion.

3.9 Transactional Analysis. Eric Berne's transactional analysis (Games People Play, 1964) grew from Paul Federn's ego states. Structural analysis divides the personality into Parent (copied from parent figures), Adult (present reality) and Child (replayed from childhood), with Controlling and Nurturing Parent, Free and Adapted Child, the Little Professor and Dusay's egogram; contamination and exclusion disturb the boundaries. Transactions are complementary, crossed or ulterior, and three rules of communication govern them. A stroke is a unit of recognition; Steiner described the stroke economy, and time is structured from withdrawal to intimacy. Games are repeated ulterior transactions with a payoff, played out on Karpman's drama triangle of Persecutor, Rescuer and Victim. Scripts pass down through counterinjunctions, injunctions (the Gouldings' twelve) and Kahler's drivers. Berne described four life positions, which Thomas Harris popularised in I'm OK, You're OK (1967); therapy uses contracts, permission and redecision.

3.10 Supportive Psychotherapy, Hypnotherapy and Narcoanalysis. Supportive psychotherapy uses direct measures to relieve symptoms and to maintain, restore or improve self-esteem, ego function and adaptive skills (Pinsker and colleagues); Wolberg sorted therapies into supportive, re-educative and reconstructive. It suits crises, weak ego strength, chronic illness and busy clinics, and it is the first response in crisis management. Its techniques include ventilation, reassurance, guidance, suggestion, persuasion, environmental manipulation and externalisation of interests, and Gabbard places every intervention on an expressive-supportive continuum; Neki's guru-chela paradigm fits it to Indian expectations. Hypnosis is focused attention with an enhanced response to suggestion. It helps surgical pain and irritable bowel syndrome, Hilgard explained it by neodissociation, and it raises confidence in recall but not accuracy. Narcoanalysis, the amytal interview, yields no truth, and Selvi v. State of Karnataka (2010) barred its involuntary use in India.

3.11 Does Psychodynamic Therapy Work? Karl Popper called psychoanalysis unfalsifiable; Adolf Grünbaum (1984) replied that it is testable but that its clinical data are contaminated by suggestion and Freud's tally argument fails. Eysenck's 1952 review put psychoanalysis below no formal therapy, and Ellenberger (1972) showed that Anna O. had not been cured. Wallerstein's Menninger project found that supportive elements did much of the work, often through a transference cure. Meta-analyses by Leichsenring (2004) and Abbass (Cochrane, 2006) show short-term psychodynamic therapy beating control conditions; Leichsenring and Rabung's effect size of 1.8 for long-term therapy (2008) shrank to a moderate one in 2011, and Shedler (2010) defended the field. MBT and TFP help in borderline personality disorder, psychodynamic therapy roughly equals CBT for depression, and exposure-based treatments still lead for PTSD, OCD and phobias. Blagys and Hilsenroth (2000) listed seven distinctive features of psychodynamic process.
