From the past papers

  1. In view of most psychodynamic theorists, dissociative symptoms are produced by which of the following? (RML 2022)

    (a) Repression (b) Ego state (c) Conflict (d) Gender identity issue

    Answer: (a) Repression. Freud held that unbearable ideas are actively pushed out of awareness and kept out, and most psychodynamic writers followed him rather than Janet, for whom dissociation was a weakness of integration. "Ego states" is Federn's and Berne's idea, not a cause of dissociation; conflict is what every defence responds to, while repression is the specific mechanism; gender identity has nothing to do with it.

  2. Psychodynamic therapists believe that disorder stems from: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Disturbed thought patterns (b) Emotional trauma experienced in childhood (c) A failure to live up to one's full potential (d) Inappropriate learning patterns

    Answer: (b) Emotional trauma experienced in childhood. The psychodynamic answer always points to unresolved unconscious conflict and emotional experience from childhood. Disturbed thinking is the cognitive answer, unfulfilled potential the humanistic one and faulty learning the behavioural one.

  3. In psychodynamic therapy, analysis of resistance is where: (RML 2021)

    (a) The client is asked to talk about anything that comes to mind (b) The client transfers their feelings towards significant others onto the therapist (c) The client obstructs the analysis and the therapist explores reasons for this (d) The therapist interprets the dreams of the client

    Answer: (c). Freud's crucial shift was to treat the patient's obstruction of the work as material to be understood, not overpowered. Option (a) is free association, (b) is transference and (d) is dream analysis: the four options are the four classic techniques, and only (c) is resistance.

  4. In psychoanalytic theory, what term describes the mental representation of psychic energy being invested or attached to a particular object, person, or idea? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Libido (b) Cathexis (c) Fixation (d) Sublimation

    Answer: (b) Cathexis. Cathexis (Strachey's word for Freud's Besetzung) is where the energy is placed. Libido is the energy itself, fixation is energy stuck at an earlier psychosexual stage, and sublimation is energy redirected to a socially valued aim.

  5. In the psychodynamic framework, which concept refers to the defense mechanism where an individual unconsciously attributes their own unacceptable thoughts or feelings to someone else, thereby avoiding direct confrontation with those feelings? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Rationalization (b) Projection (c) Displacement (d) Sublimation

    Answer: (b) Projection. Projection places one's own unacceptable feeling in someone else, as when a hostile patient is sure the therapist dislikes him. Displacement keeps the feeling as one's own but changes its target, rationalisation offers a respectable excuse, and sublimation redirects a drive into a valued activity.

  6. 'Counter transference' in psychotherapy refers to (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) therapists' emotional reactions to the client based on the therapists' own unresolved conflict (b) client's projection onto the therapist (c) client's resistance to therapy (d) behavioral reinforcement techniques

    Answer: (a). Freud named countertransference in 1910 as the analyst's unconscious reactions to the patient, arising from the analyst's own unresolved conflicts; Heimann (1950) later made it a tool. The client's projection onto the therapist is transference, obstruction of the work is resistance, and reinforcement belongs to behaviour therapy.

  7. The process in which therapist expressed his negative feelings towards patients is termed as: (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Negative Transference (b) Counter transference (c) Positive transference (d) Negative counter transference

    Answer: (d) Negative countertransference. Direction decides the term: therapist to patient is countertransference, and irritation, boredom, dislike or punitive remarks make it negative. Negative transference is the patient's hostility towards the therapist, positive transference the patient's affection, and plain "countertransference" is true but less exact than the keyed answer.

  8. A working relationship has all of the following characteristics EXCEPT: (RML 2020)

    (a) Observable ability of the worker and the client to work together (b) A committed belief in the helping relationship (c) A psychodynamic theory base (d) A contract to work together realistically and collaboratively

    Answer: (c) A psychodynamic theory base. The working alliance began as a psychoanalytic idea (Zetzel, Greenson), but Bordin (1979) made it pantheoretical: agreement on goals and tasks plus an emotional bond. A working relationship is defined by its features, the other three options, not by any theory.

  9. Managing transference and countertransference is critical in which of the following theory-based models of therapy? (RML 2020)

    (a) behaviour modification (b) cognitive-behavioural therapy (c) psychodynamic models of therapy (d) solution focused models of therapy

    Answer: (c) Psychodynamic models of therapy. Every therapist has a relationship with the client, but only psychodynamic models make the analysis of transference and countertransference the central route to change. Behaviour modification works on contingencies, CBT on thoughts and behaviour, and solution-focused therapy on exceptions and future goals.

  10. Who gave the important concept of 'identity crisis' in adolescents? (RML 2024)

    (a) Bowlby (b) Melanie Klein (c) Eric Erikson (d) Eric Berne

    Answer: (c) Erik Erikson. Erikson, an ego psychologist and student of Anna Freud, coined the identity crisis for the adolescent struggle of identity versus role confusion (Childhood and Society, 1950). Bowlby is attachment, Klein the two positions, and Berne transactional analysis; Berne is a tempting distractor only because Erikson was one of his analysts.

  11. The concept of "object relations" in psychoanalytic theory refers to: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) The relationship between the id, ego, and superego (b) The internalized mental representations of self and others and the relationship between them (c) The use of defense mechanisms to protect the ego from anxiety (d) The stage of psychosexual development focused on the genitals

    Answer: (b). The "object" is almost always a person, and object relations are the inner images of self and others, built from early experience, that shape later relationships. Option (a) is Freud's structural model, (c) is the defences of ego psychology, and (d) is the genital stage.

  12. In paranoia, the ego's defense mechanism as given by Melaine Klein is seen as: (RML 2024)

    (a) Reaction formation (b) Splitting (c) Suppression (d) Projection

    Answer: (b) Splitting. For Melanie Klein paranoia revives the paranoid-schizoid position, whose defence is splitting the world into wholly good and wholly bad. Projection is the tempting wrong answer because it is Freud's classical account of paranoia (the Schreber case, 1911); reaction formation and suppression are not Kleinian answers.

  13. Schizophrenia corresponds to the autistic phase of development and the person fails to develop an adequate and stable symbiosis, was the developmental theory of schizophrenia given by: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Ping-Nie Pao (b) Sylvano Arieti (c) Margaret Mahler (d) Edith Jacobson

    Answer: (c) Margaret Mahler. The "autistic" and "symbiotic" phases are Mahler's forerunners of separation-individuation, and her theory of psychosis grew from them. Silvano Arieti described palaeologic thinking, Pao the "organismic panic" and Jacobson psychosis as a fusion of self and object representations.

  14. A client tells you that they are "no one" and feels "empty inside." They report chronic fears of abandonment and have a history of intense, unstable relationships. According to psychoanalytic object relations theory, this pathology of the self is most likely a result of: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Fixation at the oral stage of psychosexual development (b) A failure in parental mirroring and idealizing, leading to a fragmented self (c) Unresolved Oedipal complex and castration anxiety (d) Harsh superego demands leading to constant guilt

    Answer: (b). Emptiness, a fragmented self and failed mirroring and idealising are Kohut's self psychology, which many textbooks group loosely with object relations. Oral fixation is classical drive theory, the Oedipal option is the classical account of neurosis, and harsh superego guilt is Freud's "Guilty Man", exactly what Kohut said these patients were not.

  15. Who developed the technique of Interpersonal Psychotherapy (IPT)? (RML 2022)

    (a) L. Benjamin (b) Peter Fonagy and A. Bateman (c) Otto F. Kernberg (d) G.L. Klerman and M. M. Weissman

    Answer: (d) Klerman and Weissman. Gerald Klerman and Myrna Weissman built IPT in the 1970s and published its manual in 1984. Lorna Smith Benjamin created the Structural Analysis of Social Behavior and interpersonal reconstructive therapy, Fonagy and Bateman mentalisation-based treatment, and Kernberg transference-focused psychotherapy.

  16. Interpersonal Therapy (IPT) does NOT typically include which of the following techniques or focus areas? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Role playing and role reversal techniques (b) Grief and bereavement work (c) Role conflict resolution (d) Role transition support

    Answer: (a). Grief, role disputes (also called role conflicts) and role transitions are three of IPT's four problem areas, and role reversal belongs to Moreno's psychodrama. Accept the key, but know that IPT does use role playing as a behaviour change technique to rehearse a difficult conversation; what it never uses is free association, dream analysis or transference interpretation.

  17. A male adult with Depressive Disorder and marital discord would benefit more from: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Psychodynamic psychotherapy (b) Cognitive behavior therapy (c) Marital & couple therapy (d) Supportive therapy

    Answer: (c) Marital and couple therapy. Match the intervention to what keeps the problem going: when the marriage is maintaining the depression and both partners will attend, couple therapy is the better choice. Individual psychodynamic, cognitive-behavioural or supportive therapy leaves the maintaining factor untouched; IPT with a role-dispute focus is the alternative when only the patient attends.

  18. The concept of the "collective unconscious" was given by: (RML 2024)

    (a) Sigmund Freud (b) Alfred Adler (c) Carl Jung (d) Karen Horney

    Answer: (c) Carl Jung. Jung's collective unconscious is an inherited layer shared by all humans and made up of archetypes. Freud's unconscious is personal and repressed, Adler's psychology centres on inferiority and social interest, and Horney's on basic anxiety and neurotic needs.

  19. 'Animus is the unconscious, masculine side of a woman's female persona' was given by: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Alfred Adler (b) Anna Freud (c) Carl Jung (d) Victor Emil Frankl

    Answer: (c) Carl Jung. The animus is Jung's archetype of the unconscious masculine side of a woman, and the anima the unconscious feminine side of a man. Anna Freud is known for the defences, Viktor Frankl for logotherapy and the will to meaning, and Adler for individual psychology.

  20. The concept of inferiority complex and superiority drive was given by: (RML 2024)

    (a) Carl Jung (b) Victor Frankl (c) Alfred Adler (d) Erik Erikson

    Answer: (c) Alfred Adler. For Adler normal inferiority feelings drive the lifelong striving for superiority, and overwhelming ones harden into an inferiority complex, sometimes covered by a superiority complex. Jung gave the collective unconscious, Frankl paradoxical intention in logotherapy and Erikson the psychosocial stages.

  21. The concept of 'Organ Inferiority' was given by: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Harry Stack Sullivan (b) M. Klein (c) Anna Freud (d) Alfred Adler

    Answer: (d) Alfred Adler. In Study of Organ Inferiority (1907) Adler argued that a constitutionally weak organ produces inferiority feelings that the person strives to make up for, sometimes overcompensating. Sullivan is interpersonal psychiatry, Klein the paranoid-schizoid and depressive positions, and Anna Freud the defences.

  22. Which of the following technique or techniques would not typically be included within the repertoire of an Adlerian counsellor? (RML 2022)

    (a) Acting "as if" (b) Push button (c) Encouragement (d) Analysis of transference

    Answer: (d) Analysis of transference. Acting "as if" (after Vaihinger), Mosak's push-button technique and encouragement, which Adlerians regard as their most important ingredient, are all Adlerian. Adlerians comment on the relationship but do not foster a transference neurosis or analyse the transference as a repetition of childhood.

  23. In Transactional Analysis, which "ego state" is characterized by behaviors, thoughts, and feelings copied from parents or parental figures? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Adult (b) Child (c) Parent (d) Persona

    Answer: (c) Parent. In Berne's structural analysis the Parent is a recording of real parent figures, the Adult deals with present reality and the Child replays the person's own childhood. "Persona" is Jung's archetype of the social mask, not a TA ego state.

  24. The concepts of injunctions, counterinjunctions, and games are fundamental components of which psychological theory? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Gestalt therapy (b) Transactional Analysis (c) Rational Emotive Behavior Therapy (d) Person-Centered therapy

    Answer: (b) Transactional analysis. Counterinjunctions and injunctions are the parental messages of Steiner's script matrix (the Gouldings listed twelve injunctions), and games are Berne's. Gestalt speaks of unfinished business and the empty chair, REBT of irrational beliefs and "musts", person-centred therapy of conditions of worth; the Gouldings borrowed Gestalt techniques, but the injunction is TA's.

  25. Which therapy is mostly needed in 'Crisis Management'? (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Cognitive behavior therapy (b) Acceptance & commitment therapy (c) Expressive therapy (d) Supportive therapy

    Answer: (d) Supportive therapy. In a crisis ventilation, realistic reassurance, guidance and environmental manipulation come first, to restore the person's equilibrium. Expressive therapy, whether insight-oriented or arts-based, is not the first response to an acute crisis, and CBT and acceptance and commitment therapy come later if they are needed.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Free association, obeying Freud's fundamental rule of saying whatever comes to mind without censoring it, is the central technique of psychoanalysis; systematic desensitisation, flooding and modelling are behaviour therapy, and paradoxical intention is Frankl's logotherapy. asked 5 times
  2. The four problem areas of interpersonal psychotherapy are grief, interpersonal role disputes (role conflicts), role transitions and interpersonal deficits; role reversal is Moreno's psychodrama, and schemas, cognitive distortions or bilateral stimulation belong to other therapies. asked 4 times
  3. Countertransference (named by Freud in 1910) is the therapist's emotional reaction to the client arising from the therapist's own unresolved conflicts; a therapist's irritation, boredom or punitive feelings are negative countertransference, while the client's projection onto the therapist is transference. asked 3 times
  4. Alfred Adler founded individual psychology after leaving Freud in 1911 and gave organ inferiority (1907), the inferiority complex and the striving for superiority; not Jung, Horney, Frankl, Erikson, Sullivan, Klein or Anna Freud. asked 3 times
  5. Carl Jung's analytical psychology gave the collective unconscious of inherited archetypes, including the animus (the unconscious masculine side of a woman) and the anima (the unconscious feminine side of a man). asked twice
  6. Transference is the client's unconscious redirection onto the therapist of feelings about significant figures from the past; with free association it is a central technique of psychoanalytic therapy, and the therapist's own reaction is countertransference. asked 3 times
  7. Repression, Freud's "cornerstone", is the psychodynamic answer both to what produces dissociative symptoms and to the primary defence against anxiety; dissociation as a weakness of integration is Janet's view. asked twice
  8. Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) is the father of psychoanalysis, at once a theory of the mind and personality, a method of investigating the mind and a treatment; Bernheim, Breuer and Charcot were its forerunners. asked twice
  9. Working through (Freud, 1914) is the repeated re-examination of the same conflicts, resistances and interpretations across many contexts until insight becomes lasting change in behaviour and character; a single interpretation is not enough. asked twice
  10. For survivors of sexual assault the evidence-based core is trauma-focused CBT and EMDR; interpersonal psychotherapy, built for depression, is the therapy "not commonly used", and psychodynamic therapy is not first-line for PTSD. asked twice
  11. Cathexis is the investment of psychic energy in the mental representation of an object, person or idea; libido is the energy itself, fixation energy stuck at a stage and sublimation energy redirected to a valued aim. asked once
  12. In the analysis of resistance the client obstructs the analysis and the therapist explores the reasons; saying whatever comes to mind is free association, and redirecting feelings onto the therapist is transference. asked once
  13. Projection attributes one's own unacceptable thoughts or feelings to someone else; displacement changes the target, rationalisation offers an excuse and sublimation redirects a drive to a valued aim. asked once
  14. Psychodynamic therapists trace disorder to emotional trauma and unconscious conflict from childhood; disturbed thinking is the cognitive answer, faulty learning the behavioural one and unfulfilled potential the humanistic one. asked once
  15. Managing transference and countertransference is critical in psychodynamic models, not in behaviour modification, cognitive-behavioural or solution-focused therapy. asked once
  16. A working relationship needs a realistic, collaborative contract, an observable ability to work together and a committed belief in the helping relationship, but no psychodynamic theory base; Bordin (1979) made the alliance (goals, tasks, bond) pantheoretical. asked once
  17. Object relations are the internalised mental representations of self and others and the relationships between them, not the id, ego and superego, the defences or the genital stage. asked once
  18. In paranoia Melanie Klein's defence is splitting, the defence of the paranoid-schizoid position; projection is Freud's classical account (the Schreber case, 1911). asked once
  19. Margaret Mahler held that schizophrenia corresponds to the autistic phase, the person failing to develop an adequate and stable symbiosis; not Arieti, Pao or Jacobson. asked once
  20. Feeling "empty" and like "no one", with a fragmented self, reflects a failure of parental mirroring and idealising in Heinz Kohut's self psychology; not oral fixation, an unresolved Oedipus complex or a harsh superego. asked once
  21. Erik Erikson coined the identity crisis of adolescence (identity versus role confusion, Childhood and Society, 1950); not Bowlby, Klein or Berne. asked once
  22. Interpersonal psychotherapy was developed by Gerald Klerman and Myrna Weissman (manual 1984); mentalisation-based treatment is Bateman and Fonagy's, transference-focused psychotherapy Kernberg's, and SASB Lorna Smith Benjamin's. asked once
  23. Acting "as if", Mosak's push-button technique, encouragement and spitting in the client's soup are Adlerian; analysis of transference is not. asked once
  24. In transactional analysis the Parent ego state holds behaviour, thoughts and feelings copied from parents or parent figures; the Adult deals with present reality, the Child replays one's own childhood, and the persona is Jung's. asked once
  25. Injunctions, counterinjunctions and games are core concepts of transactional analysis (Berne; Steiner's script matrix, 1966; the Gouldings' twelve injunctions), not of Gestalt therapy, REBT or person-centred therapy. asked once
  26. Supportive therapy (ventilation, realistic reassurance, guidance, environmental manipulation) is the therapy most needed in crisis management, before CBT, acceptance and commitment therapy or expressive therapy. asked once
  27. An adult with depressive disorder and marital discord benefits more from marital and couple therapy than from psychodynamic, cognitive-behavioural or supportive therapy: treat what maintains the problem. asked once
  28. The treatment of choice for an adjustment disorder is crisis intervention, not psychoanalytic psychotherapy, group therapy or CBT. asked once
  29. Pierre Janet, who explained hysteria by dissociation, is the name most often attached to la belle indifférence, not Freud, Anna Freud or Hippocrates. asked once
  30. Pharmacotherapy is a treatment but not a psychotherapy; cognitive, behavioural and psychodynamic therapies are psychotherapies. asked once
